Independent fact-checkPrimary sources onlyLast audited: 24 October 2026

Federal Cannabis & Hemp Law — Audited

An independent audit of the deep-research dossier on United States federal cannabis and hemp law, checked claim by claim against primary sources only — congress.gov, uscode.house.gov, eCFR, the Federal Register, and official DEA, DOJ, USDA, FDA, IRS, FinCEN, ATF, USPS, DOT, VA, USCIS, OPM and Supreme Court pages. Part One is the audit itself: every claim, its verdict, its primary source, and the corrected wording. Part Two is the complete report, rewritten with every correction applied in place.

522 verdict rows 358 Confirmed 120 Corrected 0 Removed 44 Unverified
Part One

The Audit

522 verdict rows — 358 Confirmed · 120 Corrected · 0 Removed · 44 Unverified.

Note on the totals: these figures count the verdict rows in the audit tables on this page. The five section audits' own header tallies differed slightly from their tables — the Parts 6–8 table contains one verdict row its header does not count, and the Parts 12–14 header counted four items that appear in verdict-less resolution and sources tables.

Five section audits, merged in Part order (Parts 0–14, then Research Notes and Sources). Verdicts: Confirmed — the claim matches the primary source; Corrected — the claim was wrong or stale and the corrected wording is given; Removed — wrong with no correct version (none occurred: every false claim had a correct version in a primary source); Unverified — no primary source could confirm it. Internal contradictions and link errors are folded into each audit block; everything still unverified is consolidated at the end, with where it can be confirmed.

Audit — Federal Cannabis & Hemp Report, Parts 0–2

Scope: Part 0 (Headlines), Part 1 (Controlled Substances Law, §§1.1–1.3), Part 2 (Hemp Law, §§2.1–2.3) of Federal_Cannabis___Hemp_Law.html. Method: Primary sources only — congress.gov, uscode.house.gov, eCFR, Federal Register / govinfo, and official DEA, DOJ, USDA (AMS), FDA, Treasury/IRS, GAO and Supreme Court pages. Nothing was confirmed from news, law-firm blogs, trade press, Wikipedia, CRS or AI summaries; claims supported only by such sources are marked Unverified. Audit date: 23 October 2026. The original HTML was not edited. Verdict totals (150 rows): Confirmed 124 · Corrected 14 · Removed 0 · Unverified 12.

Part 1.1 — CSA fundamentals

SectionClaim (short)VerdictPrimary source (exact URL)Corrected wording
§1.1"Marijuana" is defined at 21 U.S.C. 802(16)(A) (cannabis plant, parts, and compounds/manufactures/salts/derivatives/preparations thereof)Confirmeduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 802(16) (definition also reproduced verbatim in 91 FR 22714)—
§1.1Exclusions at 802(16)(B): (i) hemp as defined in 7 U.S.C. 1639o; (ii) mature stalks, fibre, oil or cake from seeds, other stalk/fibre/oil/cake compounds (except resin), and sterilised seed incapable of germinationConfirmeduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 802(16)(B)—
§1.1Schedule I listings: marijuana 21 CFR 1308.11(d)(23), marijuana extract (d)(58), tetrahydrocannabinols (d)(31)ConfirmedeCFR, 21 CFR 1308.11(d) (paragraphs also cited in 91 FR 22714)—
§1.1The April 2026 rule added the Schedule III listings at "21 CFR 1308.13(g)(2)–(4)"Correctedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm"The April 2026 rule amended 21 CFR 1308.13 by adding paragraphs (g)(2) through (5): (g)(2) marijuana, (g)(3) marijuana extract, (g)(4) naturally derived delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinols, and (g)(5) [Reserved] — in each case only to the extent of an FDA-approved drug product or a state medical marijuana licence."
§1.1Schedule I findings under 21 U.S.C. 812(b) (flagged "Verify" in the report)Confirmeduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 812(b)(1): (A) high potential for abuse; (B) no currently accepted medical use in treatment in the United States; (C) lack of accepted safety for use under medical supervision—
§1.1Schedule III findings paraphrased as abuse "leading only to moderate or low physical dependence or high psychological dependence"Correcteduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 812(b)(3)"Schedule III requires (A) a potential for abuse less than the drugs or other substances in Schedules I and II, (B) a currently accepted medical use in treatment in the United States, and (C) that abuse of the drug or other substance may lead to moderate or low physical dependence or high psychological dependence (21 U.S.C. 812(b)(3))."
§1.121 U.S.C. 841(a) prohibition summary (manufacture, distribution, dispensing, possession with intent, except as authorised)Confirmeduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 841(a)—
§1.1841(b)(1)(A)(vii): 1,000 kg or more / 1,000 or more plants → 10 years to lifeConfirmeduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 841(b)(1)(A)(vii)—
§1.1841(b)(1)(B)(vii): 100 kg or more / 100 or more plants → 5 to 40 yearsConfirmeduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 841(b)(1)(B)(vii)—
§1.1841(b)(1)(D): less than 50 kg of marijuana → up to 5 years, no mandatory minimumConfirmeduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 841(b)(1)(D)—
§1.1The "in brief" tier list presents (A)(vii), (B)(vii) and (D) as the complete marijuana quantity ladderCorrecteduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 841(b)"Marijuana quantity tiers under 21 U.S.C. 841(b) are: 1,000 kg or more (or 1,000 or more plants) — 10 years to life, §841(b)(1)(A)(vii); 100 kg or more (or 100 or more plants) — 5 to 40 years, §841(b)(1)(B)(vii); 50 kg or more but less than 100 kg — the general §841(b)(1)(C) tier, up to 20 years; and less than 50 kg — up to 5 years with no mandatory minimum, §841(b)(1)(D)."
§1.121 U.S.C. 844 simple possession: first offence up to 1 year + minimum $1,000 fine; one prior drug conviction 15 days–2 years + min $2,500; two or more priors 90 days–3 years + min $5,000Confirmeduscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 844 (the report cites law.cornell.edu — see Link Errors)—
§1.1The April 2026 rule did not amend 21 U.S.C. 841 or 844Confirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm (amendments are to 21 CFR Parts 1300, 1301, 1308, 1312 only)—
§1.1Gonzales v. Raich, 545 U.S. 1 (2005): Congress may regulate intrastate, state-authorised medical marijuana under the Commerce Clause (decided 6 June 2005)Confirmedsupremecourt.gov, Gonzales v. Raich, 545 U.S. 1 (2005)—
§1.1Canna Provisions petition: Canna Provisions, Inc., Gyasi Sellers, Wiseacre Farm, Inc. and Verano Holdings Corp. v. Pamela J. Bondi — Commerce Clause challenge to the CSA as applied to intrastate state-regulated marijuana; certiorari deniedConfirmedhttps://www.supremecourt.gov/orders/courtorders/121525zor_10n2.pdf—

§1.2April 2026 rule citation block: 91 FR 22714–22723, FR Doc. 2026-08176, AG Order No. 6754-2026, amending 21 CFR Parts 1300, 1301, 1308, 1312; "DATES: Effective April 28, 2026"Confirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.2The rule was dated/signed 22 April 2026 by Acting Attorney General Todd Blanche (22/23 April dates in secondary sources are the signature date / the Treasury release, not the effective date)Confirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm (signature block: "Dated: April 22, 2026. Todd Blanche, Acting Attorney General"; filed 27 April 2026)—
§1.2Legal basis: 21 U.S.C. 811(d)(1) (Single Convention implementation), acting "without regard to" the findings and procedures of 811(a)/(b) and 812(b); APA notice-and-comment treated as inapplicableConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.2Scope: the rule covers CSA marijuana, marijuana extracts, and naturally derived delta-9-THC/other compounds derived from the marijuana plant (other than mature stalks/seeds) outside the hemp definition, only to the extent they are (a) in an FDA-approved drug product or (b) subject to a state-issued licence to manufacture/distribute/dispense marijuana for medical purposesConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.2A new definition of "state medical marijuana license" was added at 21 CFR 1300.01Confirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.2Verbatim: "any form of marijuana other than in an FDA-approved drug product or marijuana subject to a state medical marijuana license remains a schedule I controlled substance…"Confirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.2The rule does not apply to synthetically derived THC ("synthetic THC remains in schedule I," e.g. delta-10-THC obtainable only by artificial synthesis)Confirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.2The rule does not affect hemp (7 U.S.C. 1639o) and does not reschedule products already removed from Schedule I (e.g. Marinol, Syndros)Confirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.2Unlicensed bulk marijuana, crops and extracts not yet in an FDA-approved product remain Schedule IConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.2New registration framework at 21 CFR 1301.13(k), with expedited review for state-licensee applicantsConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm (21 CFR 1301.13(k))—
§1.21301.13(k)(2): a state licence "shall constitute conclusive evidence" of state authorisationConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.21301.13(k)(3): DEA registration is automatically suspended if the underlying state licence is suspended, revoked or expiresConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.21301.13(k)(1)(iv): the new registrations "do not authorize" activity for non-medical purposesConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.21301.13(k)(5): state certifications may suffice in lieu of CSA prescriptions, subject to stated conditionsConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.21301.13(k)(7): DEA will "make every effort to process all applications submitted within 60 days of the publication of this regulation in the Federal Register within six months," and applicants filing in that window may operate in conformity with their state licence while the application is pendingConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm—
§1.2Registration fees: manufacturers $3,699 per year; distributors $1,850 per year; dispensers (incl. pharmacies) $888 per 3-year registrationConfirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm (91 FR 22719)—

§2.1Negligence ceiling: a producer does not commit a negligent violation, despite reasonable efforts, unless the cannabis has a total THC concentration of more than 1.0 percent on a dry weight basis — 1.0% in the final rule, not the interim rule's 0.5% (§990.29(a)(3))Confirmedhttps://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-7/subtitle-B/chapter-IX/part-990 (§990.29(a)(3))—
§2.1A producer may not commit more than one negligent violation per calendar year (§990.29(a))Confirmedhttps://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-7/subtitle-B/chapter-IX/part-990—

§2.1A negligent violation alone does not trigger criminal enforcement action by any Federal, State, Tribal or local government (§990.29(c))Confirmedhttps://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-7/subtitle-B/chapter-IX/part-990—
§2.1Non-compliant plants are marijuana under the CSA and must be disposed of via a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement, or on-site, with notice and verification (§990.27)Confirmedhttps://www.ecfr.gov/current/title-7/subtitle-B/chapter-IX/part-990 (§990.27)—

§2.2Exclusion (1)(C)(ii) (intermediate products): containing cannabinoids (I) "not capable of being naturally produced," (II) naturally producible but "synthesized or manufactured outside the plant," or (III) more than 0.3 percent combined total of total THCs (incl. Part 2.3 — Delta-8 and intoxicating hemp under current law

SectionClaim (short)VerdictPrimary source (exact URL)Corrected wording
§2.3Until the §781 effective dates, the delta-9-only test in current 7 U.S.C. 1639o(1) governs, and §781 operates prospectively onlyConfirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title7-section1639o&num=0&edition=prelim—
§2.3Blanket statement: products derived from hemp meeting the current definition — including hemp-derived delta-8 at ≤0.3% delta-9 THC — "are not CSA controlled substances"CorrectedFederal Register, 85 FR 51639 (21 Aug 2020), Docket No. DEA-500; compare https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title7-section1639o&num=0&edition=prelim"Products derived from hemp that meets the current 7 U.S.C. 1639o(1) definition — including delta-8 THC products at not more than 0.3 percent delta-9 THC — fall outside the CSA definition of 'marijuana' on the statutory text, and the Ninth Circuit has read the text that way; but DEA's stated position since its 2020 interim final rule is that synthetically derived tetrahydrocannabinols remain Schedule I controlled substances, so the federal status of converted or synthesised delta-8 is contested, not settled."
§2.3AK Futures LLC v. Boyd St. Distro, LLC, 35 F.4th 682 (9th Cir., 19 May 2022), a Lanham Act/trademark case, held hemp-derived delta-8 within the Farm Act's plain text; quote at 693: "If Congress inadvertently created a loophole legalizing vaping products containing delta-8 THC, then it is for Congress to fix its mistake"ConfirmedOpinion text (cite the govinfo USCOURTS PDF for the Ninth Circuit in the rewrite; the opinion itself was verified through case-text excerpts in this audit, not opened on uscourts.gov/govinfo)—
§2.3DEA's 2020 interim final rule, "Implementation of the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018," 85 FR 51639 (21 August 2020), Docket No. DEA-500, RIN 1117-AB53, states (at 85 FR 51641) that "all synthetically derived tetrahydrocannabinols remain Schedule I controlled substances" (flagged "Verify" in the report)ConfirmedFederal Register, 85 FR 51639 (21 August 2020), via federalregister.gov document data—
§2.3The 13 February 2023 letter from Dr. Terrence L. Boos (Chief, DEA Drug & Chemical Evaluation Section) concerned delta-8-THCO and delta-9-THCO (THC acetate esters) — not delta-8 THC itself — and stated they do not occur naturally in the cannabis plant, can only be obtained synthetically, do not fall under the definition of hemp, and are controlled in Schedule I under 21 CFR 1308.11(d)(31) / 21 U.S.C. 812(c)UnverifiedThe report's account of the letter's content — including its correction of the common misattribution of the letter to delta-8 THC itself — matches all located reproductions, but every located copy is a law-firm or other secondary reproduction; no DEA-hosted copy was found. ---

Internal contradictions — resolved against the primary source
  1. S. 2860 "died in committee" (Part 0) vs. the bill's actual record. Congress.gov's all-actions page shows S. 2860 was ordered reported (27 Sept 2023), reported with amendments and placed on the Senate Legislative Calendar, Calendar No. 215 (28 Sept 2023), with hearings on 6 Dec 2023. Resolution: the bill cleared committee; it died on the Senate calendar at the end of the 118th Congress. Corrected in the table.
  2. Hearing participant list (§1.2) vs. DEA's official list. The report lists six designated participants; DEA's rescheduling page lists seven. Resolution: DEA's page controls — the Tennessee Bureau of Investigation (participant #2) must be added.
  3. "21 CFR 1308.13(g)(2)–(4)" (§1.1) vs. the rule's amendatory text. The April 2026 rule adds paragraphs (g)(2) through (5); (g)(5) is [Reserved]. Resolution: the Federal Register text (91 FR 22714) controls; the three substantive categories are (g)(2)–(4) but the amendment runs to (g)(5).
  4. Effective date vs. signature date of the April 2026 order. Secondary sources cited around the report variously call 22 or 23 April 2026 the "effective" or "issuance" date. Resolution: FR Doc. 2026-08176 controls — the order is dated (signed) 22 April 2026 by Acting AG Todd Blanche, filed 27 April, and its DATES section states "Effective April 28, 2026." The 23 April date belongs to the separate Treasury/IRS press release SB0471. The report's own treatment is correct.
  5. GAO caseload figure (§1.2) vs. GAO-26-108623 itself. "84 completed scheduling cases, 2020–2025" collapses three different numbers. Resolution: the GAO report controls — 208 actions reviewed; HHS evaluation required for 95 (all considered); 84 of those 95 had published final decisions by 31 Dec 2025 and DEA aligned with HHS in all 84; the other 11 were under extended temporary orders.
  6. Prospective vs. completed hearing (§1.2, and Part 0's framing). §1.2 quotes DEA's page in the future tense ("scheduled to commence June 29, 2026") while later text reports the hearing as held and stayed. Resolution: the Chief ALJ's order of 14 October 2026 (DEA Docket No. 1362, Hearing Docket No. 26-96) confirms the hearing did run 29 June – 15 July 2026 and the matter remains stayed; the prospective wording must be updated, and the stay is no longer a trade-press-only claim.
  7. Government deadline around the stay motions (§1.2). The report implies the Government's response was due 13 October 2026. Resolution: the ALJ order shows the Government's opposition was filed 9 October 2026; 13 October was the response in support by SAM and the States of Idaho, Indiana and Nebraska.
  8. Dispenser registration cite (§1.2 Q&A) vs. the new regulatory text. The Q&A ties state-licensee dispensers to 21 U.S.C. 823(g). The rule's preamble does describe patient-facing entities registering as "practitioners" under 823(g), but the operative framework is the new 21 CFR 1301.13(k) (manufacturer/distributor/dispenser registrations, assessed against the 823(e)–(g) factors "as applicable"). Resolution: cite both; 823(g) alone is incomplete.
  9. §812(b) paraphrase (§1.1) vs. the statute. The report's "abuse leading only to moderate or low physical dependence…" inserts "only" and drops "may lead to." Resolution: 21 U.S.C. 812(b)(3)(C) controls — "abuse… may lead to moderate or low physical dependence or high psychological dependence."
  10. Delta-8 (§2.3) vs. itself. §2.3's opening states flatly that hemp-derived delta-8 products "are not CSA controlled substances," then two paragraphs later reports DEA's contrary position. Resolution: the statutory text and AK Futures support the hemp reading, but DEA's 2020 IFR position — all synthetically derived THCs remain Schedule I — means the status of converted delta-8 is contested; the opening sentence must be qualified (corrected wording in the table).
  11. The 11 December 2026 boundary (Part 0 / §2.2 table) vs. §2019's text. The table prints "after 11 Dec 2026" for the remainder of §781 while §2019 says the limitation applies "until December 11, 2026" without stating whether that date is inclusive, and amends no effective-date clause. Resolution: the enacted text does not resolve the boundary day; the report's caveat is correct but must sit inside the effective-date table, not only in prose.
  12. "0.03%" vs. 0.3% (§2.2). Checked and resolved in the report's favour: some secondary summaries print 0.03%; the statute (7 U.S.C. 1639o as amended) says 0.3 percent. No error in the report.

3 — Food / §301(ll)FD&C §301(ll) (21 U.S.C. 331(ll)) prohibits introducing into interstate commerce any food (including animal food/feed) to which THC or CBD has been added, because each is an active ingredient in an approved drug (or was under substantial clinical investigation)Confirmed21 U.S.C. 331(ll) (uscode.house.gov: https://uscode.house.gov/us/usc/119-120/t21/s331/); FDA-QA Q10Keep, with the statutory text corrected as in the next row. Statute prohibits food "to which has been added a drug approved under section 355 …, or a drug … for which substantial clinical investigations have been instituted and for which the existence of such investigations has been made public."
3 — Food / §301(ll)"no §301(ll)(2) regulation has been issued for any substance"ConfirmedFDA-QA Q10 ("To date, no such regulation has been issued for any substance.")Keep.
3 — Food / §301(ll)Report presents the three hemp-seed ingredients as "the Exception" to §301(ll) and does not state the statutory exceptionsCorrected21 U.S.C. 331(ll)(1)–(4) (URL above); FDA-QA Q10, Q12The hemp-seed ingredients are not a §301(ll) exception; they are separate GRAS ingredients containing only trace THC/CBD picked up in harvesting/processing. Corrected structure: "§301(ll) has four statutory exceptions — (1) the drug was marketed in food before approval/investigation; (2) an FDA regulation under §301(ll)(2) approving the use; (3) safety-enhancing use conforming to a §348 regulation, a GRAS listing/affirmation, or an effective GRAS/food-contact notification; and (4) a new animal drug whose use is not unsafe under §360b. FDA has concluded none of these is the case for THC or CBD."
3 — Food / GRASThree hemp-seed ingredients — hulled hemp seed, hemp seed protein powder, hemp seed oil — received "no questions" GRAS responses in December 2018 and may be lawfully marketed in human food as described in the notices; seeds contain only trace THC/CBD picked up in harvesting/processingConfirmedFDA-QA Q12Keep, and add the notice numbers and notifier for citability: GRN 765 (hulled hemp seed), GRN 771 (hemp seed protein powder), GRN 778 (hemp seed oil); notices submitted by Fresh Hemp Foods, Ltd.; FDA completed its evaluation in December 2018. 3 — Supplements / §201(ff)(3)(B)THC and CBD products are excluded from the dietary supplement definition under 21 U.S.C. 321(ff)(3)(B) because CBD is the active ingredient in Epidiolex and THC (dronabinol) in Marinol/SyndrosConfirmed21 U.S.C. 321(ff)(3)(B) (uscode.house.gov: https://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=(title:21+section:321+e — text: the term "does … not include — (i) an article that is approved as a new drug under section 355 …, or (ii) an article authorized for investigation as a new drug … for which substantial clinical investigations have been instituted and for which the existence of such investigations has been made public, which was not before such approval … marketed as a dietary supplement or as a food unless the Secretary … has issued a regulation, after notice and comment, finding that the article would be lawful under this chapter"); FDA-QA Q9, Q11Keep. 3 — Jan 2023 statementFDA statement of 26 Jan 2023 by Principal Deputy Commissioner Janet Woodcock, M.D.: FDA "concluded that a new regulatory pathway for CBD is needed"; denied three citizen petitions asking for rulemaking to allow CBD in supplements; "we have not found adequate evidence to determine how much CBD can be consumed, and for how long, before causing harm. Therefore, we do not intend to pursue rulemaking allowing the use of CBD in dietary supplements or conventional foods"; same conclusion for animal food; FDA would continue enforcement, prioritising risky products, while working with CongressConfirmedFDA-2023 (opened directly; statement dated January 26, 2023, "Statement From: Janet Woodcock, M.D., Principal Deputy Commissioner")Keep — every element verified against the statement text, including the three-petition denial and the animal-food sentence ("we also do not intend to pursue rulemaking allowing the use of CBD in animal food"). 3 — Enforcement (omission / currency)Report presents the January 2023 enforcement posture as FDA's current position, with no later FDA actionCorrectedFDA Commissioner letter, 1 Apr 2026, "Hemp-Derived Cannabidiol Products in Medical Research Models" (Makary to Diamantas/Hoeg): https://www.fda.gov/media/191782/downloadAdd: "On 1 April 2026 FDA announced a narrow enforcement-discretion posture: FDA does not intend to enforce FD&C §§502(f)(1) or 505 against an orally administered, hemp-derived CBD product solely because it contains CBD, provided the product (1) is manufactured, marketed and labelled consistently with the dietary supplement framework (Supplement Facts panel, structure/function claims), (2) is not contaminated, (3) is not packaged or labelled in a way attractive to, or marketed for, children, and (4) is provided to a beneficiary through a programme of medical items/services payable under Title XVIII of the Social Security Act, under the direction of the patient's treating physician, ancillary to covered items/services (the CMS Substance Access model). This does not make CBD lawful in food or supplements generally, and it does not withdraw the January 2023 conclusion." Without this, Part 3 is out of date.

SectionClaimVerdictPrimary source (URL)Corrected wording

4 — CHAMPUnder Californians Helping to Alleviate Medical Problems, Inc. (CHAMP) v. Commissioner, 128 T.C. 173 (2007), expenses of a separate, non-trafficking trade or business (in CHAMP, caregiving services) are not barred by §280E — flagged Verify ("CHAMP/CCA texts not opened on irs.gov")ConfirmedCRS Report R46709, "The Application of Internal Revenue Code Section 280E to Marijuana Businesses: Selected Legal Issues" (6 Feb 2026) (mirror: https://www.EveryCrsReport.com/reports/R46709.html) — Tax Court held CHAMP engaged in two separate trades or businesses (caregiving services; dispensing medical marijuana) and could deduct the caregiving expenses; COGS point at CHAMP, 128 T.C. at 178 n.4Resolve the flag; keep the claim and cite the case directly (128 T.C. 173 (2007)) with CRS R46709 as the official summary. Add the limit the report omits, from the same CRS analysis: later Tax Court cases (Olive, Alterman/Gibson, Harborside) refuse separate-business treatment where the activities share a "close and inseparable organizational and economic relationship" or the non-marijuana activity is merely ancillary — CHAMP is not a general apportionment rule. The IRS Chief Counsel Advice on COGS is CCA 201504011 (irs.gov CCA releases).
4 — IR-2024-177IR-2024-177 (28 June 2024), "IRS: Marijuana remains a Schedule I controlled substance; Internal Revenue Code Section 280E still applies"; "Until a final federal rule is published… marijuana remains a Schedule I controlled substance"; amended-return §280E refund claims "are not entitled to a refund or payment"; COGS sentence as quoted — report sourced it "via Tax Notes republication, index-level"ConfirmedIRS-177 (opened directly on irs.gov: "IR-2024-177, June 28, 2024" — all quoted language verified verbatim)Keep the substance; replace the source note. Corrected source line: "IRS News Release IR-2024-177, 28 June 2024, irs.gov (opened directly)" and give the URL (see Link errors). The Tax Notes detour is no longer needed.
4 — Effect of April 2026 ruleBecause §280E reaches only Schedule I/II trafficking, the DOJ rule states state medical licensees "will no longer be subject to the deduction disallowance imposed by Section 280E" (91 FR 22714)ConfirmedFR-2026, "Tax Implications" (91 FR 22720–22721): "state licensees will no longer be subject to the deduction disallowance imposed by Section 280E of the Internal Revenue Code, which applies only to businesses engaged in 'trafficking in controlled substances … in a schedule I or II,' 26 U.S.C. 280E" (stated twice in the rule)Keep, and restore the rule's own caveat, which the report's Part 4 paraphrase drops: "Nothing in this rule constitutes a determination regarding federal tax liability, and qualifying state licensees should consult with tax counsel regarding the applicability of Section 280E to their specific circumstances." The rule also "encourages the Secretary of the Treasury to consider providing retrospective relief" for earlier years — encouragement only, not relief.
4 — Treasury announcementTreasury/IRS announcement, 23 April 2026, press release SB0471 — flagged Verify (index-level): Treasury and IRS "plan to issue guidance" expected to (a) clarify that for multi-activity businesses "section 280E applies only to those activities related to trafficking in Schedule I or II controlled substances (e.g., by apportioning expenses)", and (b) include "a transition rule providing that, for purposes of section 280E, rescheduling generally will be considered to first apply for a business's full taxable year that includes the effective date of the Final Order, for the business's activities that do not involve Schedule I or II controlled substances as a result of the Final Order" — for calendar-year taxpayers, relief effective for all of 2026, but not for pre-2026 yearsConfirmedTREAS-SB0471 — "Treasury, IRS Announce Process for Tax Guidance Following DOJ Final Order on Medical Marijuana Rescheduling", April 23, 2026 (both quoted passages verified verbatim in the release; the release also states "rescheduling generally removes section 280E as a bar to claiming deductions and credits for businesses that as a result of the Final Order no longer traffic in Schedule I or II controlled substances under the CSA")Resolve the Verify flag. Keep both quotes, and keep the report's gloss with one tightening: the release is an announcement of forthcoming guidance, not guidance — the transition rule and the apportionment method do not exist as operative IRS guidance until issued. "Not for pre-2026 years" is the report's inference from the transition-rule wording; the release does not address pre-2026 years expressly — label it as an inference or delete "but not for pre-2026 years" and substitute "the release says nothing about pre-2026 years".
4 — Treasury guidance status"As of October 2026, secondary tax reporting indicates no implementing Notice/Revenue Procedure/regulation had yet issued, and the 2026–2027 Priority Guidance Plan (29 Sept 2026) lists 'Guidance under §280E' as an open project" — flagged Verify (secondary)UnverifiedNo IRS/Treasury document opened in this audit confirms either limb; the Priority Guidance Plan point is corroborated only by secondary reporting (which consistently gives: plan dated 29 Sept 2026, 121 projects, "Guidance under §280E" under "Other Priorities", no deadline stated)Keep the Verify flag. Confirm via: (1) the 2026–2027 Priority Guidance Plan PDF on irs.gov (search "Priority Guidance Plan 2026-2027" at irs.gov); (2) a search of IRS notices/revenue procedures for 2026 on irs.gov for any §280E item. Do not state "no guidance has issued" as fact until limb (2) is run.
4 — Consistency statement"280E relief today covers only state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved-product activities; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E" — stated to be consistent across Parts 1, 4 and 6ConfirmedFR-2026 (rule scope; 280E passages); TREAS-SB0471Confirmed as between Part 1 and Part 4 (both rest on the rule's two categories). Part 6 is outside this audit's scope and was not re-checked. One precision: for FDA-approved products the "relief" is academic for Marinol/Syndros/Cesamet (synthetic, never §280E marijuana businesses in the ordinary case) and nil for Epidiolex (not a controlled substance since 2020); the practical relief is for state-licensed medical marijuana activities. Recommend rewording to say so.
4 — Other rules: incomeAll marijuana/hemp income is taxable, including cash income (IRS cannabis industry page)ConfirmedIRS-CANN ("Income from any source is taxable … Many cannabis industry businesses conduct transactions in cash, which need to be reported, like any other form of payment.")Keep.
4 — Form 8300"Any person in a trade or business who receives more than $10,000 in cash in a single transaction or in related transactions must file Form 8300… within 15 days after receiving payment"ConfirmedIRS-CANN ("Large cash amounts" — verbatim)Keep verbatim. Full form name: Form 8300, Report of Cash Payments Over $10,000 Received in a Trade or Business. Statutory basis (not in the report): 26 U.S.C. 6050I.
4 — Cash payment options"the IRS offers cash tax-payment options for unbanked taxpayers"ConfirmedIRS-CANN ("Cash payment options are available for unbanked taxpayers. Some IRS Taxpayer Assistance Centers accept cash. Call 844-545-5640 … Publication 5435 provides additional details")Keep. The phone number is an IRS programme number, not a personal number — safe to reproduce.
4 — Hemp taxesHemp businesses are not §280E businesses (hemp is not a controlled substance) and are taxed under normal business rules, subject to the same Form 8300 and employment-tax obligationsConfirmed§280E by its terms (USC-280E) read with 7 U.S.C. 1639o / 21 U.S.C. 802(16)(B)(i) (hemp excluded from "marijuana"); IRS-CANN (Form 8300 and filing duties are industry-general)Keep. Note the proposition is an inference from the statute, not a sentence on the IRS cannabis page — the page does not address hemp businesses expressly. Forward-looking caveat (from Part 2, outside this audit): products that lose hemp status when the §781 redefinition takes effect (Nov/Dec 2026) become "marijuana" and their sellers can become §280E businesses — Part 4 should cross-refer rather than state the hemp point unconditionally.
4 — IRS page currency"One secondary source noted the IRS cannabis page (last reviewed 6 Feb 2026) still states the pre-rescheduling rule without qualification" — flagged VerifyCorrectedIRS-CANN (opened directly, Oct 2026: the page's §280E section still reads, in full, "Businesses that traffic marijuana in contravention of federal or state law are subject to the limitations of Internal Revenue Code (IRC) Section 280E", with no mention of Schedule III, the April 2026 rule, or the forthcoming guidance)Resolve the flag on substance: "As opened in October 2026, the IRS cannabis industry page still states the §280E rule without any reference to the April 2026 rescheduling or the announced guidance." The specific "last reviewed 6 Feb 2026" date stamp could not be read from the page as fetched — drop the date or keep it flagged.

Internal contradictions — resolved against the primary source
  1. §5.2 vs §5.1/§5.3 — which AMS page governs plan status. §5.2 builds the lists on the contact list ("Current as of March 2025") and calls it "the latest official list located", while §5.1 and §5.3 admit a separate status page exists but was "not opened directly". Resolution (AMS-STATUS, updated 17 Feb 2026, opened in this audit): the status page is newer and contradicts the contact list in four places — Ohio (own-plan → USDA licence), state counts (39/11 → 38/12), territories (Guam missing; 2 → 3), tribal own-plans (44 → 57). The status page controls for status; the contact list remains the source for contacts. All §5.2 numbers must be re-based.
  2. Part 3 Epidiolex indication vs the report's own Part 14 note. Part 3's table presents "patients 1 year and older" (all three syndromes) alongside the 25 June 2018 approval date, implying that was the approved scope in 2018; the dossier's Research Notes record that the original approval was confirmed on Drugs@FDA and that the original indications wording rested on the FDA press announcement. Resolution (FDA-QA Q3/Q22; Drugs@FDA NDA 210365): original approval 25 June 2018 = LGS/DS, age 2+; TSC and age 1+ came by supplement in 2020. Part 3 must separate "originally approved" from "currently indicated".
  3. Part 4 — IR-2024-177 sourcing. Part 4 quotes IR-2024-177 at length while labelling it "(text via Tax Notes republication, index-level)", and the Sources list likewise classes IRS material as index-level — yet the quotes are presented as verbatim. Resolution (IRS-177 opened directly): every quoted sentence is verbatim on irs.gov. The contradiction is in the sourcing label, not the text; upgrade the citation and delete the Tax Notes provenance.
  4. Part 4 — Treasury SB0471 sourcing. Same pattern: detailed verbatim quotes flagged "index-level / Verify". Resolution (TREAS-SB0471 opened): both quotes are verbatim in the 23 April 2026 release. Upgrade to Confirmed; the remaining caveat is substantive (announced guidance ≠ issued guidance), not evidentiary.
  5. Part 4 — retrospective relief. Part 4's Treasury summary says relief is "not for pre-2026 years", while the April 2026 rule it relies on "encourages" Treasury to consider retrospective relief. Resolution (FR-2026; TREAS-SB0471): the release's transition rule speaks only to the taxable year including the Final Order's effective date and is silent on earlier years; the rule's encouragement is not relief and Treasury has not adopted it in any document opened. Rewrite must present both as open, not as "no pre-2026 relief" settled law.
  6. Part 5 — dispenser fee. §5.1 gives the (k) dispenser fee as $888/3 years "(per 91 FR 22714)"; the rule's fee list in fact sits in its FDA-approved-products section and §1301.13(k) sets no fee, while DEA's dispensary portal is reported to charge $794. Resolution: state the general §1301.13(e)(1) fees as general fees, attribute the rule's list correctly, and flag the $794 portal figure as unverified-on-primary (see Still-unverified #1). Do not print "$888 (per 91 FR 22714)" as the (k) dispenser fee.
  7. Part 3 — enforcement posture vs Part 3's own date. Part 3 (accessed Oct 2026) presents the January 2023 statement's enforcement posture as current and lists no later FDA action, yet its own source page's framework is pre-2026. Resolution (FDA 1 Apr 2026 letter): the Jan 2023 conclusion stands, but a narrow Title XVIII enforcement-discretion posture was added on 1 April 2026. Part 3 must carry both, dated.
  8. Part 4 consistency claim. Part 4 asserts its 280E-relief statement is "consistent across Parts 1, 4 and 6". Within this audit's scope, Parts 1 and 4 agree (both track the rule's two categories). Part 6 was not audited here, so the three-way consistency claim is only partially verified — the rewrite should either drop "and 6" or have Part 6's auditor confirm it.
Link errors — with correct official URLs

Link errors (Parts 3–5 anchors in the HTML)

  • No broken hrefs were found among the Parts 3–5 links in the HTML: the FDA Q&A link, the FDA 26 Jan 2023 statement link, the IRS cannabis-industry link, and both AMS grower-contact-list links all resolve to the correct official pages and were opened successfully in this audit. The defects are citation defects, not href defects:
  1. IR-2024-177 cited via Tax Notes. Correct official URL: https://www.irs.gov/newsroom/irs-marijuana-remains-a-schedule-i-controlled-substance-internal-revenue-code-section-280e-still-applies
  2. AMS status page cited by a wrong/old title with no URL. Correct: "List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans" — https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review
  3. Treasury SB0471. The URL printed on the archived release reads home.treasury.gov/news/pressreleases/sb0471; Treasury's canonical pattern is https://home.treasury.gov/news/press-releases/sb0471. Verified copy used in this audit: https://fraser.stlouisfed.org/files/docs/publications/treaspr/2026/2026-04-23_sb0471.pdf — link the canonical Treasury URL in the rewrite and keep the FRED archive as a fallback.
  4. DEA portal cited as "(dea.gov)" with no URL. Correct: https://mmapplication.diversion.dea.gov/ (Diversion Control Division registration portal; lists Form 224M/224Ma).
  5. Drugs@FDA cited with no URL. Correct entry point: https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/scripts/cder/daf/index.cfm
  6. FDA Jan 2023 statement title in the report ("…on a New Regulatory Pathway") does not match FDA's current page title ("…on a New Way Forward") — same URL, fix the title text.
  7. 85 FR 51645 pinpoint. The document citation is 85 FR 51639 (21 Aug 2020); "51645" is at best an internal page. Cite the document, not the page.

Personal-data flags (must NOT be reproduced in the rewrite)

The report itself reproduces no personal phone number or personal email in Parts 3–5 — its withholding practice is correct. The risk is in its main §5.2 source: AMS-CONTACT publishes named individuals with direct-dial phones and personal/work emails, including state programme staff (e.g. the named contacts for Maine, Maryland, Nevada, North Dakota, South Dakota and Puerto Rico) and dozens of tribal contacts, several at personal or non-government addresses (gmail.com, yahoo.com and icloud.com addresses, a law-firm address, and a consultant's gmail). Do not copy any name-plus-email or name-plus-phone pair from AMS-CONTACT into the rewrite. Programme-level contacts that are safe to reproduce: Farmbill.Hemp@usda.gov and (888) 721-HEMP (4367) (USDA programme); IRS cash-appointment line 844-545-5640 (programme). For every state/tribe, link AMS-CONTACT / AMS-STATUS instead of transcribing contacts. (The DEA contact named in 91 FR 22714 is a federal official acting in an official rule-making capacity, not a licence holder; the personal-data rule in the brief concerns licence/registration holders — no Part 3–5 licence holder is named as an individual anywhere in the report.)

7.2"The April 2026 rule moved the two categories from the Schedule I import/export regime (essentially research-only) to the Schedule III regime — a procedural easing for registered importers/exporters"Correctedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm (91 FR 22714, at 22718–22719 and regulatory text at 22723)"The April 2026 rule added the two new Schedule III categories to the §1312.30 list of non-narcotic Schedule III–V substances that require an import/export permit. Its stated purpose was continuity, not easing: because the categories were leaving Schedule I (where a permit was already required), listing them in §1312.30 ensures 'all importations and exportations of products containing marijuana remain subject to the permit requirement' under the Single Convention. Commercial import of state-legal marijuana would still need DEA importer/exporter registration and a permit for each shipment, and compliance with 21 U.S.C. 952/953."

8.2Pre-2026 text of 27 CFR 478.11: an unlawful user/addict included "any person who is a current user of a controlled substance in a manner other than as prescribed by a licensed physician," with an inference of current use from (i) a conviction within the past year, (ii) multiple arrests within the past 5 years if the most recent was within the past year, or (iii) a drug test within the past yearCorrected (incomplete)https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-01-22/html/2026-01141.htm (91 FR 2698, at 2699–2700, reciting the 1997 definition, 62 FR 34634)Add the two omitted elements: (1) the pre-2026 "addicted" prong — a person who "has lost the power of self-control with reference to the use of controlled substances"; and (2) a fourth inference example — "for current or former Armed Forces members, a recent disciplinary or other administrative action based on confirmed drug use (including a court-martial conviction, non-judicial punishment, or administrative discharge based on drug use or rehabilitation failure)." The IFR removed all the inference examples, not only the three listed.
8.2New text is from an ATF interim final rule, "Revising Definition of 'Unlawful User of or Addicted to Controlled Substance,'" RIN 1140-AB03, "Docket ATF-2026-0034 / Rule 2025R-54T," effective 22 Jan 2026, comments to 30 June 2026Corrected (citation incomplete)https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-01-22/html/2026-01141.htm ; https://www.atf.gov/rules-and-regulations/rulemaking-notices/revising-definition-unlawful-user-or-addicted-to-controlled-substance-rin-1140-ab03Cite it as: 91 FR 2698 (22 Jan 2026), FR Doc. 2026-01141, Docket No. ATF-2026-0034; ATF No. 2025R-54T, RIN 1140-AB03 — effective 22 Jan 2026; comments on or before 30 June 2026. 8.2New 27 CFR 478.11 definition, as quoted: "(1) A person who uses a controlled substance and demonstrates a pattern of compulsively using the controlled substance, characterized by impaired control over use, is addicted… (2) A person who regularly uses a controlled substance over an extended period of time continuing into the present, without a lawful prescription or in a manner substantially different from that prescribed by a licensed physician, is an unlawful user… (ii) A person is not an unlawful user… if the person has ceased regularly unlawfully using the substance, or if the person's unlawful use is isolated or sporadic… A person is also not an unlawful user if the person, while using a lawfully prescribed controlled substance, deviates slightly or immaterially from the instructions of the prescribing physician."Confirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-01-22/html/2026-01141.htm (91 FR 2707–2708, regulatory text)— (verified word-for-word. Note for completeness: paragraph (i) also contains a sentence the report elides — "A person may be an unlawful current user of a controlled substance even though the substance is not being used at the precise time the person seeks to acquire, ship, transport, receive, or possess the firearm." The report's ellipsis covers the omission.)
8.2ATF's stated rationale: the single-incident inferences "had produced thousands of NICS denials (≈4,284/yr per ATF; FY2025: 9,163 (g)(3) denials, >half single-incident-based, per the rule preamble as reported)"Correctedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-01-22/html/2026-01141.htm (91 FR 2701–2702, Tables 1–2 and accompanying text)"In FY2025 NICS denied 9,163 transfers under the §922(g)(3) prohibitor. Of the 8,817 standard denials, 4,284 (adjusted for duplicates) were predicated on a single-use inference; total single-incident inference responses were 4,560 — just under half (49.8%) of the 9,163, not 'more than half.' ATF did not refer 8,893 of the 9,163 cases for further investigation. ATF projects about 4,284 persons per year were being denied on the basis of inferences the IFR removes." (Delete ">half single-incident-based.")

8.5Held (syllabus, verbatim): "The government's prosecution of Mr. Hemani under §922(g)(3)'s unlawful user provision is inconsistent with the Second Amendment."Confirmedhttps://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/25pdf/24-1234_new_6k47.pdf (syllabus at 1)—
8.5Hemani reasoning: the government conceded its construction "automatically ban[s]" a user "regardless of what controlled substance an individual uses, in what amounts, whether his drug use has ever made him a danger to himself or others, why he keeps a gun, or how safely he does so"; the "habitual drunkard" vagrancy/civil-commitment/surety analogues fail on why and how (historical drunkards were incapacitated and "incapable of conducting [their] own affairs"; those laws required process before liberty was lost, whereas §922(g)(3) as construed "automatically divests… without any pre-deprivation process")Confirmedhttps://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/25pdf/24-1234_new_6k47.pdf (syllabus at 2–4)—
8.5United States v. Connelly, 117 F.4th 269 (5th Cir. 5 Sept 2024): §922(g)(3) not facially unconstitutional, but unconstitutional as applied to Connelly (occasional marijuana use as a sleep/anxiety aid, not shown intoxicated while possessing); history supports banning carrying while actively intoxicated, not possession by regular-but-sober usersConfirmedhttps://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/25pdf/24-1234_new_6k47.pdf (petition-stage materials summarising Connelly, 117 F.4th at 272, 275–276: "no historical justification for disarming sober citizens not presently under an impairing influence"); opinion itself via https://www.ca5.uscourts.gov/opinions/ (No. 23-50866)—

The report omits the remand reporter citation." Keep the Verify flag on post-Hemani cert status.

Delete 'Remand proceedings pending as of Oct 2026.'" (The 3 Nov 2025 dismissal is taken from the district court's order; verify on the W.D. Okla. docket via PACER before republication.)
8.58th Circuit: no official (g)(3) opinion under the name Seher could be confirmed; the citable comparator is United States v. Jackson, 69 F.4th 495 (8th Cir. 2023), upholding §922(g)(1) — a (g)(1) case, not (g)(3)Confirmedhttps://www.ca8.uscourts.gov/ (Jackson, 69 F.4th 495)— (the report's handling — flagging Seher and substituting Jackson with the (g)(1)/(g)(3) distinction — is correct)
8.511th Circuit: no published post-Bruen merits decision invalidating or squarely upholding (g)(3) was located — Verify/UnavailableUnverified—Keep the flag; confirm via https://www.ca11.uscourts.gov/opinions and the Hemani cert-stage filings, which survey the circuit split
8.5Range v. Attorney General, 69 F.4th 96 (3d Cir. 2023) (en banc): GVR'd after Rahimi, re-decided en banc Dec 2024 reaching the same result — §922(g)(1) unconstitutional as applied to Range; Range is a (g)(1) case and does not control (g)(3)Confirmedhttps://www.ca3.uscourts.gov/ (Range, 69 F.4th 96; on remand, en banc, Dec 2024)—
8.6Hemp is excluded from the CSA definition of marijuana (21 U.S.C. 802(16)(B)) and is not a controlled substance, so a user of lawful hemp/CBD (≤0.3% THC, current definition) is not an "unlawful user of… any controlled substance" by virtue of that hemp use alone; product >0.3% THC is marijuana, not hempConfirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title21-section802&num=0&edition=prelim—
8.6§922(g)(3) does not say "Schedule I" — it says "unlawful user of… any controlled substance (as defined in… 21 U.S.C. 802)," so moving a category to Schedule III does not by itself remove its users from (g)(3); a Schedule III substance used without a lawful prescription / otherwise unlawfully is still used "unlawfully"Confirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title18-section922&num=0&edition=prelim—
8.6"For other Schedule III drugs, lawful use means use pursuant to a valid prescription from a DEA-registered practitioner (21 U.S.C. 829(b); 21 CFR 1306.04); state medical programmes use recommendations/certifications, not federal prescriptions"Corrected (incomplete after April 2026)https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htm (91 FR 22714, "Registration of State Licensees" discussion)Add: "The April 2026 rule narrows that gap for the new category: for state-licensed medical marijuana, state-authorised medical marijuana certifications or similar documents are sufficient to permit dispensing by a 21 CFR 1301.13(k) registrant, provided they include the user's name and address, are dated and signed on the day of issuance, and identify the issuing practitioner. The prescription framework of 21 U.S.C. 829(b)/21 CFR 1306.04 continues to govern FDA-approved drug products."
8.6Bottom line (a): adult-use users unchanged — Schedule I, federally unlawful, (g)(3) applies by its terms (subject to Hemani and the new regular-use definition)Confirmed91 FR 22714; 91 FR 2698; Hemani slip op. (above)—
8.6Bottom line (b): for state-licensed medical users the "unlawful" predicate is now contestable and agency trajectory points toward treating compliant state-medical use as not unlawful, but as of 23 Oct 2026 no final ATF rule, final Form 4473, or DOJ guidance states that conclusion in so many words — VerifyConfirmed (as a negative finding)https://www.atf.gov/rules-and-regulations/rulemaking-notices/revising-definition-unlawful-user-or-addicted-to-controlled-substance-rin-1140-ab03 ; https://public-inspection.federalregister.gov/2026-09182.pdf— (strengthened by two audit findings the report missed: the 2011 Open Letter's rescission, and the IFR's "without a lawful prescription" wording, which neither includes nor excludes state certifications)
8.6Bottom line (c): FDA-approved drug products (Epidiolex etc.) used as prescribed are lawful use — not (g)(3) unlawful useConfirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title18-section922&num=0&edition=prelim ; 27 CFR 478.11 as amended (91 FR 2707–2708)— (note: Epidiolex itself has not been a controlled substance at all since 2020 — see Part 3 audit — so it is an a fortiori example)
8.6Even where use remains "unlawful," a status-only in-home-possession prosecution of a regular marijuana user may not proceed on the automatic theory; what remains prosecutable by Hemani's own reservation: addicts; persons presently intoxicated; cases with individualised proof of danger; any new narrower statute. FFL transfer law (§922(d)(3)) was not before the CourtConfirmedhttps://www.supremecourt.gov/opinions/25pdf/24-1234_new_6k47.pdf (syllabus at 4)— (add, per the reservation row above, prosecutions supported by "proof that a certain drug always renders its users dangerous")

Internal contradictions — resolved against the primary source
  1. §6.1 vs §6.1 (FinCEN counts). The report says the Marijuana Banking Updates "stopped after Q2 FY2020" and that the latest official DI count is "Unavailable," yet in the same section reports a Q3 FY2019 cumulative SAR total as its latest FinCEN figure. Resolution: the updates continued — Q4 FY2020, Q2 FY2021 and Q4 FY2021 are on fincen.gov. The latest FinCEN DI count is 755 (Q4 FY2021, through 30 Sept 2021); the Q3 FY2019 SAR figures are accurate but are not the latest data in the series. The report also missed that FinCEN data is the basis of the GAO-26-107498 figure it flagged as secondary-only.
  2. §6.2 vs Part 0 (S. 2860). Part 6's table correctly records that S. 2860 was reported and placed on Senate Legislative Calendar No. 215. Any "died in committee" shorthand elsewhere in the report contradicts it. Resolution (congress.gov): reported out 28 Sept 2023, Calendar No. 215; died on the calendar at the end of the 118th Congress. Part 6 is right.
  3. §7.2 vs the April 2026 rule itself (§1312.30). The report calls the §1312.30 change a "procedural easing" from the Schedule I regime, while elsewhere (§7.2, first paragraph) it correctly stresses that Schedule III status loosens nothing without DEA registration. Resolution (91 FR 22714): the rule added the new categories to the §1312.30 permit list precisely to prevent an easing — to keep every import/export of marijuana products subject to a permit as the Single Convention requires. §7.2's characterisation is reversed in the TEXT EDITS.
  4. §7.3 vs §7.3 (retention period). The section quotes both the 2019 "2 years" text and the 2021 "3 years" text. This is not a contradiction once dated — the report itself flags the trap — and the audit confirms: 2019 = 2 years (superseded); 2021/current = 3 years. Resolved in favour of the report's own "Trap" note.
  5. **§8.2 vs §8.5 (Hemani timing).** §8.2 says the IFR's rationale "conflicted with circuit case law requiring regular, contemporaneous use" and treats the IFR as the last word on the definition, while §8.5 correctly records that Hemani (June 2026) post-dates the IFR (Jan 2026) and that no post-Hemani revision had issued. Resolution: the IFR remains the operative regulatory text, but it was written before Hemani; any statement of current law must present the definition as "IFR (Jan 2026), not yet revised after Hemani (June 2026)." The report's §8.2 Verify flag is the correct treatment and is confirmed.
  6. §8.4 vs §8.6 (medical card). §8.4 states the 2011 letter's card = "reasonable cause" rule as ATF guidance, then asks whether it survives; §8.6(b) concludes there is no published safe harbour either way. Resolution: both are superseded in part by the audit finding that ATF's index now marks the 2011 letter rescinded — the card rule's only ATF source document no longer stands, so §8.4 must be rewritten in the past tense and §8.6(b)'s "no primary source states that conclusion in so many words" should add "and the 2011 letter that stated the opposite conclusion has been rescinded."
  7. §8.3 vs §8.3 (comment dates). The report reports "6/7 July–6 Aug 2026 — dates conflict in secondary reports." Resolution (NPRM, FR Doc. 2026-09182): no conflict — 6 Aug 2026 is the 90-day comment deadline for the proposed rule; ≈7 July 2026 is the 60-day deadline for the form information collection. Two notices, two clocks.
  8. Part 8 headline vs §8.5 (vote). The headline and §8.5 give Hemani's lineup in full but nowhere state the vote. Resolution (slip op. syllabus): judgment unanimous (9–0); majority opinion joined by 7 justices; Alito, J., concurring in the judgment (joined by Kagan, J.). Add the vote to the headline.

Audit — Parts 9, 10 and 11: Federal Cannabis & Hemp Law Report

Scope: Part 9 (Employment & Drug Testing), Part 10 (Immigration), Part 11 (Tribal Land) only. Method: Primary sources only — transportation.gov / ODAPC, eCFR, Federal Register / govinfo, uscode.house.gov, opm.gov material as reproduced in official-adjacent records, dni.gov (ODNI), samhsa.gov / Federal Register certified-lab notices, uscis.gov Policy Manual (live text opened), cdc.gov Technical Instructions, justice.gov, ams.usda.gov, app.leg.wa.gov, army.mil / navy.mil. Where only news, law-firm, trade-press or court-reporter secondary material exists, verdict = Unverified (per brief), even if the claim is probably true. Audit date: 23 October 2026 (report's own access date). Post-report primary pages live at audit time are used and dated. Headline finding: The report's central premise for Parts 9–10 — "no change / no revised guidance after the April 2026 rescheduling" — is wrong as stated for DOT. The outcome (marijuana testing unchanged) is right, but DOT did issue revised guidance: the 19 Dec 2025 ODAPC marijuana notice, the 15 May 2026 ODAPC/OGC Part 40 Q&A on the Schedule III order, and a 19 May 2026 final rule amending the oral-fluid rule. The report missed all three because it relied on a secondary report of an HHS statement instead of transportation.gov.

9.1"DOT had issued no revised MRO guidance for [state-medical, now Schedule III] marijuana as of Oct 2026 — Verify"Correctedhttps://www.transportation.gov/odapc/ListServe_NoticesFalse — flag resolved against the report. ODAPC Listserve, 15 May 2026: "ODAPC Publishes Official Q&A Today." The Q&A (ODAPC with the Office of the General Counsel, official Part 40 guidance) answers the exact question — can an MRO verify a lab-confirmed marijuana positive as negative if the employee claims a state-licensed product? — "No. Currently, there is no instance when the MRO could verify a laboratory-confirmed marijuana positive drug test result as 'negative' when an employee claims the positive was caused by a State licensed marijuana product," because state-dispensed marijuana is not FDA-approved, cannot be lawfully prescribed under federal law, and a "legitimate medical explanation" requires a legally prescribed controlled substance used in compliance with federal law (49 CFR 40.137(a), 40.141(b)). State medical cards, physician recommendations and dispensary records do not satisfy it.

Note the May 2026 Q&A deliberately does not rest on §40.151(e) for Schedule III medical marijuana — it rests on §§40.137/40.141 (no lawful federal prescription). The report should say so.

(The report's cross-reference "same predicate analysed in Part 8" should point to §3343(b)'s "unlawful user" language.)
9.3ODNI clarification, 21 Dec 2021 (DNI Avril Haines): prior marijuana use not automatically disqualifying; weigh recency/frequency and willingness to comply going forward; current use and investment in/involvement with marijuana businesses remain relevant under Guidelines H, E and JCorrectedhttps://archive.dni.gov/index.php/ncsc-how-we-work/ncsc-security-executive-agent/ncsc-policy?tmpl=component&print=1Document confirmed on ODNI's policy page: "Security Executive Agent Clarifying Guidance Concerning Marijuana for Agencies Conducting Adjudications of Persons Proposed for Eligibility for Access to Classified Information or Eligibility to Hold a Sensitive Position (December 2021)," signed 21 Dec 2021. Its operative sentence is: "Disregard of federal law pertaining to marijuana remains relevant, but not determinative" to adjudications. Corrections: (1) the guidance addresses three topics — recency of recreational use, use of CBD products, and investment in marijuana businesses; the report omits the CBD topic entirely (guidance warns CBD products may exceed 0.3% THC and cause positive tests/investigations). (2) On investment, the standard is narrower than the report states: eligibility "may be impacted negatively" by knowing and direct investment in stocks/businesses of marijuana growers and retailers while cultivation/distribution remains illegal under the CSA; indirect investment through a diversified mutual fund publicly traded on a U.S. exchange is presumed unwitting, and divestment/disassociation is mitigating. (3) Agencies are encouraged to advise candidates to refrain from any future use upon signing the SF-86 certification. The "Guidelines H, E and J" attribution was not in the guidance text located — delete or source it.

9.4Drug-Free Workplace Act, 41 U.S.C. 8101–8106; §8102(a)(1) quoted: non-individual over the simplified acquisition threshold must publish a prohibition statement, run an awareness programme, give each contract employee the statement, require 5-day employee notice of a workplace criminal-drug conviction, notify the agency within 10 days, sanction/rehabilitate within 30 days, and make a continuing good-faith effortConfirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title41-section8102&num=0&edition=prelimVerbatim core confirmed against the U.S. Code text, including the 5-day / 10-day / 30-day periods and subparagraphs (A)–(G). Two precision corrections: (1) §8102(a)(1) by its terms excludes contracts for commercial products/commercial services — the report omits the exclusion. (2) §8102(a)(2) imposes parallel (slightly shorter) duties on individual contractors over the threshold; the report's "non-individual" framing should not imply individual contractors are outside §8102.
9.4"§8103 imposes parallel duties on grantees; §8104 authorises suspension/termination/debarment (up to 5 years); §8106 defines terms"Confirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title41-chapter81&num=0&edition=prelimConfirmed in substance: §8103 (grantees, incl. individuals at §8103(a)(2)), §8104 (violation consequences — suspension of payments, suspension/termination of contract or grant, and debarment for a period not to exceed 5 years), §8106 (definitions/construction).
9.4"The Act does NOT require drug testing… Its prohibition is a workplace prohibition — not a federal ban on employees' off-duty state-legal use, though a contractor may adopt a stricter policy"Confirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?req=granuleid:USC-prelim-title41-section8102&num=0&edition=prelimConfirmed: nothing in §§8102–8103 requires testing; the required statement prohibits unlawful manufacture/distribution/dispensation/possession/use "in the person's workplace," and the conviction-notification duty is limited to violations "occurring in the workplace." Testing authorities listed (DOT Part 40, NRC 10 CFR Part 26, agency contract clauses, employer policy) are correctly identified as separate authorities.
9.5UCMJ Article 112a (10 U.S.C. 912a): wrongful use/possession/manufacture/distribution/importation/introduction onto an installation of a controlled substance (incl. marijuana) punishable by court-martial; wrongful use may be inferred from metabolites absent lawful justification; no military prescription channel for state-programme marijuanaConfirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title10-section912a&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimConfirmed in substance. Add the distinction the report blurs in the next paragraph: hemp/CBD as such is not punishable under Article 112a (hemp is excluded from the CSA definition of marijuana); the hemp ban is punishable as violation of a lawful general order/regulation under Article 92 (10 U.S.C. 892) — which is how the service orders are framed.
9.5DoD hemp/CBD ban: Memorandum of the (Acting) Under Secretary of Defense for Personnel and Readiness, 26 Feb 2020, "effective 1 Mar 2020": all services must issue punitive general orders prohibiting use of hemp products, to "preserve the integrity of the drug testing program…"Correctedhttps://home.army.mil/belvoir/index.php/about/Garrison/public-affairs/digital-belvoir-eagle/reminder-cbd-oil-remains-illegal-dod-personnelMemo confirmed: Acting USD(P&R) Matthew Donovan, 26 Feb 2020. Wording correction: 1 Mar 2020 was the deadline by which services were directed to issue general orders/regulations, not the memo's own "effective date." The memo's stated rationale (as issued): a finding of military necessity "to ensure the military drug testing program continues to be able to identify the use of marijuana" and to protect readiness — quote that, rather than the report's compressed paraphrase presented as a quotation. No 2025–2026 rescission was located (see Unverified list — negative finding).
9.5Army Regulation 600-85 (23 July 2020), quoted: use of hemp products incl. CBD, "regardless of the product's THC concentration, claimed or actual, and regardless of whether such product may lawfully be bought, sold, and used under the law applicable to civilians, is prohibited" — incl. ingested, inhaled, food, topical lotions/oils, soaps and shampoos; exceptions only FDA-approved prescribed drugs (e.g. Epidiolex) and durable goodsConfirmedhttps://home.army.mil/gordon/download_file/force/1337/464Confirmed verbatim, AR 600-85, para. 4-2 (23 July 2020). Complete the exception list the report truncates: the prohibition also does not apply to (1) legitimate law-enforcement activities, (2) authorised personnel in performance of medical duties, or (3) use without knowledge the product was hemp-derived where the lack of knowledge is honest and reasonable. The provision is expressly punitive under Art. 92(1), UCMJ, and a general-intent offence.
9.5"Navy ALNAV 074/20 is to the same effect" [as AR 600-85, incl. soaps/shampoos]Correctedhttps://www.opss.org/infographic/alnav-07420-prohibits-sailors-and-marines-using-products-made-or-derived-hemp-includingSubstantially correct for 074/20 but the report invites a known conflation. ALNAV 074/19 (SecNav Richard V. Spencer) prohibited knowing use of hemp products but expressly exempted topical products such as shampoos, conditioners, lotions and soaps. ALNAV 074/20 (2020, SecNav Kenneth J. Braithwaite) superseded it and removed that exemption, expressly including "topical products containing hemp, such as shampoos, conditioners, lotions, lip balms, or soaps," with exceptions for FDA-approved prescribed formulations (Marinol/Syndros, Epidiolex), unknowing use, and durable goods. Cite 074/20 by year/signer and note 074/19 is superseded; do not cite the Spencer text for the current rule.
9.5"VA/DoD distinction: DoD prohibits and punishes; VA neither provides marijuana nor punishes mere participation in state programmes — but VA employees are federal employees under EO 12564"Unverifiedhttps://www.archives.gov/federal-register/codification/executive-order/12564.htmlThe EO 12564 limb is confirmed (VA employees are federal employees). 10 (header)"Run 2 — no USCIS update reflecting the April 2026 split schedule was located as of 23 Oct 2026 — Verify"Confirmedhttps://www.uscis.gov/policy-manual/volume-12-part-f-chapter-5Confirmed by direct opening of the live chapter at audit time: Vol. 12, Part F, Ch. 5 still states, without qualification, "Marijuana, however, remains classified as a 'Schedule I' controlled substance under the federal CSA" (§C.2). 10.18 U.S.C. 1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II): alien convicted of, or who admits having committed / admits acts constituting the essential elements of, a violation of any law "relating to a controlled substance (as defined in [21 U.S.C. 802])" is inadmissibleConfirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title8-section1182&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimVerbatim text: "any alien convicted of, or who admits having committed, or who admits committing acts which constitute the essential elements of— … (II) a violation of (or a conspiracy or attempt to violate) any law or regulation of a State, the United States, or a foreign country relating to a controlled substance (as defined in section 802 of title 21), is inadmissible." The report omits "or a conspiracy or attempt to violate" and the State/foreign-country scope — restore both.
10.1"…except the single-offence simple-possession-of-30-grams-or-less-of-marijuana tradition (see the §1182(h) waiver…)" — presented as an exception to §1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II)Correctedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title8-section1182&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimThere is no 30-gram exception in §1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II) itself. The ground applies regardless of amount. The 30-gram figure appears only (i) in the waiver, §1182(h), which permits waiver of the controlled-substance ground only "insofar as it relates to a single offense of simple possession of 30 grams or less of marijuana" (and only for an applicant meeting §1182(h)'s family/rehabilitation routes), and (ii) in the deportability exception, §1227(a)(2)(B)(i), and the GMC regulation, 8 CFR 316.10(b)(2)(iii). Rewrite so no reader thinks ≤30 g possession is not a ground of inadmissibility.
10.1"No conviction is required: a qualifying admission suffices, if made with the Matter of K- safeguards"Unverifiedhttps://www.uscis.gov/policy-manual/volume-12-part-f-chapter-5The no-conviction/admission proposition is confirmed by the statute (above) and by USCIS PM F.5 fn. 26 ("long held requirements for a valid 'admission'"). The Matter of K- citation itself (BIA precedent on formal admission safeguards) was not re-opened on an official EOIR/justice.gov source in this audit. Keep the proposition; cite the precedent as: Matter of K-, 2 I&N Dec. 253 (BIA 1945) — pin-cite Unverified, confirm via justice.gov/eoir or govinfo.
10.18 U.S.C. 1182(a)(1)(A)(iv): alien determined (per HHS regulations, 42 CFR Part 34) to be a drug abuser or addict is inadmissible; "sustained remission (CDC Technical Instructions: generally ≥12 months) removes the finding"Correctedhttps://www.cdc.gov/immigrant-refugee-health/hcp/panel-physicians/mental-health.htmlStatutory limb confirmed verbatim: "who is determined (in accordance with regulations prescribed by the Secretary of Health and Human Services) to be a drug abuser or addict, is inadmissible" — cite as §1182(a)(1)(A)(iv). Remission limb is understated: current CDC Technical Instructions for Panel Physicians require, for CSA substance-use disorders, both (1) at least 12 consecutive months meeting no DSM criteria (except craving) and (2) full abstinence verified by a minimum of four random laboratory screenings on short notice over the 12-month period. Replace "generally ≥12 months" with that two-part test. Parallel instructions apply to civil surgeons.
10.18 U.S.C. 1182(a)(6)(C)(ii): false claim to U.S. citizenship "for any purpose or benefit" is inadmissible and generally waiver-poor; relevance is the false claim (e.g. I-9 attestation, registering/voting in connection with licensing), not the marijuanaConfirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title8-section1182&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimConfirmed, with two elements the report omits: the claim must have been made on or after 30 Sept 1996, and "for any purpose or benefit under this chapter or any other Federal or State law." Limited exceptions exist at §1182(a)(6)(C)(iii) (e.g. certain children of U.S. citizens/permanent residents who reasonably believed they were citizens). "Waiver-poor" is fair summary — there is no §1182(h)-type waiver for this ground for immigrants — but say that expressly instead of "generally waiver-poor."
10.18 U.S.C. 1227(a)(2)(B): convicted after admission of a controlled-substance offence (other than "a single offense involving possession for one's own use of 30 grams or less of marijuana") is deportable; §1227(a)(2)(B)(ii) separately makes a person who "is, or at any time after admission has been, a drug abuser or addict" deportableConfirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title8-section1227&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimVerbatim confirmed for both clauses: (B)(i) "Conviction — Any alien who at any time after admission has been convicted of a violation of (or a conspiracy or attempt to violate) any law or regulation of a State, the United States, or a foreign country relating to a controlled substance (as defined in section 802 of title 21), other than a single offense involving possession for one's own use of 30 grams or less of marijuana, is deportable"; (B)(ii) "Drug abusers and addicts — Any alien who is, or at any time after admission has been, a drug abuser or addict is deportable." Note (B)(i) requires a conviction (an admission alone does not trigger it, unlike §1182) — the report should state this asymmetry expressly.
10.1Good moral character, 8 U.S.C. 1101(f)(3) and 8 CFR 316.10: a person who during the statutory period committed an offence described in §1182(a)(2), incl. a controlled-substance violation, cannot establish GMC, "subject to the single-offence ≤30 g marijuana exception in §1101(f)(3)'s flush language"Correctedhttps://www.uscis.gov/policy-manual/volume-12-part-f-chapter-5Substance confirmed; attribution corrected. The operative exception is stated in 8 CFR 316.10(b)(2)(iii)–(iv) and USCIS PM F.5 §C.3 ("The conditional bar… does not apply if the violation was for a single offense of simple possession of 30 grams or less of marijuana," extended to related paraphernalia offences). §1101(f)(3) bars GMC for a person described in §1182(a)(2)(A) (among other paragraphs); do not attribute the 30 g exception to "flush language" in §1101(f)(3) without a pinpoint quote.

10.2USCIS Policy Alert, 19 Apr 2019 (PA-2019-02), "Controlled Substance-Related Activity and Good Moral Character Determinations": violations of federal controlled substance law, including marijuana, are generally a bar to GMC for naturalization even where not an offence under state law; a person involved in certain marijuana-related activities may lack GMC even if the activity has been decriminalised under state lawConfirmedhttps://www.uscis.gov/policy-manual/volume-12-part-f-chapter-5Confirmed; the alert's highlights and its citation (Vol. 12, Part F, Ch. 5, Conditional Bars for Acts in Statutory Period [12 USCIS-PM F.5]) match the live chapter. 10.2Vol. 12, Part F, Ch. 5, §C: GMC bar for violation of "any controlled substance-related federal or state law or regulation of the United States or law or regulation of any foreign country" during the statutory period; triggered by conviction or admission; covers manufacture/production (incl. cultivation), distribution, dispensing, possession; employment in the marijuana industry (and benefitting from a spouse's/parent's trafficking) can establish it; exception for single offence of simple possession of ≤30 g marijuanaConfirmedhttps://www.uscis.gov/policy-manual/volume-12-part-f-chapter-5Confirmed against live text (§§C.1–C.3): bar applies to a conviction, an admission to the offence, or an admission to acts constituting the essential elements; trafficking and financially benefitting from a spouse/parent's trafficking are expressly included; "possession of marijuana for recreational or medical purposes or employment in the marijuana industry may constitute conduct that violates federal controlled substance laws." Add the chapter's burden caveat the report only paraphrases: even without a conviction or valid admission, the applicant "may be unable to meet the burden of proof to show that he or she has not committed such an offense."
10.2Chapter's marijuana text: "Marijuana, however, remains classified as a 'Schedule I' controlled substance under the federal CSA" and CSA-violating conduct "continues to constitute a conditional bar to GMC… even where such activity is not a criminal offense under state law"Confirmedhttps://www.uscis.gov/policy-manual/volume-12-part-f-chapter-5Verbatim confirmed in the live chapter, §C.2, at audit time. This is the single most important Part 10 fact: USCIS's published instruction is unrevised after the April 2026 split schedule, so the sentence is simultaneously (a) an accurate quotation and (b) no longer accurate as a blanket statement of federal scheduling. The rewrite must present it as a dated quotation, not as the report's own assertion.
10.2"An admission of marijuana use to a USCIS officer or civil surgeon can support both a GMC bar and §1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II)/(a)(1)(A)(iv) findings"Correctedhttps://www.uscis.gov/policy-manual/volume-12-part-f-chapter-5Overstated as written. A GMC admission must meet the "long held requirements for a valid 'admission'" (PM F.5 fn. 26). A statement to a civil surgeon/panel physician is a medical-history disclosure supporting a §1182(a)(1)(A)(iv) drug-abuser/addict determination under the CDC Technical Instructions; it is not, without more, a formal §1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II) admission, which requires the admission safeguards. Separate the two routes.

10.3"No USCIS policy alert, Policy Manual update, or Federal Register notice revising the marijuana/GMC guidance to reflect the split schedule was located on uscis.gov as of 23 Oct 2026 — Verify; 2025–2026 policy change: none located in either direction"Confirmedhttps://www.uscis.gov/policy-manual/volume-12-part-f-chapter-5Confirmed as a dated negative finding by direct inspection of the live chapter (still "Schedule I," §C.2) and the absence of a superseding policy alert in the chapter's revision history.
