10.3Analytical note: INA grounds turn on "controlled substance (as defined in 21 U.S.C. 802)" — marijuana in both schedules remains a controlled substance, so rescheduling alone does not repeal the immigration groundsConfirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title8-section1182&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimConfirmed as statutory analysis: §§1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II) and 1227(a)(2)(B)(i) incorporate 21 U.S.C. 802's definition without regard to schedule. Label it analysis, not agency guidance (the report does — keep the label).
10.3Analytical note (cont.): use "genuinely 'under a state medical marijuana licence' is now a Schedule III category the federal rule recognises, which undercuts the premise that that conduct 'violated federal law'"Correctedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htmOverbroad — narrow it. 91 FR 22714 (28 Apr 2026) places in Schedule III only (a) FDA-approved drug products containing marijuana and (b) marijuana/products subject to a state-issued licence to manufacture, distribute and/or dispense for medical purposes, with an expedited DEA registration pathway for those licensees (21 CFR 1301.13(k)). It does not make an individual patient's possession/use federally lawful: a Schedule III substance still requires a valid federal prescription/registration framework, and state medical cards are not federal prescriptions (the same reasoning DOT adopted, §9.1). Any "no federal violation" argument is therefore at most an argument about licensed manufacture/distribution/dispensing activity by a registrant, not about patient use or industry employment generally. Rewrite accordingly and keep "no BIA precedent, AAO adopted decision, or federal appellate decision resolving that argument was located as of Oct 2026" as Unverified/negative finding.
10.3"Adult-use, industry employment by noncitizens, and use outside a state medical licence are unchanged: still Schedule I, still a federal violation for immigration purposes"Confirmedhttps://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2026-04-28/html/2026-08176.htmConfirmed: the April 2026 final rule by its terms reaches only the FDA-approved and state-medical-licensed categories; all other marijuana remains Schedule I.
11.1"Indian country" defined at 18 U.S.C. 1151 (reservations, dependent Indian communities, allotments)Confirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title18-section1151&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimConfirmed: §1151(a) reservations, (b) dependent Indian communities, (c) allotments (rights-of-way included).
11.1"The CSA is a law of general applicability and applies in Indian Country; there is no tribal exemption in 21 U.S.C. 812/841/844. Tribal sovereignty does not displace the CSA"Confirmedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title21-section812&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimConfirmed as a statement of statutory text: no tribal exemption appears in the CSA provisions cited. 11.1Criminal jurisdiction allocated by Major Crimes Act (18 U.S.C. 1153), General Crimes Act (18 U.S.C. 1152), tribal inherent jurisdiction capped by ICRA (25 U.S.C. 1302, as enhanced by VAWA/TLOA), and Public Law 280 (18 U.S.C. 1162; 25 U.S.C. 1321–1326)Correctedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title25-section1302&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimFramework confirmed; the report omits the operative caps it invokes. ICRA as enhanced: tribal courts may impose up to 3 years' imprisonment and/or a $15,000 fine per offence (stackable to 9 years) only if TLOA's procedural conditions are met (licensed counsel, law-trained judge, recorded proceedings, published law); otherwise the baseline cap is 1 year / $5,000 (25 U.S.C. 1302(a)(7), (b)–(c)). Add those figures or delete "capped… as enhanced." Also: §1152 extends federal enclave criminal law to Indian country with enumerated exceptions (Indian-against-Indian offences, treaty exclusivity, prior tribal punishment); §1153 lists enumerated major crimes committed by Indians.
11.1Public Law 280 "transferred federal criminal jurisdiction (including drug offences) to certain states — in PL-280 states, state drug law is the operative criminal law in Indian Country"Correctedhttps://uscode.house.gov/view.xhtml?path=&req=granuleid%3AUSC-prelim-title18-section1162&f=&fq=&num=0&hl=false&edition=prelimImprecise and internally contradictory with the preceding paragraph (see §2). PL 280 (18 U.S.C. 1162(a)) grants the named states criminal jurisdiction "over offenses committed by or against Indians" in the listed Indian country "to the same extent" as elsewhere in the state; it does not repeal the CSA or oust federal enforcement of laws of general applicability, and federal jurisdiction under §§1152/1153 is largely (not wholly) displaced for covered offences. Corrected wording: "In mandatory PL-280 states, state criminal law is the operative law for most state prosecutions in Indian country, while the federal CSA continues to apply as a law of general applicability."
11.1California v. Cabazon Band, 480 U.S. 202 (1987): civil/regulatory vs criminal/prohibitory distinctionConfirmedhttps://supremecourt.gov/opinions/boundvolumes/480BV.pdfCitation and test confirmed: "if the intent of a state law is generally to prohibit certain conduct, it falls within Pub. L. 280's grant of criminal jurisdiction, but if the state law generally permits the conduct at issue, subject to regulation, it must be classified as civil/regulatory and Pub. L. 280 does not authorize its enforcement on an Indian reservation" (480 U.S. at 209). Note in the rewrite that Cabazon was a gaming case; its application to drug law is by analogy, and a generally prohibitory state drug law would fall on the criminal/prohibitory side of its own test.
11.2Wilkinson Memo: "Policy Statement Regarding Marijuana Issues in Indian Country," Monty Wilkinson, 28 Oct 2014; applied the eight Cole Memo priorities to Indian Country, invited consultation with the local U.S. Attorney, stated DOJ would focus enforcement where priorities were implicated; expressly not a defence, safe harbour, or change in lawConfirmedhttps://www.justice.gov/usao-edwa/pr/federal-marijuana-enforcement-policyDate, author (Monty Wilkinson, Director, Executive Office for U.S. Attorneys), title and content confirmed: the memo states the Cole Memorandum's eight priorities "provide guidance" in Indian Country, that U.S. Attorneys should consult with tribal leaders, and (standard Cole disclaimer) that it creates no rights/defence. Add Wilkinson's role (EOUSA Director) — the report's bare name invites confusion with an AG/DAG memo.
11.2Rescission: Sessions Memorandum, 4 Jan 2018, rescinded prior marijuana guidance by name, including Ogden (19 Oct 2009), Cole I (29 June 2011), Cole II (29 Aug 2013), Cole financial-crimes guidance (14 Feb 2014) and the Wilkinson Policy Statement (28 Oct 2014), directing prosecutors to follow general federal prosecution principles, with no replacement prioritiesConfirmedhttps://www.justice.gov/usao-edwa/pr/federal-marijuana-enforcement-policyRescission and date confirmed on justice.gov (Attorney General Sessions's 4 Jan 2018 memorandum on federal marijuana enforcement policy, directing U.S. Attorneys to the Principles of Federal Prosecution, U.S. Attorneys' Manual ch. 9-27.000). The five-document rescission list, including Wilkinson by name and date, is confirmed in the Sessions memorandum text as issued. The report's conclusion is therefore correct: Wilkinson was rescinded by Sessions in 2018; it is not still operative guidance. (Answer to the brief's open question: rescinded, not surviving.)

Keep flagged. Confirm the letter via congress.gov or the signing senators' official Senate sites, and DOJ guidance via justice.gov/opa and the Justice Manual.
11.37 U.S.C. 1639p: a tribe may submit a tribal hemp production plan to USDA for approval (7 CFR Part 990), covering licensing, THC testing, disposal, enforcement and reporting; a tribe without an approved plan is subject to the USDA plan (direct USDA licensing)Confirmedhttps://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-reviewConfirmed. Precision note: Part 990 testing is for delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis (post-decarboxylation / "total THC" methodology as specified in 7 CFR 990.25) — say "delta-9 THC (total THC, post-decarboxylation)" rather than bare "total THC."
11.3"Per Run 1 (USDA AMS list, current as of Mar 2025): 44 further tribes run their own approved plans and 8 tribes are USDA-managed, alongside 39 states + PR + USVI with own plans and 11 USDA-managed states"Correctedhttps://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-reviewOutdated counts — replace everywhere. USDA AMS list updated 17 Feb 2026 (the current list at audit time): Tribes: 57 with Approved Plans and 8 under USDA Hemp Producer License (Fort Peck Assiniboine & Sioux, Bois Forte, Confederated Salish & Kootenai, Lower Brule Sioux, Mississippi Band of Choctaw, Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate, Walker River Paiute, White Earth Band of Minnesota Chippewa). States: 38 with Approved Plans and 12 USDA-managed (HI, IA, KS, MS, MO, NE, NH, NC, OH, UT, VT, WI — Ohio is the addition that breaks the report's "11"). Territories: 3 approved (Guam, Puerto Rico, U.S. Virgin Islands). Do not carry the Mar 2025 figures forward.
11.3"The §781 redefinition (12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026) applies to tribal hemp identically"Unverifiedhttps://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-reviewCross-reference to Parts 1–8 (§781 / PL 119-37 / PL 119-103 analysis), not re-audited within Parts 9–11. Keep only as a cross-reference with the Part 1–8 audit's verdict; do not restate dates here as independently confirmed.
11.3Tribal cannabis compacts (e.g. Washington State–tribal compacts under Wash. Rev. Code §43.06.490) are state–tribal agreements; they bind the state, not the United States, and confer no federal immunityConfirmedhttps://app.leg.wa.gov/rcw/default.aspx?cite=43.06.490Statute confirmed (current citation style: RCW 43.06.490, "Cannabis agreements — Federally recognized Indian tribes," as amended by 2022 c. 16 and 2025 c. 58; governor may enter agreements addressing law enforcement, regulation, research, taxation — tribal cannabis tax of at least 100% of the state excise tax — immunities/preemption and dispute resolution). The "binds the state, not the United States" proposition is correct as a matter of the statute's own scope: the RCW authorises state agreements and grants no federal-law defence.
11.4Alturas / XL Ranch (Modoc County, CA), July 2015: federal agents raided grows associated with Alturas Indian Rancheria and Pit River Tribe's XL Ranch, seizing ~12,000 plants and >100 lb processed marijuanaConfirmedhttps://www.justice.gov/usao-edca/pr/federal-and-local-law-enforcement-execute-search-warrants-large-scale-commercialConfirmed and dated precisely by DOJ (E.D. Cal.), 8 July 2015: BIA and DEA special agents, assisted by other federal/state agencies and the Modoc County Sheriff, executed search warrants at two large-scale cultivation facilities on tribal lands at the Alturas Indian Rancheria and the XL Ranch; "at both sites, law enforcement seized a total of at least 12,000 marijuana plants and over 100 pounds of processed marijuana." Add what the report omits: "Other than contraband marijuana and items of evidentiary value, no tribal property was seized, and no federal charges are pending" at the time of the release; the investigation concerned the financing and management of the projects.
11.4Alturas (cont.): "the U.S. Attorney stated the operations exceeded local limits and product was destined off tribal lands"Correctedhttps://www.justice.gov/usao-edca/pr/federal-and-local-law-enforcement-execute-search-warrants-large-scale-commercialNot in the DOJ release in that form. The release notes the grows' publicity and the DOJ tribal-marijuana guidance and the financing/management investigation; the diversion/exceeds-limits characterisations come from the unsealed search-warrant affidavits and contemporaneous statements as reported secondarily. Rewrite: "The unsealed warrant affidavits alleged a large commercial operation financed and managed with outside involvement, capable of production far exceeding California medical-marijuana limits" — attributed to the affidavits, not stated as adjudicated fact.
11.4Menominee (Wisconsin), Oct 2015: DEA raided the Menominee Indian Tribe's industrial-hemp grow, seizing/destroying ~30,000 plants the tribe maintained were lawful hemp research plants; tribe sued; court ruled for the government (2016) on the ground the grow was not under a 2014 Farm Bill §7606 research programme — "Verify pin-cite/docket"Correctedhttps://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-reviewPin-cite resolved (court opinion; reporter source, noted as such): ***Menominee Indian Tribe of Wisconsin v. Drug Enforcement Administration*, 190 F. Supp. 3d 843 (E.D. Wis. 2016), Case No. 15-CV-1378-WCG, Decision and Order filed 23 May 2016 (Chief Judge Griesbach), granting the government's motion to dismiss and denying the tribe's summary-judgment motion. Reasoning corrections: (1) raid date 23 Oct 2015; lead description should be federal agents (DEA/DOJ) raiding reservation lands. (2) The holding was narrower and more specific than the report states: the tribe was not a "State" within §7606 (codified at 7 U.S.C. 5940), and its cultivation was not "allowed under the laws of the State" because Wisconsin law did not permit it — partnership with the College of Menominee Nation did not cure either defect. (3) The ~30,000-plant figure** appears in the tribe's pleadings/reporting, not as a judicial finding — mark the count Unverified on primary sources. Wisconsin at the time had no hemp programme (its pilot programme came in 2017) — that limb is confirmed.
11.4Flandreau Santee Sioux (South Dakota), Nov 2015: after legalising marijuana under tribal law for a planned resort, and after consultations in which federal officials signalled a possible raid, the tribe burned its crop (~600 plants) on 7 Nov 2015 and suspended operations; no raid occurred; two non-Indian consultants were later prosecuted by the stateUnverifiedhttps://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-reviewCore narrative (tribal legalisation for a resort, crop destroyed in Nov 2015 for fear of a federal raid, no raid, state prosecution of consultants Eric Hagen and Jonathan Hunt of Monarch America) is consistent across accounts, but no primary federal source (DOJ/BIA/S.D. U.S. Attorney) documenting the episode was located, and the specific 7 Nov 2015 date and ~600-plant count rest on secondary reporting only. Outcomes omitted by the report and likewise secondary-only: Hunt pleaded guilty to a state drug-conspiracy count (2016); Hagen was acquitted by a jury (2017). Verdict Unverified under the primary-sources rule; confirm via South Dakota Unified Judicial System / Moody County court records (Case: State v. Hagen / State v. Hunt) and the tribe's own ordinance/resolution. Note for context (primary): Flandreau Santee Sioux now holds a USDA-Approved tribal hemp plan (AMS list, 17 Feb 2026).
11.5"No IHS clinical guidance authorising cannabis in IHS facilities, and no BIA cannabis-specific enforcement policy distinct from CSA enforcement, was located on ihs.gov/bia.gov — Unavailable"Unverifiedhttps://www.justice.gov/usao-edca/pr/federal-and-local-law-enforcement-execute-search-warrants-large-scale-commercialKeep as a dated negative finding for IHS guidance. For BIA, qualify it: BIA was a lead agency in the July 2015 Alturas/XL Ranch warrants (DOJ release, §11.4), so "no BIA cannabis enforcement role" would be wrong; the accurate claim is only that no free-standing BIA cannabis policy document distinct from CSA enforcement was located on bia.gov.
11.5"No enacted federal statute creating a tribal cannabis safe harbour exists; 119th-Congress proposals touching tribes (STATES 2.0, H.R. 2934; S. 4315; tribal provisions in CAOA/MORE) are proposals only (Part 13)"Unverifiedhttps://www.congress.gov/search?q=%7B%22source%22%3A%22legislation%22%7DBill-status verification for Part 13 items is outside the Parts 9–11 record set and was not completed against congress.gov in this audit. Keep as cross-reference only, with each bill's status to be taken from the Part 13 audit. Do not restate any of them as pending/enacted here.
11.5Saint Regis Mohawk / Akwesasne cross-border: territory straddles the U.S.–Canada border; movement across it implicates 21 U.S.C. 952/960 and customs law; "no official federal source (CBP/DEA/BIA) specifically addressing Saint Regis Mohawk cannabis as of Oct 2026 was located — Unavailable"Unverifiedhttps://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-reviewNegative finding stands for a CBP/DEA/BIA cannabis-specific source. Add the one primary fact the report misses: the Saint Regis Mohawk Tribe holds a USDA-Approved tribal hemp plan (AMS list, 17 Feb 2026) — relevant, lawful-channel context that should accompany any Akwesasne discussion. The 21 U.S.C. 952/960 importation framework is confirmed statutory text; its application to any specific Akwesasne conduct is not documented on a primary source located.

Row count: 72. Verdict tally: Confirmed 34 · Corrected 26 · Removed 0 · Unverified 12. No claim required a standalone "Removed" verdict: every false claim had a correct version in primary sources (the two nearest cases — "50 U.S.C. 3100a" and the Mar 2025 USDA counts — are handled as Corrected with the wrong text ordered deleted in §5).

Internal contradictions — resolved against the primary source
  1. Part 9 header vs Part 9.1 body — "no change" vs "no revised guidance located." The header asserts no change from rescheduling; §9.1 then concedes DOT had issued "no revised MRO guidance… Verify." Both are superseded by primary sources: DOT issued a new marijuana notice (19 Dec 2025), an official Part 40 Q&A resolving the Schedule III MRO question (15 May 2026), and an oral-fluid final rule (19 May 2026). Resolution: outcome unchanged, guidance changed. Rewrite the header as in the table, row 1.
  2. Part 9.1 DOT rationale vs Part 10.3 / Part 1 analysis — "Schedule I" reasoning. §9.1 notes §40.151(e) bars MRO reliance on a recommendation to use a Schedule I drug, which no longer covers state-medical marijuana. The report leaves this as an open "wrinkle." Resolution: DOT closed it on different statutory ground — §§40.137(a)/40.141(b) "legitimate medical explanation" requires a lawful federal prescription; state-licensed marijuana is not FDA-approved and cannot be so prescribed (May 2026 Q&A). The wrinkle is resolved, not open.
  3. Part 9.2 SAMHSA paragraphs — 2017/2019 guidelines presented as current vs "no revision located." The report cites the 2017 urine and 2019 oral-fluid guidelines as operative and then says no revision was located. In fact both were revised in Oct 2023 (88 FR 70768; 88 FR 70814). Resolution: cite the 2023 guidelines; the accurate negative finding is only that no 2024–2026 revision removing marijuana was located.
  4. Part 9.5 Army vs Navy hemp scope. The report presents AR 600-85 (topicals/soaps included) and then says ALNAV 074/20 "is to the same effect," while the most accessible navy.mil text (ALNAV 074/19) expressly exempts shampoos/lotions/soaps. Resolution: 074/19 exempted topicals; 074/20 removed the exemption and includes them. Both statements are true of different documents; the rewrite must date/signer-tag each ALNAV.
  5. Part 10.1 §1182 exception vs §§1227/316.10 exception. §10.1 first attaches the ≤30 g single-offence exception to §1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II), then correctly treats it as a §1182(h) waiver matter and a GMC exception. Resolution: no exception exists in (a)(2)(A)(i)(II); the figure lives only in §1182(h) (waiver), §1227(a)(2)(B)(i) (deportability), and 8 CFR 316.10(b)(2)(iii) (GMC).
  6. Part 10.2 "Schedule I" quotation vs Part 10.3 analysis. The report quotes USCIS's "remains classified as a 'Schedule I' controlled substance" and then argues the premise is undercut for state-medical use — without flagging that the quotation is now partly false as scheduling fact. Resolution: keep the quotation, dated and attributed to USCIS PM F.5 §C.2 (live, unrevised, checked 23 Oct 2026); keep the analysis separate and narrowed per table row 10.3 (licensed activity/registration only, not patient use).
  7. Part 11.1 PL-280 paragraph vs its own preceding paragraph. "CSA applies in Indian Country / sovereignty does not displace the CSA" sits next to "in PL-280 states, state drug law is the operative criminal law." Resolution: both, properly scoped — PL 280 grants state criminal jurisdiction over Indian-country offences by/against Indians in the named states; it does not repeal the CSA or bar federal enforcement under laws of general applicability. Corrected wording in the table.
  8. Part 11.5 BIA "Unavailable" vs Part 11.4 Alturas. §11.5 says no BIA cannabis-specific enforcement policy was located, while §11.4 describes a BIA-participating raid without naming BIA. Resolution: DOJ's release names BIA and DEA as the executing agencies at Alturas/XL Ranch. State that expressly in §11.4; narrow §11.5 to "no free-standing BIA policy document located."

S. 5022 (tax)"Excise tax reported at 5%→12.5% small/mid and 10%→25% large producers [secondary — Verify in text]"Confirmedgovinfo bill text, BILLS-119s5022is (tax chapter)Verify resolved in substance. The introduced text imposes the tax at an "applicable percentage" of removal price of 10% (years 1–2), 15% (year 3), 20% (year 4), 25% (year 5), converting thereafter to an equivalent per-unit amount; the small/mid-sized-producer rate is half the applicable percentage (5% rising to 12.5%). Rewrite without the "[secondary]" tag, citing the text's applicable-percentage schedule.

1.5 Personal contact details scan

The full HTML (Federal_Cannabis___Hemp_Law.html) was scanned for email addresses and phone-number patterns.

  • Emails found: exactly one — Farmbill.Hemp@usda.gov, a USDA programme mailbox for the hemp programme. It is an agency contact, not an individual licence/registration holder's personal email. No removal required.
  • Phone numbers found: none.
  • No personal phone number or email of any individual licence holder appears anywhere in Parts 12–14, the Research Notes or the Sources list (or elsewhere in the file). Nothing is marked Removed on this ground. If licence-holder names are retained anywhere in the report, they should stay in the form name + link to the official source, with no personal contact details added.

---

Internal contradictions — resolved against the primary source

C-1. "Primary sources only" vs the Sources list itself. The report claims a primary-sources-only rule, but its own Sources list admits reliance on Cornell LII, EveryCRSReport mirrors, Mondaq, OpenJurist, a Pressbooks textbook, PR Newswire, the FDA Law Blog, Tax Notes, a FRED-hosted PDF, LegiScan and The Marijuana Herald. Resolution: the claim is false as written. Either (a) replace each non-primary source with the official version listed in Section 4 (possible for almost all of them), or (b) reword the sourcing claim to "primary sources wherever an official version exists; secondary sources are flagged in the text where used." Option (a) is achievable for every item except the Epidiolex descheduling letter and the HHS recommendation letter (Section 3).

C-2. VA guidance is stale against the report's own core finding. VA's public page "VA and Marijuana – What Veterans need to know" was last updated 1 Aug 2023 and still describes marijuana as a Schedule I substance without qualification — inconsistent with the 28 Apr 2026 rule (91 FR 22714) that the rest of the report correctly describes. Resolution: no contradiction in VA policy — Directive 1315's prohibition on recommending/referring/completing forms stands until amended, and VA has issued no post-rule guidance. But Part 12 should say expressly that VA's public-facing scheduling language predates the April 2026 rule and has not been updated, so the page cannot be cited for the current schedule status of the state-medical category.

C-3. §12.2 (HUD position) vs §13.3 (housing parity bills). Read quickly, the pending Marijuana in Federally Assisted Housing Parity Act (H.R. 6807 / S. 3537) could be mistaken for a change to the HUD position described in §12.2. Resolution: the bills are introduced only and are not law; §12.2's description of current HUD law and guidance is unchanged. The report states this correctly; make the cross-reference explicit in both places ("pending only — see §13.3; §12.2 states current law").

C-4. Part 14 table vs the research brief on two dates. Brief: Raich decided 5 June 2005; Ogden Memo 20 Oct 2009. Primary sources: 6 June 2005 (545 U.S. 1) and 19 October 2009 (DOJ text). Resolution: the table is right and the brief was wrong on both. The Research Notes already record this correctly; no table change needed.

C-5. H.R. 7010 vs H.R. 7024. The report calls H.R. 7010 the "(§781 delay companion)" of H.R. 7024. In fact Baird filed two separate House bills with identical operative text one day apart — H.R. 7010 (12 Jan 2026, no short title) and H.R. 7024 (13 Jan 2026, "Hemp Planting Predictability Act") — and the Senate companion to the titled bill is S. 3686. Resolution: describe them as two separate House bills with identical operative text; reserve "companion" for S. 3686.

C-6. Two different "HEMP Acts" in the 119th. S. 2112 is the "Hemp Economic Mobilization Plan Act of 2025" and H.R. 7212 is the "Hemp Enforcement, Modernization, and Protection Act" — both acronym to "HEMP Act." Resolution: always cite with bill numbers and full short titles; never "the HEMP Act" unqualified.

C-7. Two different "HOPE Acts." The cannabis expungement-grants HOPE Act exists only in the 118th (H.R. 2677 / S. 4161); the 119th H.R. 5252 "HOPE Act" is the Having Overdose Protection Equipment Act (opioids). Resolution: the report already handles this correctly — retain as a standing disambiguation note.

C-8. Secondary-source noise the report must not absorb. (i) A secondary tracker misdescribes H.R. 3082 as a GAO-study bill — wrong against the text (it repeals 21 U.S.C. 1703(b)(12)). (ii) Secondary sources date H.R. 6209 to 17 Nov 2025 — congress.gov says 20 Nov 2025. (iii) Secondary sources date S. 4315 to 20 Apr 2026 (a sponsor press release) — govinfo says introduced 16 Apr 2026 (legislative day 14 Apr 2026). Resolution: in each case the primary source controls; the corrected wording is in the Part 13 table.

C-9. Signing date of the April 2026 final order. Secondary sources conflict (22 vs 23 Apr 2026). Resolution: as the report already does, assert only the verified publication/effective date — 28 Apr 2026 (91 FR 22714). No change.

4.2 Other sourcing defects to fix

  • Tax Notes (for IRS IR-2024-177): cite the IRS newsroom original on irs.gov. (Run 1 item, flagged here because the Sources list presents it without distinction.)
  • FRED-hosted PDF (Treasury SB0471): cite the Treasury press release on home.treasury.gov.
  • opss.org / home.army.mil (DoD hemp materials): acceptable as official military pages, but cite the issuing DoD/Army source page directly rather than an aggregator where possible.
  • Reagan Library text of EO 12564: replace with the Federal Register / govinfo text (archives.gov is also official).
  • Cloudfront CRS mirror (d27r7ebsgu0es2.cloudfront.net, RL31100): same fix as S-3 — cite the opinion, or the CRS original on crsreports.congress.gov.
  • Non-citable entries as written: several Sources-list entries give only a domain or a bare label — e.g. "govinfo.gov," "uscode.house.gov," "ams.usda.gov," and "justice.gov (1) / (2) / (3)." These identify no document and cannot be checked by a reader. Every entry must carry the document title plus the exact URL. The exact URLs gathered in this audit for the Parts 12–14 items are in the tables in Section 1 and should be copied into the Sources list verbatim.
  • Wikipedia: no Wikipedia citation appears in the Parts 12–14 sources, and none may be added — it was used in this audit only as a search lead and never as confirmation.

4.3 Report links checked and found correct

The following report citations resolve to the right official documents and need no change: govinfo 91 FR 22714 (FR Doc. 2026-08176); govinfo PL 119-103 (§§101, 106, 2019) and PL 119-74 (§§530–531); uscode.house.gov 7 U.S.C. 1639o amendment note; govinfo Proclamation 10467 PDF; federalregister.gov Proclamation 10688 full text and 89 FR 44597; supremecourt.gov Hemani slip opinion; justice.gov Ogden/Cole/Sessions memoranda; hud.gov MED-MARIJUANA.PDF; fsapartners.ed.gov FAFSA item; congress.gov H.R. 3884 all-info; govinfo STATUTE-50 (Marihuana Tax Act).

Research dossier · Complete — Runs 1 & 2 · Audited edition Research as of 23 October 2026Last audited: 24 October 2026 Primary sources wherever an official version exists

United States Federal Cannabis & Hemp Law

Federal cannabis law is now split. A partial DOJ/DEA final rule effective 28 April 2026 moved only marijuana in FDA-approved drug products and marijuana handled under a state medical marijuana licence from Schedule I to Schedule III. All other marijuana — including every state adult-use programme — remains Schedule I. Hemp law faces a two-step cliff in late 2026 as the rewritten hemp definition in 7 U.S.C. 1639o takes effect in stages. This dossier now covers Parts 0–14 in full. Run 2's largest change is firearms: the Supreme Court decided United States v. Hemani on 18 June 2026, and ATF narrowed the “unlawful user” definition effective 22 January 2026 — see Part 8.

Scheduling statements rest on the Federal Register text itself: the final rule at 91 FR 22714 (FR Doc. 2026-08176, AG Order No. 6754-2026) was opened and read directly for this dossier — accessed 2026-10-23; operative text quoted in Part 1.2.

Enacted law in force or enacted with a future effective date Not law proposed, introduced, or reported only Verify rests on secondary or index-level sources Unavailable not found in an official source
28 Apr 2026
Partial rescheduling final rule takes effect — FDA-approved products and state-licensed medical marijuana move to Schedule III (91 FR 22714).
12 Nov 2026
PL 119-37 §781 hemp redefinition begins to apply — first to products with cannabinoids “not capable of being naturally produced.”
11 Dec 2026
Full §781 definition applies after this date (total-THC test, 0.4 mg/container cap); the medical-marijuana rider and CR funding also run to this date.
Schedule III — since 28 Apr 2026

Two categories only

Marijuana in an FDA-approved drug product, and marijuana subject to a state medical marijuana licence — the two categories placed in Schedule III at 21 CFR 1308.13(g)(2)–(5) ((g)(2) marijuana, (g)(3) marijuana extract, (g)(4) naturally derived delta-9-THCs, (g)(5) [Reserved]). DEA registration for these categories is a separate requirement, under 21 CFR 1301.13(k); §280E no longer applies to these categories.

Schedule I — unchanged

Everything else

All state adult-use marijuana, unlicensed marijuana, and synthetic THC. Full CSA controls and sanctions, and full §280E, still apply. Hemp (7 U.S.C. 1639o) is not a controlled substance at all.

Part 0

Headlines

The most important current federal facts and recent changes. Every source below was accessed 2026-10-23.

  1. Partial rescheduling is final; general rescheduling is not. Enacted law

    DOJ/DEA final rule, 91 FR 22714 (28 April 2026), AG Order No. 6754-2026: marijuana in FDA-approved drug products and marijuana under a state medical marijuana licence → Schedule III, effective 28 April 2026. Everything else remains Schedule I. Source: govinfo.gov — FR Doc. 2026-08176 (opened directly).

  2. Broader rescheduling proceeding stayed.

    The 2024 NPRM (89 FR 44597) hearing commenced 29 June 2026 in Arlington, VA, per DEA's official rescheduling page. A Chief ALJ stay issued 29 September 2026 — on the joint motion of 28 September 2026 by DUID Victim Voices, Kenneth Finn, MD, and the National Drug & Alcohol Screening Association; the Government's opposition was filed 9 October 2026; SAM and the States of Idaho, Indiana and Nebraska responded in support on 13 October 2026; motions to reopen were filed 12 October 2026 with the Government's response due 21 October 2026 — confirmed by the Order of Chief ALJ Derek C. Julius, 14 October 2026 (DEA Docket No. 1362, Hearing Docket No. 26-96): “this matter remains STAYED.” Source: dea.gov — Marijuana Rescheduling Regulatory Actions (opened directly).

  3. Executive Order 14370 directed expedited rescheduling.

    EO 14370 (18 December 2025), “Increasing Medical Marijuana and Cannabidiol Research,” 90 FR 60541, directed the Attorney General to complete the Schedule III rulemaking expeditiously. It changed no schedule by itself. Source (index-level): whitehouse.gov.

  4. Hemp definition rewritten, effective in stages. Enacted law

    PL 119-37, Div. B, §781 (H.R. 5371, enacted 12 Nov 2025) amends 7 U.S.C. 1639o effective 12 Nov 2026; PL 119-103, §2019 (H.R. 6500, enacted 2 Sept 2026) limits §781's application until 11 Dec 2026 to the “not naturally producible” cannabinoid exclusions only. Sources (opened directly): uscode.house.gov — 7 U.S.C. 1639o; govinfo.gov — PL 119-103.

  5. Medical-marijuana rider in force. Enacted law

    PL 119-74, §531 (enacted 23 Jan 2026) bars DOJ from spending funds to prevent listed states/territories from implementing medical-marijuana laws; continued through 11 Dec 2026 by PL 119-103, §101. Source (opened directly): govinfo.gov — PL 119-74.

  6. 280E relief is partial.

    Because §280E (26 U.S.C. 280E) applies only to Schedule I/II trafficking, it no longer applies to the new Schedule III medical/FDA categories; it still applies to adult-use and all other Schedule I activity. Source: 91 FR 22714 (above); Treasury/IRS press release SB0471 (23 April 2026) announced forthcoming §280E guidance, including apportionment for multi-activity businesses and a transition rule keyed to the full taxable year that includes the Final Order's effective date

  7. Supreme Court denies certiorari in Canna Provisions v. Bondi (No. 25-518, 15 December 2025), leaving Gonzales v. Raich in force.

    Certiorari denied 15 December 2025 in Canna Provisions, Inc. v. Bondi, No. 25-518 (Commerce Clause challenge to the CSA as applied to intrastate state-regulated marijuana) — secondary reporting; verify against the Court's order list.

  8. No banking law enacted. Not law

    The SAFER Banking Act (118th Congress, S. 2860) was reported by the Senate Banking Committee and placed on the Senate Legislative Calendar (Calendar No. 215) on 28 September 2023; it received no floor vote and died on the calendar at the end of the 118th Congress; reintroduced in the 119th Congress as the SAFE Banking Act of 2026 (S. 4942 / H.R. 9471, June 2026) — introduced and referred only, not enacted. Source: congress.gov — S. 2860 actions; govinfo bill texts for S. 4942 / H.R. 9471.

  9. USDA delays DEA-lab testing enforcement.

    USDA AMS is “delaying until December 31, 2026, the enforcement of a requirement that all hemp must be tested by a Drug Enforcement Agency (DEA)-registered laboratory.” Source (opened directly): ams.usda.gov — Hemp Production.

  10. FDA's position unchanged.

    FDA has approved one cannabis-derived drug (Epidiolex) and three cannabis-related drugs (Marinol, Syndros, Cesamet); CBD may not lawfully be sold in food or as a dietary supplement; FDA's 26 January 2023 statement that existing food/supplement frameworks are “not appropriate” for CBD, and that a new congressional pathway is needed, remains its stated position. Sources (opened directly): fda.gov — CBD Q&A; fda.gov — 26 Jan 2023 statement.

Part 1

Controlled Substances Law

1.1 Where marijuana sits under the CSA

Statutory definition — 21 U.S.C. 802(16) (opened directly; verbatim):

“(16)(A) Subject to subparagraph (B), the terms 'marihuana' and 'marijuana' mean all parts of the plant Cannabis sativa L., whether growing or not; the seeds thereof; the resin extracted from any part of such plant; and every compound, manufacture, salt, derivative, mixture, or preparation of such plant, its seeds or resin. (B) The terms… do not include— (i) hemp, as defined in section 1639o of title 7; or (ii) the mature stalks of such plant, fiber produced from such stalks, oil or cake made from the seeds of such plant, any other compound… (except the resin extracted therefrom)… or the sterilized seed of such plant which is incapable of germination.”

21 U.S.C. 802(16) — uscode.house.gov · accessed 2026-10-23

Schedules — 21 U.S.C. 812. Section 812(b) states the findings required for each schedule: Schedule I requires (A) high potential for abuse, (B) no currently accepted medical use in treatment in the United States, and (C) lack of accepted safety for use under medical supervision; Schedule III requires (A) potential for abuse less than Schedules I–II, (B) a currently accepted medical use, and (C) abuse may lead to moderate or low physical dependence or high psychological dependence (21 U.S.C. 812(b)(3)(C)). The §812 page itself was not opened directly in this run; criteria summarised from the statute as quoted in the 2024 NPRM and standard codification.

Marijuana's operative placement is implemented in DEA's regulations: 21 CFR 1308.11(d)(23) (marijuana, Schedule I, with marijuana extract at 1308.11(d)(58) and tetrahydrocannabinols at 1308.11(d)(31)), and, since 28 April 2026, 21 CFR 1308.13(g)(2)–(5) ((g)(2) marijuana, (g)(3) marijuana extract, (g)(4) naturally derived delta-9-THCs, (g)(5) [Reserved]) listing marijuana, marijuana extract, and naturally derived delta-9-THCs in Schedule III only “in a U.S. Food and Drug Administration approved product or subject to a state medical marijuana license.” Source: 91 FR 22714 and 21 CFR 1308.13 as amended 28 April 2026 (see §1.2).

Penalties in brief — 21 U.S.C. 841 and 844. Section 841(a) prohibits knowingly/intentionally manufacturing, distributing, dispensing, or possessing with intent, a controlled substance except as authorised. Marijuana quantity tiers under §841(b) Verify (secondary summary): 1,000 kg or 1,000+ plants → 10-year mandatory minimum tier; 100 kg or 100+ plants → 5-year mandatory minimum tier; less than 50 kg → up to 5 years, no mandatory minimum. Section 844 (simple possession, first offence), verbatim in substance: “Any person who violates this subsection may be sentenced to a term of imprisonment of not more than 1 year, and shall be fined a minimum of $1,000, or both,” escalating for prior convictions (15 days–2 years / $2,500 minimum; then 90 days–3 years / $5,000 minimum). Source: uscode.house.gov — 21 U.S.C. 844.

Note on the April 2026 rule and penalties

The April 2026 rule did not amend §§841/844; conduct involving Schedule I marijuana (including all adult-use marijuana) remains subject to them, and Schedule III handling outside the new DEA registration framework remains unlawful.

Federal power. Gonzales v. Raich, 545 U.S. 1 (2005), upheld CSA application to intrastate, state-authorised medical marijuana; the Supreme Court declined to revisit that holding in Canna Provisions (Part 0, item 7) Verify.

1.2 Rescheduling — full history and current status

DateEventSource

Early 2025Hearing stalled amid procedural disputes and an interlocutory appeal. Verify (secondary law-firm reporting only)—

EO 14370 §2(a) (verbatim): “The Attorney General shall take all necessary steps to complete the rulemaking process related to rescheduling marijuana to Schedule III of the CSA in the most expeditious manner in accordance with Federal law, including 21 U.S.C. 811.” Section 2(b) directed work with Congress to update the statutory definition of final hemp-derived cannabinoid products to preserve access to appropriate full-spectrum CBD while restricting risky products, and directed HHS/FDA/CMS/NIH research-methods work.

The April 2026 final rule — what it does

Verified verbatim from the rule, opened directly at govinfo.gov, accessed 2026-10-23. Citation/title: “Schedules of Controlled Substances: Rescheduling of Food and Drug Administration Approved Products Containing Marijuana From Schedule I to Schedule III; Corresponding Change to Permit Requirements,” 21 CFR Parts 1300, 1301, 1308, 1312, FR Doc. 2026-08176, AG Order No. 6754-2026, signed by Acting Attorney General Todd Blanche. “DATES: Effective April 28, 2026.” Some secondary sources misstate the effective date as 22/23 April — the rule was signed 22 April; the Federal Register text controls: effective 28 April 2026.

  • Scope: the rule applies to CSA-defined marijuana, marijuana extracts, and delta-9-THC/other compounds derived from the marijuana plant (other than mature stalks and seeds) that fall outside the hemp definition, only to the extent the material is (a) included in an FDA-approved drug product, or (b) subject to a state-issued licence to manufacture, distribute, and/or dispense marijuana or products containing marijuana for medical purposes (“state medical marijuana license,” now defined at 21 CFR 1300.01).
  • Legal basis: 21 U.S.C. 811(d)(1) — because control is required by U.S. obligations under the Single Convention on Narcotic Drugs (1961), the Attorney General may schedule “without regard to” the findings and procedures of 21 U.S.C. 811(a)/(b) and 812(b); DEA therefore treated APA notice-and-comment as inapplicable to this order. The rule also recites HHS findings consistent with Schedule III (moderate/low physical dependence etc.).
  • What remains Schedule I (verbatim): “any form of marijuana other than in an FDA-approved drug product or marijuana subject to a state medical marijuana license remains a schedule I controlled substance, and those who handle such material remain subject to the regulatory controls, and administrative, civil, and criminal sanctions, applicable to schedule I controlled substances…”
  • Exclusions: the rule does not apply to synthetically derived THC (“synthetic THC remains in schedule I,” e.g. delta-10-THC obtainable only by artificial synthesis); does not affect hemp as defined in 7 U.S.C. 1639o; does not re-schedule products already moved out of Schedule I (e.g. Marinol, Syndros); unlicensed bulk marijuana, crops and extracts not yet in an FDA-approved product remain Schedule I.
  • Registration: the rule adds 21 CFR 1301.13(k), “Medical marijuana registrations.” DEA “shall establish an expedited review process for entities holding state medical marijuana licenses who seek registration as a marijuana manufacturer, distributor, or dispenser”; a state licence “shall constitute conclusive evidence” of state authorisation; if the state licence “is suspended, revoked, or expires, the DEA registration is automatically suspended”; registrations “do not authorize the manufacture, distribution, dispensing, or use of marijuana… for non-medical purposes”; state certifications/documents suffice for dispensing in lieu of CSA prescriptions (new 1301.13(k)(5)); “The Administrator shall make every effort to process all applications submitted within 60 days of the publication of this regulation in the Federal Register within six months,” and applicants filing within that 60-day window “may engage in the manufacture, distribution, and/or dispensing of marijuana… in conformity with a state-issued license during the pendency of the application.”
  • Fees for the rescheduled categories as stated in the rule: manufacturers $3,699/year; distributors $1,850/year; dispensers (incl. pharmacies) $888 per 3-year registration. The rule also adds these products to the import/export permit list (amended 21 CFR 1312.30(b)–(d), as amended at 91 FR 22723, 28 Apr 2026).
  • Tax note in the rule (verbatim): “The Acting Attorney General further notes that, as a consequence of this rule, state licensees will no longer be subject to the deduction disallowance imposed by Section 280E of the Internal Revenue Code, which applies only to businesses engaged in 'trafficking in controlled substances... in a schedule I or II,' 26 U.S.C. 280E. Nothing in this rule constitutes a determination regarding federal tax liability…” (see Part 4).
  • The rule's footnote 11 independently confirms the hemp change: “Effective November 12, 2026, the definition of hemp in 7 U.S.C. 1639o(1) is being amended, with corresponding impact on the definition of marijuana in 21 U.S.C. 802(16)(A)” — i.e., products that lose hemp status become CSA “marijuana.”

Status of the broader (all-marijuana) rescheduling as of 23 Oct 2026

DEA's official page (opened directly) states that on 28 April 2026 the Acting AG issued the notice of hearing on the NPRM, the hearing took place 29 June – 15 July 2026 at the DEA Hearing Facility, Arlington, Virginia (DEA Docket No. 1362, Hearing Docket No. 26-96), and the proceeding is currently stayed — it had been scheduled to commence 29 June 2026, 9:00 a.m. ET, DEA Hearing Facility, 700 Army Navy Drive, Arlington, VA, to conclude not later than 15 July 2026, under 5 U.S.C. 556/557 and 21 CFR 1308.41–1308.45, 1316.41–1316.68, and lists the seven designated participants (the National Drug & Alcohol Screening Association; the Tennessee Bureau of Investigation; Smart Approaches to Marijuana; the States of Nebraska, Idaho, Indiana and Louisiana; DUID Victim Voices; Kenneth Finn, MD; Phillip A. Drum, PharmD). A DOJ document (opened directly) records that the earlier hearing notice “was withdrawn… The hearing on the proposed rule was cancelled, and all related proceedings were terminated.” Sources: dea.gov; justice.gov document.

Verify Secondary trade-press reporting only; no DEA/DOJ document opened: the hearing ran 11 days and concluded 15 July 2026; post-hearing briefs followed; on 29 September 2026 Chief ALJ Derek C. Julius stayed the proceeding (DEA Docket 1362) pending a decision on a motion to admit GAO report GAO-26-108623 (released 23 Sept 2026, “Drug Scheduling: While DEA Decisions Have Aligned with Recent HHS Recommendations, Both Need Comprehensive Policies” — covering 208 DEA scheduling actions from 2020–2025, of which 95 required an HHS evaluation; of the 84 of those with published final decisions as of 31 December 2025, DEA aligned with HHS in all 84; no recommendation on marijuana) and to allow supplemental briefing. A 23 October 2026 trade-press item reported the GAO report was admitted, testimony not reopened, supplemental briefs due 29 October and replies 5 November 2026. Do not treat as settled. The stay does not affect the April 2026 final rule.

Verify Secondary reporting only; no free public docket copy obtained: petitions for review of the April 2026 rule are pending in the D.C. Circuit, lead case SAM, Inc. v. Department of Justice, No. 26-1106 (consolidated Nos. 26-1130, 26-1136, including a 22 May 2026 petition by Nebraska, Indiana and Louisiana); the court reportedly denied a stay on 9 September 2026, so AG Order No. 6754-2026 remains in force while review proceeds.

Exactly what Schedule III does and does not do

As verified against the April 2026 rule, the NPRM, and the CSA:

QuestionAnswer
Does it legalise state adult-use marijuana?No. Adult-use marijuana is outside both categories in the April 2026 rule and remains Schedule I. Even under the pending NPRM, DOJ stated (verbatim): “If marijuana is transferred into schedule III, the manufacture, distribution, dispensing, and possession of marijuana would remain subject to the applicable criminal prohibitions of the CSA.”
Does it authorise interstate commerce?No. Nothing in the rule or the NPRM authorises interstate transfer outside the CSA registration/permit system (imports/exports now require permits under amended 21 CFR 1312.30).
Is DEA registration required?Yes for handlers of the Schedule III categories: 21 U.S.C. 822/823 registration; new expedited medical-marijuana registrations under 21 CFR 1301.13(k); dispensers/practitioners under 21 U.S.C. 823(g); prescriptions/certifications under 21 U.S.C. 829 as modified by 1301.13(k)(5). DEA operates a “Medical Marijuana Dispensary Registration Portal” (linked from the DEA rescheduling page).
Does 280E relief cover all marijuana businesses?No — only the Schedule III categories. The rule states state (medical) licensees “will no longer be subject to” §280E. Adult-use and other Schedule I activity remains fully subject to §280E (Part 4). Any claim that rescheduling ends 280E “for all marijuana businesses” is true only if the broader NPRM is finalised, which it has not been.
Is FDA approval still needed for drugs?Yes. The NPRM states (verbatim): “Any drugs containing a substance within the CSA's definition of 'marijuana' would also remain subject to the applicable prohibitions in the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act.” State-licensed medical products are not FDA-approved drugs (Part 3).
Does it change hemp?No. Hemp (7 U.S.C. 1639o) is expressly unaffected by the April 2026 rule; hemp changes come from PL 119-37 §781 (Part 2.2).

1.3 Federal enforcement policy — DOJ memos and the appropriations rider

Cole Memo (29 Aug 2013), Deputy AG James M. Cole: set eight federal enforcement priorities and deprioritised state-compliant marijuana conduct where states maintained strong regulatory systems. Rescinded 4 January 2018 by AG Jeff Sessions' memorandum directing U.S. Attorneys to “follow the well-established principles that govern all federal prosecutions.” Verify justice.gov originals were not opened directly in this run; content from secondary sources. No later DOJ memorandum reinstating the Cole framework was located. FinCEN's 2014 banking guidance (Part 6) still keys SAR categories to the Cole priorities — an unresolved tension FinCEN has acknowledged without rescinding the guidance.

Appropriations rider — current enacted text (verified verbatim, opened directly). PL 119-74, the “Commerce, Justice, Science; Energy and Water Development; and Interior and Environment Appropriations Act, 2026” (H.R. 6938), enacted 23 January 2026, 140 Stat. 5, Division A, §531:

“Sec. 531. None of the funds made available under this Act to the Department of Justice may be used, with respect to any of the States of Alabama, Alaska, Arizona, Arkansas, California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Hawaii, Illinois, Indiana, Iowa, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Massachusetts, Michigan, Minnesota, Mississippi, Missouri, Montana, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Texas, Utah, Vermont, Virginia, Washington, West Virginia, Wisconsin, and Wyoming, or with respect to the District of Columbia, the Commonwealth of the Northern Mariana Islands, the United States Virgin Islands, Guam, or Puerto Rico, to prevent any of them from implementing their own laws that authorize the use, distribution, possession, or cultivation of medical marijuana.”

PL 119-74, §531 — govinfo.gov · accessed 2026-10-23
  • Notable details: Nebraska is not in the enacted list; the enacted §531 has no subsection (b) (a House-version subsection concerning 21 U.S.C. 860 did not survive). An advocacy claim (Americans for Safe Access, 5 Jan 2026) that the rider had been omitted from the conferenced bill is contradicted by the enacted text and is superseded. The preceding §530 separately bars DOJ/DEA funds being used in contravention of §7606 of the Agricultural Act of 2014 (industrial hemp research).
  • Is it in force on 23 Oct 2026? FY2026 ended 30 Sept 2026, but the FY2027 continuing resolution, PL 119-103, §101, appropriates funds “at a rate for operations as provided in the applicable appropriations Acts for fiscal year 2026 and under the authority and conditions provided in such Acts,” expressly listing “(2) The Commerce, Justice, Science, and Related Agencies Appropriations Act, 2026 (division A of Public Law 119-74), except sections 521(c)(2) and 544” — §531 is not excepted — and §106 makes CR funding available until the earliest of FY2027 enactment etc. or 11 December 2026. On the enacted texts, the rider's conditions therefore continue through 11 Dec 2026. Source: govinfo.gov — PL 119-103.
  • History and case law: substantively identical riders (originally “Rohrabacher–Farr”) have been renewed annually since FY2015. In United States v. McIntosh, 833 F.3d 1163 (9th Cir. 2016), the Ninth Circuit held the rider bars DOJ from spending funds to prevent states implementing medical-marijuana laws, and that prosecuting individuals who are not in strict compliance with state law does not violate it (see CRS Legal Sidebar LSB10694, index-level). The rider limits DOJ spending only; it does not amend the CSA or legalise any conduct.
Part 2

Hemp Law

2.1 The 2018 Farm Bill framework and USDA's rule (7 CFR Part 990)

Current definition — 7 U.S.C. 1639o(1) (§297A of the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946, added by the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018, PL 115-334, Title X, §10113, 20 Dec 2018, 132 Stat. 4908; opened directly; verbatim):

“The term 'hemp' means the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis.”

7 U.S.C. 1639o(1) — uscode.house.gov · accessed 2026-10-23

Because 21 U.S.C. 802(16)(B)(i) excludes “hemp, as defined in section 1639o of title 7” from “marijuana,” hemp meeting this definition is not a controlled substance.

Programme structure — 7 U.S.C. 1639p–1639s. States and tribes may submit plans to USDA for primary regulatory authority; where a state/tribe has no approved plan (or elects not to have one), producers are licensed directly by USDA. Per §1639p as read on the opened page, state/tribal plans must include: land records (retained ≥3 years); testing “using post-decarboxylation or other similarly reliable methods” for delta-9 THC; disposal procedures; enforcement procedures; annual random-sample inspections; submission of information to USDA within 30 days; and certification of adequate resources/personnel. The Secretary must approve or disapprove a plan within 60 days. The statute does not preempt more stringent state/tribal law. A negligent-violation/corrective-action framework follows in §1639p(e).

USDA final rule — 7 CFR Part 990 (86 FR 5596, effective 22 March 2021). The full part was not re-read section-by-section in this run; the following are from eCFR text retrieved in search results Verify (index-level — verify on ecfr.gov):

  • Acceptable hemp THC level / total THC: compliance is measured on a total THC basis after post-decarboxylation testing (e.g. HPLC), applying measurement uncertainty; a lot at or below the acceptable level may enter commerce (see §990.26).
  • Sampling/harvest window (§990.26): harvest must occur no later than 30 days after sample collection; if harvest is not completed within 30 days, a second pre-harvest sample is required. (The “15 days” figure reflects the 2019 interim final rule; the final rule uses 30 days.)
  • Negligence threshold (§990.29) — correction: negligent violations include producing cannabis exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level, but “Hemp producers do not commit a negligent violation under this paragraph (a) if they make reasonable efforts to grow hemp and the cannabis does not have a total THC concentration of more than 1.0 percent on a dry weight basis.” The operative negligence ceiling in the final rule is 1.0%, not 0.5% (0.5% was the interim final rule figure). Negligent violations require corrective action plans (≥2 years) and do not result in criminal enforcement referral solely for negligence; licence suspension — on credible evidence of a violation or of failure to comply with a written AMS order, with the suspended producer barred from handling or removing hemp from the licensed location — is addressed at 7 CFR 990.30; revocation, including the one-year revocation that follows certain audit failures, is addressed in the compliance and audit provisions of Part 990 (§§990.28–990.31), not in §990.30 alone.
  • Eligibility: a 10-year bar for persons with controlled-substance felony convictions applies, with an exception for a person who was lawfully growing hemp under section 7606 of the Agricultural Act of 2014 (7 U.S.C. 5940) before December 20, 2018, and whose conviction also occurred before that date (7 CFR 990.20(b)); the parallel state/tribal-plan provision at 7 CFR 990.6(e) separately exempts persons lawfully growing hemp under a section 7606 pilot programme before October 31, 2019 — the two dates should not be conflated.
  • Licensing models: (i) USDA-approved state plan; (ii) USDA-approved tribal plan; (iii) direct USDA licence via the Hemp eManagement Platform (HeMP), applications “accepted on a rolling basis throughout the year” (AMS page, opened directly). All licensed producers must establish an FSA farm profile and report hemp plantings (annual deadline 15 July; within 30 days of planting per AMS FAQ, index-level).
  • Fees: 7 CFR Part 990 prescribes no fee for a USDA producer licence.
  • DEA-lab testing delay: USDA AMS is “delaying until December 31, 2026, the enforcement of a requirement that all hemp must be tested by a Drug Enforcement Agency (DEA)-registered laboratory” (AMS page, opened directly): ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp.

2.2 The new hemp definition — PL 119-37, §781, as phased in by PL 119-103, §2019

Enacting law (verified from the U.S. Code amendment notes on the opened §1639o page and the enacted texts): the “Continuing Appropriations, Agriculture, Legislative Branch, Military Construction and Veterans Affairs, and Extensions Act, 2026” (H.R. 5371), Public Law 119-37, Division B, Title VII, §781, enacted 12 November 2025, 139 Stat. 558. The amendment is effective “365 days after the enactment” — i.e. 12 November 2026 (U.S. Code note, verbatim on the opened page).

What §781 changes (full amended text read verbatim on the opened 7 U.S.C. 1639o page):

  1. Total-THC standard. New §1639o(1)(A): hemp must have “a total tetrahydrocannabinols concentration (including tetrahydrocannabinolic acid) of not more than 0.3 percent on a dry weight basis” — replacing the delta-9-only test.
  2. Industrial hemp included. §1639o(1)(B): “Such term includes industrial hemp.” New §1639o(2) defines “industrial hemp” as hemp grown for: stalk/fibre or other non-cannabinoid stalk derivatives; whole grain, oil, cake, nut, hull or other non-cannabinoid seed compounds; microgreens/edible leaf from immature plants grown from compliant seed; plants not entering commerce, for research at institutions of higher education or independent research institutes; and viable seed produced solely for those materials.
  3. Exclusions (§1639o(1)(C)) — the following are not hemp:
    • (i) “any viable seeds from a Cannabis sativa L. plant that exceeds a total tetrahydrocannabinols concentration (including tetrahydrocannabinolic acid) of 0.3 percent in the plant on a dry weight basis”;
    • (ii) any intermediate hemp-derived cannabinoid products containing: (I) “cannabinoids that are not capable of being naturally produced by a Cannabis sativa L. plant”; (II) cannabinoids that “(aa) are capable of being naturally produced… and (bb) were synthesized or manufactured outside the plant”; or (III) “more than 0.3 percent combined total of— (aa) total tetrahydrocannabinols (including tetrahydrocannabinolic acid); and (bb) any other cannabinoids that have similar effects (or are marketed to have similar effects) on humans or animals as a tetrahydrocannabinol (as determined by the Secretary of Health and Human Services)”;
    • (iii) intermediate products “marketed or sold as a final product or directly to an end consumer for personal or household use”;
    • (iv) any final hemp-derived cannabinoid products containing (I)/(II) material as above, or (III) “greater than 0.4 milligrams combined total per container of— (aa) total tetrahydrocannabinols (including tetrahydrocannabinolic acid); and (bb)” similar-effects cannabinoids as determined by the HHS Secretary.
  4. New definitions (§1639o(3)). “Hemp-derived cannabinoid product” = any intermediate or final product derived from hemp (other than industrial hemp), containing cannabinoids in any form, intended for human or animal use (inhalation, ingestion, topical application, etc.); “intermediate” = not yet in final form, or a powder/liquid/tablet/oil etc. intended to be mixed or formulated into another substance before administration/consumption; “container” = “the innermost wrapping, packaging, or vessel in direct contact with a final hemp-derived cannabinoid product in which the final hemp-derived cannabinoid product is enclosed for retail sale to consumers, such as a jar, bottle, bag, box, packet, can, carton, or cartridge” (excluding bulk shipping/outer wrappings). The definition excludes a drug that is the subject of an approved application under 21 U.S.C. 355(c) or (j).
  5. FDA lists (§781(3) statutory note, verbatim in substance): “Within 90 days of the enactment of this act [by 10 Feb 2026], the Food and Drug Administration, in consultation with other relevant Federal agencies, shall publish— (A) a list of all cannabinoids known to FDA to be capable of being naturally produced by a Cannabis sativa L. plant…; (B) a list of all tetrahydrocannabinol class cannabinoids known to the agency to be naturally occurring in the plant; (C) a list of all other known cannabinoids with similar effects…; and (D) additional information and specificity about the term 'container'…” Per CRS's 28 Sept 2026 update, these lists had not been published as of that date Verify (as of 23 Oct 2026 — secondary source).

The 2026 phasing amendment — PL 119-103, §2019 (verified verbatim, opened directly). The “Continuing Appropriations and Extensions Act, 2027” (H.R. 6500), Public Law 119-103, enacted 2 September 2026, 140 Stat. 987, §2019 (“SECTION 781 EXTENSION”):

“Until December 11, 2026, the amendments made by section 781 of division B of Public Law 119-37 (7 U.S.C. 1639o note) shall only apply with respect to products described in paragraphs (1)(C)(ii)(I) and (1)(C)(iv)(I) of section 297A of the Agricultural Marketing Act of 1946 (7 U.S.C. 1639o) (as amended by such section 781).”

PL 119-103, §2019 — govinfo.gov · accessed 2026-10-23

Effective-date table (enacted law only)

PeriodRule in force
Now → 11 Nov 2026Current definition: delta-9 THC ≤0.3% dry weight (7 U.S.C. 1639o(1), pre-amendment). Intoxicating hemp products meeting it are “hemp” federally (§2.3).
12 Nov 2026 → 11 Dec 2026§781 applies only to the (1)(C)(ii)(I) and (1)(C)(iv)(I) exclusions — intermediate and final products containing cannabinoids “not capable of being naturally produced by a Cannabis sativa L. plant” cease to be hemp (and so become CSA marijuana unless otherwise excluded).
After 11 Dec 2026Remainder of §781 applies: total-THC (incl. THCA) ≤0.3% standard; seed exclusion; “synthesized or manufactured outside the plant” exclusion; 0.3% combined cap for intermediates; 0.4 mg combined total per container cap for final products; industrial-hemp carve-outs.

Verify Caveat: §2019's sentence does not itself state whether “until December 11, 2026” is inclusive of that date, and it amends no effective-date clause; the table follows the enacted text and the common (CRS/secondary) reading. The CR's funding provisions themselves run to 11 Dec 2026 (PL 119-103, §106(3)).

Proposals that are NOT law Not law

Bills reported by CRS to push the §781 change back (e.g. two-year delay bills cited as H.R. 7024, H.R. 7010, S. 3686) or to repeal it (H.R. 6209) had not passed as of CRS's Sept 2026 update Verify (on congress.gov). EO 14370 §2(b) also directed executive-branch work with Congress on preserving access to appropriate full-spectrum CBD products — a direction to engage, not a change in law. Any secondary summary stating a “0.03%” limit is a typographical error; the statute says 0.3 percent.

Boundary caveat (on the table above): PL 119-103 §2019 does not state whether “until December 11, 2026” includes 11 December itself, and it amends no effective-date clause, so the exact boundary day (11 vs 12 December 2026) is not resolved by the enacted text.

2.3 Delta-8 and other intoxicating hemp products under current law (before the §781 dates)

  • Delta-9-only test governs until 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026. Products derived from hemp meeting the current definition — including hemp-derived delta-8 THC products at ≤0.3% delta-9 THC — fall outside the CSA definition of “marijuana” on the statutory text (and the Ninth Circuit has so held), but DEA maintains that synthetically derived tetrahydrocannabinols remain Schedule I, so the status of converted or synthesised delta-8 is contested under the CSA/Farm Act text.
  • AK Futures LLC v. Boyd St. Distro, LLC, 35 F.4th 682 (9th Cir. 19 May 2022): the Ninth Circuit held hemp-derived delta-8 products within the 0.3% delta-9 limit are lawful “hemp” for Lanham Act trademark purposes: “If Congress inadvertently created a loophole legalizing vaping products containing delta-8 THC, then it is for Congress to fix its mistake” (at 693). (Secondary sources; note the holding's trademark context.) Section 781 is Congress's prospective fix; it does not apply retroactively.
  • DEA 2020 interim final rule, “Implementation of the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018,” 85 FR 51639 (21 Aug 2020): DEA's position is that synthetically derived tetrahydrocannabinols remain Schedule I controlled substances even after the Farm Bill (the rule conformed 21 CFR to the Farm Act; see amendment history at 85 FR 51639). Verify (full IFR text not opened in this run).
  • Correction — the DEA February 2023 letter: the 13 Feb 2023 letter from DEA's Terrence Boos concerned delta-8-THCO and delta-9-THCO (THC acetate esters), not delta-8 THC itself: “Delta-9-THCO and delta-8-THCO do not occur naturally in the cannabis plant and can only be obtained synthetically, and therefore do not fall under the definition of hemp,” and are Schedule I tetrahydrocannabinols under 21 CFR 1308.11(d)(31). Verify (secondary reporting of the letter).
  • THC drinks / edibles: federally, a hemp-derived beverage within the current delta-9 limit is “hemp” under the CSA, but FDA maintains that adding THC or CBD to food in interstate commerce is prohibited under FD&C Act §301(ll) (Part 3), and FDA/FTC have taken enforcement action against delta-8 copycat food products (joint releases 5 July 2023 and 16 July 2024, listed on FDA's cannabis page). State law varies and is outside this run's scope.
Part 3

FDA Rules

Source for this Part (opened directly, accessed 2026-10-23): FDA, “FDA Regulation of Cannabis and Cannabis-Derived Products, Including Cannabidiol (CBD)” (Q&A): fda.gov (news table last updated 16 July 2024 on the page as read).

Approved drugs

FDA (verbatim in substance): “To date, the agency has not approved a marketing application for cannabis for the treatment of any disease or condition. FDA has, however, approved one cannabis-derived and three cannabis-related drug products… only available with a prescription”:

DrugWhat it isNotes
EpidiolexPurified cannabidiol — cannabidiol oral solution, NDA 210365 (GW Pharmaceuticals/Greenwich Biosciences).Originally approved 25 June 2018 for seizures associated with Lennox-Gastaut syndrome and Dravet syndrome in patients 2 years of age and older; a 2020 supplement added tuberous sclerosis complex and lowered the age floor, so the current indications cover all three syndromes in patients 1 year of age and older. DEA placed FDA-approved CBD drug products with ≤0.1% THC in Schedule V (83 FR 48950, 28 Sept 2018) and Epidiolex was later removed from the schedules entirely in 2020: DEA notified the sponsor by letter on 6 April 2020, and DEA's interim final rule implementing the 2018 Farm Bill, 85 FR 51639 (21 Aug 2020), conformed the regulations by removing FDA-approved CBD drug products from Schedule V.
MarinolDronabinol capsules (NDA 018651) — synthetic delta-9-THC. Indications: (1) anorexia associated with weight loss in patients with AIDS; (2) nausea and vomiting associated with cancer chemotherapy in patients who have failed to respond adequately to conventional antiemetic treatments. Schedule III.Originally approved 31 May 1985 for chemotherapy-induced nausea and vomiting (sponsor: Unimed); the AIDS-anorexia indication was added 22 December 1992.
SyndrosDronabinol oral solution (NDA 205525) — synthetic delta-9-THC in liquid form; the same two adult indications as Marinol. Schedule II (21 CFR 1308.12(f)(2)).Approved 1 July 2016 (Insys Therapeutics).
CesametNabilone capsules (NDA 018677) — a synthetic cannabinoid chemically similar to THC, not derived from the plant. Schedule II (21 CFR 1308.12(f)(1)).Approved 26 December 1985 for one indication only: nausea and vomiting associated with cancer chemotherapy in patients who have failed to respond adequately to conventional antiemetic treatments. It has no AIDS-anorexia indication.

Current schedules: Epidiolex — not a controlled substance (2020). Marinol (dronabinol capsules) — Schedule III. Syndros (dronabinol oral solution) — Schedule II, 21 CFR 1308.12(f)(2). Cesamet (nabilone) — Schedule II, 21 CFR 1308.12(f)(1). The April 2026 rule did not change any of these.

FDA states: “Aside from Epidiolex, there are no other FDA-approved drug products that contain CBD.”

Food — FD&C §301(ll) (21 U.S.C. 331(ll))

No. It is prohibited to introduce into interstate commerce any food (including animal food/feed) to which THC or CBD has been added, because each is an active ingredient in an approved drug (or was under substantial clinical investigation); no §301(ll)(2) regulation has been issued for any substance. Statutory exceptions (§301(ll)(1)–(4)): prior marketing in food; an FDA regulation under §301(ll)(2); safety-enhancing uses conforming to a §348 regulation, GRAS listing/affirmation or effective GRAS/food-contact notification; and new animal drugs not unsafe under §360b. FDA has concluded none applies to THC or CBD. Separately, three hemp-seed ingredients — hulled hemp seed (GRN 765), hemp seed protein powder (GRN 771) and hemp seed oil (GRN 778) — received “no questions” GRAS responses in December 2018 and may be lawfully marketed in human food as described in the notices; the seeds contain only trace THC/CBD picked up in harvesting/processing. “Aside from the three hemp seed ingredients…, no other cannabis or cannabis-derived ingredients have been the subject of a food additive petition, an evaluated GRAS notification, or have otherwise been approved for use in food by FDA.”

Dietary supplements — FD&C §201(ff)(3)(B) (21 U.S.C. 321(ff)(3)(B))

No. THC and CBD products are excluded from the dietary supplement definition because CBD is the active ingredient in Epidiolex and THC (dronabinol) in Marinol/Syndros; FDA has concluded the “marketed as” exception does not apply, and no regulation under the exclusion has been issued “for any substance.” Ingredients from parts of the plant without THC/CBD might fall outside the exclusion but remain subject to all supplement law (including new-dietary-ingredient notification).

Cosmetics

Cosmetic products/ingredients need no FDA premarket approval (except most colour additives); no cannabis-derived ingredient is currently prohibited or restricted by regulation, but a cosmetic may not be adulterated or misbranded, and drug claims make a product an unapproved drug.

Animal / pet products

The §301(ll) prohibition covers animal food/feed; As of the Q&A, FDA had received no GRAS notices for hemp-derived ingredients in animal food, and there are no approved food additive petitions and no AAFCO Official Publication ingredient definitions for any hemp-derived substance in animal food (Q12, Q25). FDA has not approved cannabis for any use in animals (Q24).

The January 2023 statement

FDA, “FDA Concludes that Existing Regulatory Frameworks for Foods and Dietary Supplements are Not Appropriate for Cannabidiol, Will Work with Congress on a New Way Forward” (26 Jan 2023, statement of Principal Deputy Commissioner Janet Woodcock, M.D.; opened directly): FDA “concluded that a new regulatory pathway for CBD is needed”; denied three citizen petitions asking it to rulemake CBD into supplements; stated “we have not found adequate evidence to determine how much CBD can be consumed, and for how long, before causing harm. Therefore, we do not intend to pursue rulemaking allowing the use of CBD in dietary supplements or conventional foods”; reached the same conclusion for animal food; and said it would continue enforcement, prioritising risky products, while working with Congress. Source: fda.gov.

Update (1 April 2026): FDA announced a narrow enforcement-discretion posture for orally administered, hemp-derived CBD products provided to Medicare (Title XVIII) beneficiaries through the CMS Substance Access model under a treating physician’s direction, where the product is made and labelled consistently with the dietary supplement framework, is not contaminated, and is not packaged or marketed attractively to children: FDA does not intend to enforce FD&C §§502(f)(1) or 505 against such a product solely because it contains CBD. This does not change the January 2023 conclusion for food and supplements generally. Source: FDA Commissioner letter, 1 Apr 2026, fda.gov

Enforcement and pending matters

  • Warning letters: FDA issues warning letters to firms marketing unapproved CBD/cannabis products with disease claims and to sellers of delta-8 copycat foods (with FTC). FDA's page links a “Warning Letters for Cannabidiol-Related Products” resource; FDA does not publish warning-letter counts by year on the pages opened — Unavailable.
  • A March 2026 report that FDA submitted a “CBD Products Compliance and Enforcement Policy” to OIRA is secondary only — Verify.
  • Pending legislation (status only — NOT law): the CBD Product Safety and Standardization Act of 2023, H.R.1628 (118th Congress), which would have required FDA to regulate hemp-derived CBD in food — referred to the Subcommittee on Health on 24 March 2023 and died at the end of the 118th Congress; no 119th-Congress reintroduction was located on congress.gov. Not law. — .
  • The 2018 Farm Bill “explicitly preserved FDA's authority” under the FD&C Act and §351 of the Public Health Service Act regardless of a substance's hemp status (FDA Q&A).
Part 4

Federal Taxes

26 U.S.C. 280E — verbatim

“No deduction or credit shall be allowed for any amount paid or incurred during the taxable year in carrying on any trade or business if such trade or business (or the activities which comprise such trade or business) consists of trafficking in controlled substances (within the meaning of schedule I and II of the Controlled Substances Act) which is prohibited by Federal law or the law of any State in which such trade or business is conducted.”

26 U.S.C. 280E (added by PL 97-248, §351(a), 3 Sept 1982, 96 Stat. 640) — uscode.house.gov · accessed 2026-10-23

How it applies: §280E bars deductions and credits (rent, wages, marketing, etc.) for a business trafficking in Schedule I/II substances, but it does not bar reducing gross receipts by properly calculated cost of goods sold (COGS) — gross income is computed first. Under Californians Helping to Alleviate Medical Problems, Inc. (CHAMP) v. Commissioner, 128 T.C. 173 (2007), expenses of a genuinely separate, non-trafficking trade or business (in CHAMP, caregiving services) are not barred by §280E; later cases (Olive; Alterman & Gibson; Harborside) deny separate-business treatment where the activities are closely and inseparably related or the second activity is merely ancillary. IRS Chief Counsel Advice on COGS for marijuana businesses: CCA 201504011.

IRS position before the final rule — IR-2024-177 (28 June 2024)

“IRS: Marijuana remains a Schedule I controlled substance; Internal Revenue Code Section 280E still applies” (IRS News Release IR-2024-177, https://www.irs.gov/newsroom/irs-marijuana-remains-a-schedule-i-controlled-substance-internal-revenue-code-section-280e-still-applies — opened directly): “Until a final federal rule is published… marijuana remains a Schedule I controlled substance”; taxpayers who filed amended returns claiming §280E refunds “are not entitled to a refund or payment”; §280E “does not, however, prohibit a participant in the marijuana industry from reducing its gross receipts by its properly calculated cost of goods sold to determine its gross income.”

Effect of the April 2026 final rule

Because §280E by its terms reaches only Schedule I/II trafficking, the DOJ rule states state medical licensees “will no longer be subject to the deduction disallowance imposed by Section 280E” (91 FR 22714, quoted in Part 1.2). Treasury/IRS announcement, 23 April 2026 (press release SB0471, https://home.treasury.gov/news/press-releases/sb0471 ): Treasury and the IRS “plan to issue guidance” that is expected to (a) clarify that for multi-activity businesses “section 280E applies only to those activities related to trafficking in Schedule I or II controlled substances (e.g., by apportioning expenses),” and (b) include “a transition rule providing that, for purposes of section 280E, rescheduling generally will be considered to first apply for a business's full taxable year that includes the effective date of the Final Order, for the business's activities that do not involve Schedule I or II controlled substances as a result of the Final Order” — for calendar-year taxpayers, the announced transition rule would treat relief as effective for all of 2026. The release does not address pre-2026 years; the April 2026 rule separately “encourages” Treasury to consider retrospective relief, which Treasury has not adopted. Both the transition rule and the apportionment method remain announcements of forthcoming guidance, not operative guidance. As of October 2026, secondary tax reporting indicates no implementing Notice/Revenue Procedure/regulation had yet issued, and the 2026–2027 Priority Guidance Plan (29 Sept 2026) lists “Guidance under §280E” as an open project Verify (secondary).

Consistent statement across Parts 1, 4 and 6

280E relief today covers only state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved-product activities; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E.

Other federal tax rules

  • All marijuana/hemp income is taxable, including cash income (IRS cannabis industry page: irs.gov — Cannabis Industry, index-level).
  • Form 8300: “Any person in a trade or business who receives more than $10,000 in cash in a single transaction or in related transactions must file Form 8300… within 15 days after receiving payment”; the IRS offers cash tax-payment options for unbanked taxpayers.
  • Hemp businesses are not §280E businesses (hemp is not a controlled substance) and are taxed under normal business rules, subject to the same Form 8300 and employment-tax obligations.
  • As opened in October 2026, the IRS cannabis industry page still states the §280E rule with no reference to the April 2026 rescheduling or the announced guidance. — .
Part 5

Federal Licences & Data Sources

Licence and registration types only — not individual licence holders — with requirements, fees, application routes, and every official source of holder data located.
