AMS plan/contact list (§5.2); the separate AMS plan-status page is the “List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans,” https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/state-and-tribal-plan-review (updated 17 February 2026). URL.
DEA registration — state medical marijuana manufacturer / distributor / dispenser (NEW, 2026) — 21 CFR 1301.13(k) Manufacture/distribute/dispense Schedule III marijuana for medical purposes only, within the scope of the holder's state licence. Hold a valid state medical marijuana licence (conclusive evidence of state authorisation); registration auto-suspends if the state licence lapses; minimal federal records (state records accepted “to the maximum extent permissible”); Single Convention Art. 23 nominal-price crop purchase mechanism for manufacturers. §1301.13(k) prescribes no fee. General DEA fees (21 CFR 1301.13(e)(1)), recited as “current fees” in the April 2026 rule: manufacturer $3,699/year; distributor $1,850/year; dispenser $888 per 3 years. The fee actually charged by DEA's Medical Marijuana Dispensary portal (Form 224M) is reported as $794 — Verify on the portal before relying on either figure for (k) dispensers. DEA forms + proof of state licence; expedited review; applications filed within 60 days of 28 Apr 2026 could operate under state licence while pending; DEA to “make every effort” to process within 6 months; DEA Form 224M (dispensaries) via the Medical Marijuana Dispensary Registration Portal, https://mmapplication.diversion.dea.gov/ ; Form 225 (manufacturers/distributors); in each case with proof of the state medical marijuana licence. DEA Diversion Control Division registration system; no public holder list/count located. Unavailable
DEA registration — FDA-approved marijuana drug products (manufacturer/distributor/dispenser; importer/exporter) — 21 CFR Parts 1301, 1312 Handle Schedule III FDA-approved marijuana products; import/export only with permits (amended 21 CFR 1312.30). Standard CSA registration (21 U.S.C. 822, 823, 957, 958); prescription-based dispensing (21 U.S.C. 829). Manufacturer $3,699/year; distributor $1,850/year; dispenser $888 per 3 years (21 CFR 1301.13(e)(1)). Importer/exporter registrations carry their own fee line in §1301.13(e)(1) — Verify amount on eCFR. DEA Diversion Control Division. As above — counts Unavailable.
DEA Schedule I registrations — researcher, bulk manufacturer, importer/exporter, analytical laboratory — 21 CFR Part 1301; 21 U.S.C. 823 Research on, manufacture, import/export, or analyse Schedule I substances (including non-medical marijuana). Protocol/facility review; Schedule I security and recordkeeping; quotas for bulk manufacture (21 CFR Part 1318). Fees per 21 CFR 1301.13(e)(1): manufacturer $3,699/year, distributor $1,850/year, dispenser $888/3 years; researcher, analytical laboratory and importer/exporter lines Verify at eCFR §1301.13(e)(1). Verify DEA Diversion Control Division registration portal. No official public downloadable registrant list located. Unavailable DEA validates individual registrations through its registrant tools.
FDA drug establishment registration / approved applications — 21 U.S.C. 355, 360 Manufacture/market approved drugs (e.g. Epidiolex, Marinol, Syndros, Cesamet). NDA/ANDA approval; establishment registration and drug listing. PDUFA (FY2026): application $4,682,003 (requiring clinical data) / $2,341,002 (not requiring); program fee $442,213. Establishment registration and drug listing under 21 U.S.C. 360 carry no FDA fee. FDA CDER. Drugs@FDA, https://www.accessdata.fda.gov/scripts/cder/daf/index.cfm — public database, updated as FDA acts

Part 6

Banking & Money

FinCEN Guidance FIN-2014-G001 (14 Feb 2014)

“BSA Expectations Regarding Marijuana-Related Businesses.” The guidance itself was not re-opened on fincen.gov in this run; its content and continuing operation are confirmed in FinCEN's own later Marijuana Banking Update PDFs on fincen.gov (index-level), which state verbatim: “The SAR reporting structure laid out in the 2014 guidance remains in place. FinCEN will continue to work closely with law enforcement and the financial sector… and we will notify the financial sector of any changes to FinCEN's SAR reporting expectations.” Sources: FinCEN Q2 FY2020 update (PDF) and the Q3 FY2019 update — accessed 2026-10-23.

SAR taxonomy (verbatim from FinCEN's updates)

  • “Marijuana Limited” — due diligence indicates the MRB does not raise Cole Memo red flags and is compliant with state regulations, and the institution is providing banking services;
  • “Marijuana Priority” — the MRB may raise one or more Cole Memo red flags or may not be fully compliant with state regulations, and services are provided while further investigation is conducted;
  • “Marijuana Termination” — the MRB raises red flags / is not fully compliant, or the institution has decided not to have MRB customers, and has terminated the relationship.

Institutions must also file CTRs for cash transactions over $10,000 and conduct ongoing due diligence.

The tension

The SAR categories are defined by reference to the Cole Memo priorities, which DOJ rescinded in January 2018. FinCEN has neither rescinded nor replaced FIN-2014-G001; the reporting structure “remains in place.” Hemp banking is treated separately: joint agency guidance (3 Dec 2019) and FinCEN hemp guidance (29 June 2020) treat lawful hemp businesses as ordinary commercial customers — no SAR is required solely because a customer is engaged in the growth or cultivation of hemp in accordance with applicable laws and regulations (FinCEN guidance FIN-2020-G001, fincen.gov).

Statistics: FinCEN's quarterly Marijuana Banking Updates continued after Q2 FY2020; the latest located on fincen.gov is the Q4 FY2021 update (SARs filed through 30 September 2021); the Q3 FY2019 update reported cumulative totals of 87,249 MRB SARs (68,378 “Limited”; 6,894 “Priority”; 22,132 “Termination” — categories overlap, because several SARs contain more than one key phrase). For the latest cumulative figures in the series see the Q4 FY2021 update.. Latest official FinCEN count: 755 depository institutions (553 banks, 202 credit unions) actively banking MRBs as of 30 September 2021 (Q4 FY2021 update). No later FinCEN quarterly count was located. GAO-26-107498, Banking Services: Cannabis Businesses Face Access Challenges (August 2026; dated 7 August 2026, publicly released 8 September 2026), reports from FinCEN data that about 1,000 banks and credit unions — roughly 11% of about 9,000 insured depository institutions — filed SARs indicating they served cannabis businesses in 2024; GAO cautions that a filing does not establish an ongoing customer relationship. (gao.gov) (GAO report not opened).

Banking legislation — none enacted (as of 23 Oct 2026)

As of 23 Oct 2026, congress.gov records no action on S. 4942 or H.R. 9471 beyond introduction, referral and the later addition of cosponsors (Senate: Sullivan, Cortez Masto, Cramer, Murray; House: Carter, Rogers, 20 Aug 2026). Neither bill has been enacted.

Payment processing

Card networks (Visa/Mastercard) are private companies, not federal regulators; their rules are not federal law. Federal Reserve master-account policy as applied to cannabis-adjacent institutions was not researched on federalreserve.gov in this run — Unavailable. Federal cash rules affecting unbanked cannabis businesses are at Part 4 (Form 8300; IRS cash payment options).

Part 7

Interstate Commerce & Shipping

Run 2 — all sources accessed 2026-10-23.

7.1 CSA baseline; hemp's opposite rule

Moving Schedule I marijuana across a state line is distribution/trafficking under 21 U.S.C. 841 (and, for import/export, 21 U.S.C. 952/960); §841/844 contain no interstate-commerce element in the transport itself, and there is no federal interstate medical or adult-use channel. State interstate-commerce compacts/authorisations (e.g. CA/OR/WA) have no federal effect while the product remains Schedule I. Hemp is the opposite: §10114 of the 2018 Farm Bill (7 U.S.C. 1639o note) provides in substance that no state or tribe may prohibit interstate transportation or shipment of hemp or hemp products produced in accordance with the Act through its territory. That protection attaches only to material that is hemp under the federal definition in force — which changes 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 (Part 2.2); a final product over the 0.4 mg/container cap after 11 Dec 2026 ceases to be hemp, loses §10114 protection, and becomes marijuana for CSA purposes.

7.2 The April 2026 Schedule III rule and interstate transfers; 21 CFR 1312.30

Schedule III status does not make distribution free: it still requires DEA registration (21 U.S.C. 822/823; 21 CFR Part 1301), records and inventory (Part 1304), prescriptions for dispensing (21 U.S.C. 829; Part 1306), and security controls; unregistered distribution remains a §841 offence. Nothing in the April 2026 rule creates a right for a dispensary in State A to ship to a patient or dispensary in State B — each transfer must independently satisfy DEA registration for the category (expedited registrations, 21 CFR 1301.13(k)), the receiving state's medical law, and stay within the “state medical marijuana licence” category; product diverted to adult-use falls back to Schedule I. Verify No DEA guidance specifically authorising routine interstate shipment of state-licensed medical marijuana between licensees in different states was located on dea.gov as of 2026-10-23; treat any claim of a general interstate medical market as unproven.

Import/export: 21 CFR Part 1312 governs; §1312.30 addresses permits for Schedule III/IV import/export (Schedule I/II sit under the stricter §§1312.11–1312.29 regime). The April 2026 rule amended §1312.30 to add the two new Schedule III categories to the list of non-narcotic Schedule III–V substances requiring an import/export permit — a continuity measure, not an easing: the rule states that “all importations and exportations of products containing marijuana remain subject to the permit requirement” under the Single Convention, which would otherwise have lapsed when the categories left Schedule I. Commercial import of state-legal marijuana would still need DEA importer registration, a permit for each shipment, and compliance with 21 U.S.C. 952/953. [91 FR 22714, at 22718–22719, 22723] §1312.30 as amended (91 FR 22723) adds: “(b) Marijuana, as defined in 21 U.S.C. 802(16), in a U.S. Food and Drug Administration approved product or subject to a state medical marijuana license. (c) Marijuana extract, as defined in 21 CFR 1308.11(d)(58), in a U.S. Food and Drug Administration approved product or subject to a state medical marijuana license. (d) Naturally derived delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinols in a U.S. Food and Drug Administration approved product or subject to a state medical marijuana license.” (Former paragraph (b) redesignated (e).) Schedule III status also does not make marijuana mailable (§7.3).

7.3 USPS — Publication 52, 18 U.S.C. 1716

Pub. 52 §453.31 (verified live, pe.usps.com):

“If the distribution of a controlled substance is unlawful under 21 U.S.C. § 801-971 or any implementing regulation in 21 CFR Chapter 2, then the mailing of the substance is also unlawful under 18 U.S.C. § 1716.” … “Controlled substances, and drugs that contain controlled substances, are acceptable in the domestic mail only under the following conditions:” [§453.31; conditions at §§453.311 et seq. for authorised persons]

USPS Publication 52, §453.31 · accessed 2026-10-23

Consequence: marijuana — medical or adult-use, state-legal or not — is nonmailable, except within the narrow §453 channels for DEA registrants/authorised persons (e.g. §453.36 prescription-drug returns under 21 CFR 1307.11; §453.34 small amounts to law enforcement; §453.7 DEA-authorised mail-back programmes). No April-2026 USPS revision extending mailability to Schedule III state-medical marijuana was located Verify.

Hemp — Pub. 52 §453.37. Original revision, Postal Bulletin 22521 (6 June 2019):

“453.37 Hemp-based Products — For purposes of this section, 'hemp' shall have the meaning provided under federal law, including Section 10113 of the Agricultural Improvement Act of 2018, Pub. L.115-334 (7 U.S.C. § 1639o), or any successor provision. Hemp and hemp-based products, including cannabidiol (CBD) with the tetrahydrocannabinol (THC) concentration of such hemp (or its derivatives) not exceeding a 0.3 percent limit are permitted to be mailed only when: a. The mailer complies with all applicable federal, state, and local laws … pertaining to hemp production, processing, distribution, and sales; and b. The mailer retains records establishing compliance with such laws, including laboratory test results, licenses, or compliance reports, for no less than 2 years after the date of mailing.”

Postal Bulletin 22521 (6 June 2019) — about.usps.com · accessed 2026-10-23

2021 revision, Postal Bulletin 22579 (26 Aug 2021), corrected by PB 22581 (23 Sept 2021) — the “.03 percent” in PB 22579 was a typographical error; the limit is 0.3 percent:

“Hemp and hemp-based products, including cannabidiol (CBD) with the [THC] concentration … not exceeding a 0.3 percent limit are permitted to be mailed in domestic mail only when: a. The mailer complies with all applicable federal, state, and local laws, and plans approved by the USDA under 7 CFR Part 990 pertaining to hemp production, processing, distribution, and sales; and b. The mailer retains records establishing compliance with such laws and plans, including laboratory test results, licenses, or compliance reports, for no less than 3 years after the date of mailing. Shipments of hemp and hemp-based products, including CBD are prohibited in international mail including items for delivery to overseas military and diplomatic Post Office addresses (APO, FPO, and DPO).”

Postal Bulletin 22579 (26 Aug 2021) — about.usps.com · accessed 2026-10-23

The current Postal Explorer text of §453.37 (pe.usps.com — Pub. 52 §453, verified live) retains the “or any successor provision” definition — so on 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 the USPS hemp rule automatically tracks the new §781 definition — and the domestic-only / international-and-APO/FPO/DPO prohibition. Trap: secondary sources still quoting “2 years” quote the superseded 2019 text; the operative retention period is 3 years. No per-parcel self-certification statement is required by §453.37 itself; the duty is to retain and produce compliance records.

7.4 PACT Act / ENDS — vapes, including CBD/hemp vapes

The PACT Act (15 U.S.C. 375 et seq.) was extended to electronic nicotine delivery systems by the Preventing Online Sales of E-Cigarettes to Children Act, enacted in the Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2021 (PL 116-260, 27 Dec 2020), effective 2021. The statutory ENDS definition, 15 U.S.C. 375(7): “any electronic device that, through an aerosolized solution, delivers nicotine, flavor, or any other substance to the user inhaling from the device,” including any component, liquid, part, or accessory. Is a CBD/hemp vape covered? Yes. USPS's final rule, Treatment of E-Cigarettes in the Mail, 86 FR 58398 (21 Oct 2021), implementing 18 U.S.C. 1716E, concluded “any other substance” includes hemp/CBD e-liquids and vape devices: ENDS are nonmailable to consumers, subject only to narrow exceptions (intra-Alaska/intra-Hawaii shipments; business/regulatory shipments by exception applicants; certain infrequent, lightweight, noncommercial shipments by individual adults — up to 10 ENDS per 30 days; consumer testing; public health). Hemp not in an ENDS (flower, oils, edibles) is unaffected by the PACT Act and is governed by §453.37. ATF administers PACT Act registration/reporting and the noncompliant-actor list; private delivery sellers of ENDS must register with ATF and state tax administrators, verify age, obtain adult signature, label packages, and keep delivery records (15 U.S.C. 376, 376a). Devices for dry herb / solid concentrates only fall outside the ENDS definition by the statute's own terms: 15 U.S.C. 375(7) reaches only a device that delivers a substance “through an aerosolized solution.” (86 FR 58398 preamble discussion not re-opened line-by-line.) (rule preamble as reported secondarily; preamble not re-opened line-by-line in Run 2).

7.5 Private carriers — not federal law

Carrier policies are private contract terms, not federal law: FedEx prohibits cannabis/marijuana, all THC products and marijuana-derived CBD; it accepts hemp-derived CBD products at ≤0.3% THC for parcel services where lawful (FedEx Service Guide — carrier terms, not federal law) current service guide; UPS accepts hemp/hemp-derived products (≤0.3% THC) from enrolled/approved shippers only, requires adult signature, and prohibits vape/aerosol hemp products and synthetic cannabinoids; DHL prohibits marijuana and restricts hemp/CBD, largely to B2B/international rules. A carrier's willingness to carry does not make a shipment lawful, and carrier refusal does not make lawful hemp unlawful.

7.6 DOT transport — FMCSA, hazmat

Hazmat: neither marijuana nor hemp is listed by name in the Hazardous Materials Table, 49 CFR 172.101, and cannabis/hemp is not per se a hazmat class. A specific product can become hazmat for independent reasons (ethanol-based tinctures in flammable-liquid quantities, compressed-gas cartridges, lithium batteries in vapes) — classification is product-specific. Verify No PHMSA special permit or rulemaking creating a cannabis hazmat class was located. FMCSA: the federal bite is (i) driver qualification/drug testing — a CDL driver may not use marijuana at all for DOT purposes (Part 9); a positive THC test is a Clearinghouse violation; (ii) 49 CFR 390.13 (no aiding/abetting violations); (iii) a carrier transporting Schedule I marijuana commits a CSA offence regardless of FMCSA compliance — operating authority confers no CSA immunity. Lawful hemp transport by motor carrier is lawful federally (subject to §10114 and destination-state law); drivers should carry the bill of lading, licence, and COA/test results.

7.7 Interstate-commerce legislation

Status only (Part 13): STATES 2.0 (H.R. 2934), CLIMB Act, CAOA (S. 5022), MORE Act (H.R. 5068), and the Small and Homestead Independent Producers Act (H.R. 4595) would each address interstate transport — all proposals only. No federal interstate-commerce cannabis statute has been enacted.

Part 8

Firearms

Run 2 — the largest change in this run. The statute is unchanged; the definition, the form, and the case law all moved in 2026. All sources accessed 2026-10-23.

Headline — United States v. Hemani, No. 24-1234, decided 18 June 2026

The Supreme Court held the government's prosecution of a regular marijuana user for in-home gun possession — on an automatic status-based theory, with no showing of danger and no pre-deprivation process — inconsistent with the Second Amendment (as-applied; Fifth Circuit affirmed). The Court expressly cited the April 2026 move of “some marijuana products” to Schedule III, 91 FR 22714. Hemani does not facially invalidate 18 U.S.C. 922(g)(3), and does not address addicts, persons presently intoxicated, §922(g)(1), or a prosecution with individualised proof of danger. Slip opinion verified on supremecourt.gov.

8.1 Statute — verbatim (18 U.S.C. 922)

(g)(3): “It shall be unlawful for any person— … (3) who is an unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance (as defined in section 102 of the Controlled Substances Act (21 U.S.C. 802)); … to ship or transport in interstate or foreign commerce, or possess in or affecting commerce, any firearm or ammunition; or to receive any firearm or ammunition which has been shipped or transported in interstate or foreign commerce.”

(d)(3): “It shall be unlawful for any person to sell or otherwise dispose of any firearm or ammunition to any person knowing or having reasonable cause to believe that such person— … (3) is an unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance (as defined in section 102 of the Controlled Substances Act (21 U.S.C. 802))…” Verify (d)(3) line text flagged for word-for-word punctuation against the current Code; substance as quoted.

(n): “It shall be unlawful for any person who is under indictment for a felony to ship or transport in interstate or foreign commerce any firearm or ammunition or receive any firearm or ammunition which has been shipped or transported in interstate or foreign commerce.” (§922(n) is the under-indictment provision; it contains no drug-user category.)

18 U.S.C. 922 — uscode.house.gov · accessed 2026-10-23. Penalty: 18 U.S.C. 924(a)(8) — up to 15 years for a §922(g) violation.

8.2 The definition — 27 CFR 478.11, old vs new

Pre-2026 text (eCFR): the pre-2026 definition had two prongs: an “addicted” person — one who “has lost the power of self-control with reference to the use of controlled substances” — and a “current user” of a controlled substance “in a manner other than as prescribed by a licensed physician,” with an inference of current use drawable from a conviction within the past year; multiple arrests within the past 5 years if the most recent arrest occurred within the past year; a drug test within the past year; or, for current or former Armed Forces members, a recent disciplinary or other administrative action based on confirmed drug use. The January 2026 IFR removed all of the inference examples. [91 FR 2698, at 2699–2700]

New text — ATF interim final rule, “Revising Definition of 'Unlawful User of or Addicted to Controlled Substance,'” RIN 1140-AB03, 91 FR 2698 (22 Jan 2026), FR Doc. 2026-01141, Docket No. ATF-2026-0034; ATF No. 2025R-54T, RIN 1140-AB03 — effective 22 January 2026, comments to 30 June 2026 (ATF rulemaking page, atf.gov, verified):

“(1) A person who uses a controlled substance and demonstrates a pattern of compulsively using the controlled substance, characterized by impaired control over use, is addicted to a controlled substance. (2) A person who regularly uses a controlled substance over an extended period of time continuing into the present, without a lawful prescription or in a manner substantially different from that prescribed by a licensed physician, is an unlawful user of a controlled substance. (i) Such unlawful use is not limited to using a controlled substance on a particular day, or within a matter of days before shipping, transporting, possessing, or receiving a firearm. Rather, unlawful use requires evidence that the person has unlawfully used the substance with sufficient regularity and recency to indicate that the individual is actively engaged in such conduct. … (ii) A person is not an unlawful user of a controlled substance if the person has ceased regularly unlawfully using the substance, or if the person's unlawful use is isolated or sporadic or does not otherwise demonstrate a pattern of ongoing use. A person is also not an unlawful user if the person, while using a lawfully prescribed controlled substance, deviates slightly or immaterially from the instructions of the prescribing physician.”

27 CFR 478.11, as amended by ATF interim final rule eff. 22 Jan 2026 · accessed 2026-10-23

ATF's stated rationale: the 1996/1997 single-incident inferences had produced thousands of NICS denials (FY2025: 9,163 (g)(3) NICS denials, of which 4,560 responses — 49.8% — rested on single-incident inferences, including 4,284 adjusted standard denials; ATF did not refer 8,893 of the 9,163 for further investigation, and projects ≈4,284 persons per year were denied on inferences the IFR removes — 91 FR 2701–2702) that ATF field offices themselves declined to pursue and that conflicted with circuit case law requiring regular, contemporaneous use. The rule is interim — a final rule after the comment period, and any post-Hemani revision, had not issued as of 23 Oct 2026 Verify.

8.3 ATF Form 4473, Question 21.f — form in force vs proposed form

Form in force (Rev. Aug 2023), Q.21.f, verbatim:

“Are you an unlawful user of, or addicted to, marijuana or any depressant, stimulant, narcotic drug, or any other controlled substance? Warning: The use or possession of marijuana remains unlawful under Federal law regardless of whether it has been legalized or decriminalized for medicinal or recreational purposes in the state where you reside.”

ATF Form 4473 (Rev. Aug 2023), Q.21.f · accessed 2026-10-23

A “yes” bars the transfer; a false “no” is an offence under 18 U.S.C. 922(a)(6)/924(a)(3). Proposed revision — Not law / not in force: ATF published a notice of proposed rulemaking, “Revising Firearms Transaction Record, Form 4473,” RIN 1140-AA82, Docket No. ATF-2026-0001, FR Doc. 2026-09182, on 8 May 2026; the revised form itself is a separate Paperwork Reduction Act information collection (OMB control no. 1140-0020), published concurrently with a 60-day comment period (Doc. 2026-09182; comment period reported to 6/7 July–6 Aug 2026 — dates conflict in secondary reports ), which would convert the certification to “I am not an unlawful user of…” and replace the warning with, in substance: “(Warning: You can be an unlawful user under federal law, even if your possession is legal under state law. Federal law does not permit the use or possession of marijuana for recreational purposes.)” The deliberate omission of medicinal use tracks the April 2026 Schedule III medical rule (§8.6). As of 23 Oct 2026 the revised form had not been shown to be final/in use on atf.gov Verify — dealers and buyers must use the Aug 2023 form until ATF issues the new edition.

8.4 ATF guidance; FFL duty

  • Open Letter to All FFLs, dated 21 September 2011 (the “15 September” date in secondary sources is wrong; ATF's open-letters index lists the entry as published/revised 21 November 2011 exact date on ATF PDF): “any person who uses or is addicted to marijuana, regardless of whether his or her State has passed legislation authorizing marijuana use for medicinal purposes, is an unlawful user of or addicted to a controlled substance, and is prohibited by Federal law from possessing firearms or ammunition”; an FFL may not transfer to a person the FFL knows or has reasonable cause to believe is such a user, and a state medical-marijuana card presented during a transaction gives “reasonable cause to believe” the person is a prohibited user.
  • ATF field-division clarifications (e.g. ATF St. Paul, Minnesota, 2023, atf.gov): “Regardless of the recent changes in Minnesota law… an individual who is a current user of marijuana is still federally defined as an 'unlawful user' of a controlled substance and therefore is prohibited from shipping, transporting, receiving, or possessing firearms or ammunition… Until marijuana is legalized federally, firearms owners and possessors should be mindful that it remains federally illegal to mix marijuana with firearms and ammunition.” ATF's Firearms Open Letters index (atf.gov) now marks the September 2011 marijuana open letter: “This open letter has been rescinded.” The rescinding instrument and its effective date have not been located on a primary source (RIN 1140-AB03 / 1140-AA82 dockets, regulations.gov). The letter's statements in this section are therefore historical, not current ATF guidance Verify.
  • FFL duty today: under §922(d)(3) an FFL who knows or has reasonable cause to believe a transferee is an unlawful user/addict may not transfer. After the Jan 2026 interim final rule, “reasonable cause” must be assessed against the narrower definition (regular, ongoing use — not a single incident, not ceased/sporadic use). Whether a medical card alone is still reasonable cause after the April 2026 rule and the proposed 4473 change: ATF has not, on any located primary source as of Oct 2026, published a replacement rule stating that conclusion either way Verify — the central open question in this Part. The prudent dealer position remains: do not transfer where the dealer has actual knowledge of regular current use that is unlawful under federal law; document NICS results.

8.5 Court rulings

Framework. New York State Rifle & Pistol Assn. v. Bruen, 597 U.S. 1 (2022): if the Second Amendment's plain text covers the conduct, the government must show the regulation is “consistent with the Nation's historical tradition of firearm regulation” — a “relevantly similar” analogue judged by “how and why.” United States v. Rahimi, 602 U.S. 680 (21 June 2024): §922(g)(8) is not facially unconstitutional; “An individual found by a court to pose a credible threat to the physical safety of another may be temporarily disarmed consistent with the Second Amendment”; no “historical twin” is required. Rahimi is the lens every (g)(3) court after June 2024 applies — and the lens Hemani applied to distinguish (g)(3) prosecutions lacking any judicial finding of danger.

United States v. Hemani, No. 24-1234, 608 U.S. ___ (2026) — verified on supremecourt.gov (slip opinion opened and read). Certiorari to the Fifth Circuit; petition filed 2 June 2025; cert granted 20 Oct 2025; argued 2 Mar 2026; decided 18 June 2026; affirmed. Opinion by Gorsuch, J., joined by Roberts, C.J., and Thomas, Sotomayor, Kavanaugh, Barrett, Jackson, JJ.; Thomas, J., concurring; Jackson, J., concurring (joined by Sotomayor, J.); Alito, J., concurring in the judgment (joined by Kagan, J.). Facts (syllabus): Ali Hemani cooperated in a 2022 terrorism-related search of the family home, surrendered a gun, pointed agents to marijuana, and admitted using marijuana “about every other day”; more than six months later the government prosecuted him solely on that admitted use under §922(g)(3) for possessing a gun in his home. Held (syllabus, verbatim):

“The government's prosecution of Mr. Hemani under §922(g)(3)'s unlawful user provision is inconsistent with the Second Amendment.”

United States v. Hemani, No. 24-1234 (18 June 2026), syllabus — supremecourt.gov · accessed 2026-10-23

Reasoning (syllabus): the government conceded the statute, as it construed it, “automatically ban[s] an individual from possessing a gun from the moment he becomes an unlawful user of any controlled substance… regardless of what controlled substance an individual uses, in what amounts, whether his drug use has ever made him a danger to himself or others, why he keeps a gun, or how safely he does so.” Its proffered analogues — “habitual drunkard” vagrancy, civil-commitment, and surety laws — fail on why and how: historical “habitual drunkards” were persons incapacitated and “incapable of conducting [their] own affairs,” not mere regular users; those laws required process before liberty was lost, whereas §922(g)(3) as construed “automatically divests an individual of his constitutional right… without any pre-deprivation process.” The Court also noted the government's own actions undercut a categorical-danger claim: DOJ curtailment of marijuana enforcement, widespread state legalisation, and that “the government recently moved some marijuana products from Schedule I to Schedule III, 91 Fed. Reg. 22714.” Narrowness (syllabus, in substance): “The Court's decision is narrow. It does not address efforts to ban addicts or those presently intoxicated from possessing a firearm; other prophylactic laws Congress might adopt…; §922(g)(1)'s provision disarming individuals convicted of felonies; or whether the government could bring a prosecution under §922(g)(3) accompanied by individualized proof that the defendant's drug use renders him a danger to himself or others, or proof that a certain drug always renders its users dangerous. The judgment was unanimous (9–0); the majority opinion was joined by seven justices, with Alito, J., concurring in the judgment (joined by Kagan, J.)…” Status: final; an as-applied holding — §922(g)(3) remains on the books and facially in force.

CaseHolding / status
United States v. Connelly, 117 F.4th 269 (5th Cir. 5 Sept 2024)§922(g)(3) is not facially unconstitutional, but its application to Connelly — occasional marijuana use as a sleep/anxiety aid, not shown intoxicated while possessing firearms — violated the Second Amendment; history supports banning carrying while actively intoxicated, not possession by regular-but-sober users.
United States v. Daniels, No. 22-60596 (5th Cir.)Original panel (77 F.4th 337 (2023)) held (g)(3) unconstitutional as applied to a near-daily user; Supreme Court GVR'd in light of Rahimi (2 July 2024, No. 23-376); on remand, United States v. Daniels, 124 F.4th 967 (5th Cir. 6 Jan 2025), again held (g)(3) unconstitutional as applied to Daniels — because the jury instructions permitted conviction without proof that he was intoxicated while armed. Status after Hemani: consistent with Hemani; no further cert located Verify.
United States v. Harrison, 153 F.4th 998 (No. 23-6028) (10th Cir. 26 Aug 2025)Reversed a district-court dismissal and remanded: the text covers non-intoxicated marijuana users; history supports disarming persons believed to pose a risk of future danger, but the government must be put to its proof. Harrison was an Oklahoma medical-dispensary employee without a medical card. On remand the government moved to dismiss, and the district court dismissed the indictment without prejudice on 3 Nov 2025; the case is closed .
8th CircuitUnited States v. Seher Verify — no official 8th Cir. (g)(3) opinion under that name could be confirmed on uscourts.gov in Run 2; the citable comparator is United States v. Jackson, 69 F.4th 495 (8th Cir. 2023), upholding §922(g)(1) — a (g)(1) case, not (g)(3). Several 8th Cir. district courts upheld (g)(3) pre-Hemani; their categorical-dangerousness reasoning is undermined by Hemani for status-only prosecutions.
11th CircuitNo published 11th Cir. merits decision invalidating or squarely upholding (g)(3) post-Bruen was located on official sources Verify Unavailable; challenges there were litigated principally at district level / in guilty-plea waiver postures.
Range v. Attorney General, 69 F.4th 96 (3d Cir. 2023) (en banc)GVR'd after Rahimi, re-decided en banc Dec 2024 reaching the same result: §922(g)(1) unconstitutional as applied to Range (1995 false-statement-to-obtain-food-stamps conviction, non-violent). Distinction: Range is a (g)(1) felon case; Hemani expressly reserved (g)(1). Range does not control (g)(3), and Hemani does not control (g)(1).

8.6 Precise effect of the hemp exclusion and the Schedule III medical move on §922(g)(3)

  1. Hemp: hemp is excluded from the CSA definition of marijuana (21 U.S.C. 802(16)(B)) and is not a controlled substance at all, so a user of lawful hemp/CBD (≤0.3% THC, current definition) is not an “unlawful user of … any controlled substance” by virtue of that hemp use alone. Caveats: product >0.3% THC is marijuana, not hemp; after 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 the hemp definition narrows (§781 as staged), so products lawful hemp today may become marijuana — and their regular users may become (g)(3) users — on those dates.
  2. The statute's logic: §922(g)(3) does not say “Schedule I” — it says “unlawful user of … any controlled substance (as defined in … 21 U.S.C. 802).” Moving a category to Schedule III therefore does not, by itself, remove its users from (g)(3): a Schedule III substance used without a lawful prescription / otherwise unlawfully is still used “unlawfully” (cf. oxycodone, Schedule II, lawful only on prescription). The operative question becomes whether state-licensed medical use is now lawful use under federal law.
  3. Medical use — materially different, not yet settled: for other Schedule III drugs, lawful use means use pursuant to a valid prescription from a DEA-registered practitioner (21 U.S.C. 829(b); 21 CFR 1306.04); state medical programmes use recommendations/certifications, not federal prescriptions — but for the new Schedule III category the April 2026 rule provides that state-authorised medical marijuana certifications or similar documents (naming the user, dated and signed on the day of issuance, identifying the issuing practitioner) are sufficient to permit dispensing by a 21 CFR 1301.13(k) registrant. The expedited DEA registrations under §1301.13(k) are the mechanism bringing such use inside the federal registration system. [91 FR 22714] The April 2026 rule's expedited DEA registrations (21 CFR 1301.13(k)) are the mechanism bringing such use inside the federal registration system, and ATF's trajectory points the same way (the Jan 2026 §478.11 definition; the proposed 4473 warning covering only recreational use). Bottom line: (a) adult-use users: unchanged — Schedule I, federally unlawful, (g)(3) applies by its terms (subject to Hemani's as-applied limit and the new regular-use definition); (b) state-licensed medical users: the “unlawful” predicate is now genuinely contestable and the agencies' trajectory is toward treating compliant state-medical use as not unlawful use — but as of 23 Oct 2026 no final ATF rule, final Form 4473, or DOJ guidance on a primary source states that conclusion in so many words Verify. A medical user answering Q.21.f “No” today relies on an argument, not a published safe harbour — and Hemani independently protects a regular user against a status-only prosecution even if the use is unlawful; (c) FDA-approved drug products (Epidiolex etc.) used as prescribed: lawful use — not (g)(3) unlawful use, as with any prescribed drug.
  4. Interaction with Hemani: even where use remains “unlawful,” a status-only in-home-possession prosecution of a regular marijuana user may not proceed on the automatic theory. What remains prosecutable by Hemani's own reservation: addicts; persons presently intoxicated; cases with individualised proof of danger; and any new, narrower statute Congress enacts. FFL transfer law (§922(d)(3)) was not before the Court — a dealer transfer ban as applied to medical users is untested Verify.
Part 9

Employment & Drug Testing

Run 2 — substantive outcome unchanged from rescheduling for DOT testing, federal employees, and clearances as of Oct 2026, but DOT issued new guidance: ODAPC marijuana notice (19 Dec 2025), ODAPC/OGC Part 40 Q&A on Schedule III medical marijuana (15 May 2026), and a final rule amending the oral-fluid rule (19 May 2026). No post-April-2026 OPM or ODNI revision was located. All sources accessed 2026-10-23.

9.1 DOT-regulated testing — 49 CFR Part 40

Part 40 applies to safety-sensitive employees regulated by FAA, FMCSA, FRA, FTA, NHTSA, PHMSA and OST; the U.S. Coast Guard (DHS) is treated as a DOT agency for drug-testing purposes only (49 CFR 40.3) (pilots, truck drivers, train engineers, school-bus drivers, transit operators, aircraft maintenance personnel, armed transit security, ship captains, pipeline emergency-response personnel, among others). DOT Recreational Marijuana Notice (ODAPC, 3 Dec 2012; transportation.gov), verbatim core:

“We want to make it perfectly clear that the state initiatives will have no bearing on the Department of Transportation's regulated drug testing program. The Department of Transportation's Drug and Alcohol Testing Regulation – 49 CFR Part 40 – does not authorize the use of Schedule I drugs, including marijuana, for any reason. Therefore, Medical Review Officers (MROs) will not verify a drug test as negative based upon learning that the employee used 'recreational marijuana' when states have passed 'recreational marijuana' initiatives. We also firmly reiterate that an MRO will not verify a drug test negative based upon information that a physician recommended that the employee use 'medical marijuana' when states have passed 'medical marijuana' initiatives. It is important to note that marijuana remains a drug listed in Schedule I of the Controlled Substances Act. It remains unacceptable for any safety-sensitive employee subject to drug testing under the Department of Transportation's drug testing regulations to use marijuana.”

DOT ODAPC Recreational Marijuana Notice — transportation.gov · accessed 2026-10-23

There is no Dec 2019 marijuana notice: the Medical Marijuana Notice is dated 22 Oct 2009 and the Recreational Notice 3 Dec 2012. The operative new marijuana notice is DOT's Notice on Testing for Marijuana, 19 Dec 2025. The CBD Notice (verbatim core): “Any product, including 'Cannabidiol' (CBD) products, with a concentration of more than 0.3% THC remains classified as marijuana, a Schedule I drug… 1. The Department of Transportation requires testing for marijuana and not CBD. 2. The labeling of many CBD products may be misleading… 3. [Use of CBD products] could lead to a positive marijuana test; CBD use is not a legitimate medical explanation for a laboratory-confirmed positive THC test.”

April 2026 rescheduling effect on DOT testing: none in outcome. ODAPC, 19 Dec 2025: marijuana testing, laboratory, MRO and SAP duties under 49 CFR Part 40 continue unchanged while rescheduling proceeds. ODAPC/OGC Q&A, 15 May 2026: an MRO may not verify a laboratory-confirmed marijuana positive as negative on the basis of a state-licensed marijuana product, because such a product is not FDA-approved, cannot be lawfully prescribed under federal law, and so is not a “legitimate medical explanation” under 49 CFR 40.137(a) and 40.141(b); state medical cards, physician recommendations and dispensary records do not satisfy the standard. Textual wrinkle: the DOT notice's rationale says Part 40 “does not authorize the use of Schedule I drugs” — adult-use marijuana is still Schedule I, so the notice remains literally accurate for adult-use; state-medical marijuana is now Schedule III, and DOT addressed that category directly in its 15 May 2026 Part 40 Q&A, resting on §§40.137/40.141 rather than on §40.151(e)'s Schedule I language .

Oral-fluid rule: final rule, 88 FR 27596 (2 May 2023), effective 1 June 2023 (transportation.gov ODAPC summary, verified live), amends Part 40 to authorise oral-fluid testing as an employer-chosen alternative to urine. Verbatim DOT caveat: “DOT oral fluid testing cannot be implemented until the Department of Health and Human Services (HHS) certifies at least two laboratories (one to serve as a primary laboratory, and a second to serve as a split specimen laboratory).” DOT amended the oral-fluid rule by final rule on 5 Nov 2024 and published a further NPRM on 11 Dec 2024; the interim directly-observed urine-collection regime for situations where oral fluid is required but unavailable was adopted by DOT's final rule of 19 May 2026 amending the oral-fluid rule. Status as of Oct 2026: — HHS has certified no oral-fluid laboratories (monthly Federal Register certified-lab notices, e.g. 91 FR 377–378 (6 Jan 2026): “At this time, there are no laboratories certified to conduct drug and specimen validity tests on oral fluid specimens”). Urine remains the only implementable DOT specimen.

FMCSA Drug & Alcohol Clearinghouse (49 CFR Part 382, Subpart G; operational 6 Jan 2020): employers must query pre-employment and annually and report Part 382 violations (including positive THC tests and refusals); a driver with an unresolved violation is prohibited from safety-sensitive functions until the return-to-duty process is complete; state licensing agencies must check the Clearinghouse before CDL issuance/renewal/transfer/upgrade and, since 18 Nov 2024, must downgrade the CDL of a driver in Clearinghouse prohibited status (49 CFR 383.73). Marijuana positives are by far the largest violation category in Clearinghouse data Verify current counts on fmcsa.dot.gov.

9.2 Federal employees — EO 12564, SAMHSA, OPM

Executive Order 12564 — Drug-Free Federal Workplace (15 Sept 1986), §1 verbatim: “(a) Federal employees are required to refrain from the use of illegal drugs. (b) The use of illegal drugs by Federal employees, whether on duty or off duty, is contrary to the efficiency of the service. (c) Persons who use illegal drugs are not suitable for Federal employment.” EO 12564 requires agency drug-testing plans, EAP referral, and testing of employees in sensitive/testing-designated positions and on reasonable suspicion/post-accident. It has not been amended or revoked as of Oct 2026; EO 14370 did not amend it.

SAMHSA/HHS Mandatory Guidelines: Urine Mandatory Guidelines, as revised 88 FR 70768 (12 Oct 2023, eff. 1 Feb 2024; originally 82 FR 7920), include THC on the standard panel; Oral Fluid Mandatory Guidelines, as revised 88 FR 70814 (12 Oct 2023, eff. 10 Oct 2023; originally 84 FR 57554, eff. 1 Jan 2020), authorise oral fluid for federal agency programmes (distinct from the DOT Part 40 bottleneck above). No 2024–2026 revision removing marijuana from the federal panel was located Verify.

OPM suitability (25 Feb 2021 memorandum): under 5 CFR 731.202, past marijuana use — including recently discontinued use — is not an automatic suitability bar and must be assessed case-by-case (the 5 CFR 731.202(b) factors: nature of the position; nature and seriousness of the conduct; circumstances surrounding the conduct; recency; age at the time of the conduct; contributing societal conditions; and absence or presence of rehabilitation). The 2021 memo does not address national-security eligibility, which is ODNI's responsibility. But an individual's disregard of federal law pertaining to marijuana while employed by the federal government remains relevant and may lead to disciplinary action, and state/local legalisation “do[es] not alter Federal law or Executive Branch policies regarding a drug-free workplace.” 2024–2026 changes: none located on opm.gov revising the 2021 memo after EO 14370 or the April 2026 rule Verify; a reported broader OPM suitability rulemaking's final status is also Verify. The Sept 2026 CURE Act, H.R. 10504 (Part 13), would legislate the past-use protection — proposal only.

9.3 Security clearances — SEAD 4, ODNI 2021 clarification

SEAD 4 (National Security Adjudicative Guidelines, eff. 8 June 2017), Guideline H — Drug Involvement and Substance Misuse: conditions raising a security concern include illegal manufacture, possession, distribution, or use of a controlled substance; mitigating conditions include demonstrated intent not to use in the future, successful rehabilitation, and passage of time. 50 U.S.C. 3343(b) (the Bond Amendment) bars granting/renewing a security clearance to a covered person who “is an unlawful user of a controlled substance or an addict (as defined in section 802(1) of title 21)”; subsection (b) contains no waiver provision — the same predicate analysed in Part 8. ODNI clarification (21 Dec 2021, DNI Avril Haines): prior marijuana use is not automatically disqualifying; adjudicators should weigh recency/frequency and willingness to comply with federal law going forward; current use while holding/applying for a clearance, and investment in or involvement with marijuana businesses, remain adjudicatively relevant under Guidelines H, E, and J. No superseding ODNI clarification for 2024–2026, and none reflecting the April 2026 split schedule, was located Verify. (The research brief's “Bond v???” reference: the reference is the Bond Amendment, 50 U.S.C. 3343 — a statute, not a case — .)

9.4 Federal contractors — Drug-Free Workplace Act, 41 U.S.C. 8101–8106

41 U.S.C. 8102(a)(1) (verbatim core): a non-individual “shall not be considered a responsible source … for … a contract … of a value greater than the simplified acquisition threshold, other than a contract for the procurement of commercial products or commercial services … unless the person agrees to provide a drug-free workplace by— (A) publishing a statement notifying employees that the unlawful manufacture, distribution, dispensation, possession, or use of a controlled substance is prohibited in the person's workplace…; (B) establishing a drug-free awareness program…; (C) giving each employee engaged in performance of the contract a copy of the statement; (D) notifying employees that as a condition of employment they will abide by the statement and notify the employer of any criminal drug statute conviction for a violation occurring in the workplace no later than 5 days after the conviction; (E) notifying the contracting agency within 10 days after receiving notice of a conviction; (F) imposing a sanction on, or requiring satisfactory participation in a drug-abuse assistance/rehabilitation programme by, a convicted employee (within 30 days); and (G) making a continuing good-faith effort to maintain a drug-free workplace.” §8103 imposes parallel duties on grantees; §8104 authorises suspension/termination/debarment (up to 5 years); §8106 defines terms.

Clarification: the Act does NOT require drug testing

The Drug-Free Workplace Act requires a published policy, an awareness programme, conviction-notification, and sanctions/rehabilitation for workplace convictions — of anyone, at any frequency, it requires no testing. Testing by contractors comes only from other authorities (DOT Part 40, NRC 10 CFR Part 26, DoD/DOE contract clauses, or the contractor's own policy). Its prohibition is also, by its terms, a workplace prohibition — not a federal ban on employees' off-duty state-legal use, though a contractor may adopt a stricter policy.

9.5 Military — UCMJ, DoD hemp/CBD ban

  • UCMJ Article 112a (10 U.S.C. 912a): wrongful use, possession, manufacture, distribution, importation, or introduction onto a military installation of a controlled substance (including marijuana) is punishable by court-martial; wrongful use may be inferred from metabolites absent lawful justification (there is no military prescription channel for state-programme marijuana).
  • DoD hemp/CBD ban: Memorandum of the Acting Under Secretary of Defense for Personnel and Readiness (Matthew Donovan), 26 Feb 2020, directing all services to issue punitive general orders or regulations by 1 Mar 2020 prohibiting use of hemp products by active-duty and reserve-component members, to “preserve the integrity of the drug testing program and preclude a service member from testing positive for marijuana after taking a CBD product.” Army Regulation 600-85 (23 July 2020), verbatim: “the use of products made or derived from hemp including cannabidiol CBD, regardless of the product's THC concentration, claimed or actual, and regardless of whether such product may lawfully be bought, sold, and used under the law applicable to civilians, is prohibited” — including ingested, inhaled, food, topical lotions/oils, soaps and shampoos; exceptions only for FDA-approved prescribed drugs (e.g. Epidiolex) and durable goods (rope, clothing). Navy ALNAV 074/20 (2020) is to the same effect, including topical products such as shampoos, conditioners, lotions, lip balms and soaps; it superseded ALNAV 074/19, which had exempted topicals. Hemp-product violations are punishable under Article 92, UCMJ (failure to obey a lawful general order), not Article 112a. No 2025–2026 rescission located Verify.
  • VA/DoD distinction: DoD (active force) prohibits and punishes; VA (Part 12) neither provides marijuana nor punishes mere participation in state programmes — but VA employees are federal employees under EO 12564 and are prohibited from using marijuana.
Part 10

Immigration

Run 2 — no USCIS update reflecting the April 2026 split schedule was located as of 23 Oct 2026 Verify. All sources accessed 2026-10-23. Do not advise any noncitizen that state-legal marijuana use is immigration-safe.

10.1 Statutory provisions (INA; 8 U.S.C.)

  • 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II) — an alien convicted of, or who admits having committed (or admits acts constituting the essential elements of), “a violation of (or a conspiracy or attempt to violate) any law or regulation of a State, the United States, or a foreign country relating to a controlled substance (as defined in section 802 of title 21)” is inadmissible. There is no amount-based exception in this paragraph; the single-offence simple-possession-of-30-grams-or-less-of-marijuana figure appears only in the §1182(h) waiver (which may waive this ground only insofar as it relates to such an offence), in the deportability exception at §1227(a)(2)(B)(i), and in the GMC regulation at 8 CFR 316.10(b)(2)(iii). No conviction is required: a qualifying admission suffices, if made with the Matter of K- safeguards.
  • 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(1)(A)(iv) — an alien determined (per HHS regulations, 42 CFR Part 34) to be a drug abuser or addict is inadmissible on health-related grounds; sustained remission removes the finding; under the CDC Technical Instructions for Panel Physicians, remission of a CSA substance-use disorder requires at least 12 consecutive months meeting no DSM criteria (except craving) and full abstinence verified by at least four random laboratory screenings on short notice over that period.
  • 8 U.S.C. 1182(a)(6)(C)(ii) — false claim to U.S. citizenship “for any purpose or benefit” is inadmissible and generally waiver-poor. Cannabis relevance: industry employment on an I-9 attesting citizenship, or registering/voting in connection with industry licensing — the provision is about the false claim, not the marijuana.
  • 8 U.S.C. 1227(a)(2)(B) — an alien convicted after admission of a controlled-substance offence (other than “a single offense involving possession for one's own use of 30 grams or less of marijuana”) is deportable; §1227(a)(2)(B)(ii) separately makes a person who “is, or at any time after admission has been, a drug abuser or addict” deportable.
  • Good moral character — 8 U.S.C. 1101(f)(3) and 8 CFR 316.10: a person who during the statutory period has committed an offence described in §1182(a)(2) — including a controlled-substance violation — cannot establish GMC, subject to the single-offence simple-possession-of-30-grams-or-less-of-marijuana exception stated in 8 CFR 316.10(b)(2)(iii) and USCIS Policy Manual Vol. 12, Part F, Ch. 5, §C.3.

The summaries above are paraphrase-tight; verbatim line-by-line text of §§1182/1227/1101 was not re-opened on uscode.house.gov in Run 2 — Verify for quotation purposes. The substance is confirmed by the USCIS Policy Manual discussion below.

10.2 USCIS Policy Manual — the operative guidance

Policy Alert, 19 Apr 2019 — Controlled Substance-Related Activity and Good Moral Character Determinations (uscis.gov): USCIS clarified (Policy Manual Vol. 12, Part F, Ch. 5) that “violations of federal controlled substance law, including violations involving marijuana, are generally a bar to establishing good moral character for naturalization, even where that conduct would not be an offense under state law,” and that “an applicant who is involved in certain marijuana-related activities may lack good moral character if found to have violated federal law, even if such activity has been decriminalized under applicable state laws.”

Vol. 12, Part F, Ch. 5, §C (Controlled Substance Violation) (accessed 2026-10-23): an applicant cannot establish GMC if, during the statutory period, he or she violated “any controlled substance-related federal or state law or regulation of the United States or law or regulation of any foreign country”; the bar is triggered by a conviction or an admission; it covers manufacture/production (including cultivation), distribution, dispensing, and possession; employment in the marijuana industry (and benefitting from a spouse's/parent's trafficking) can establish the violation; and the bar “does not apply if the violation was a single offense for simple possession of 30 grams or less of marijuana.” The chapter's marijuana text states that although states/DC have enacted laws permitting medical/recreational marijuana, “Marijuana, however, remains classified as a 'Schedule I' controlled substance under the federal CSA” and conduct violating the CSA “continues to constitute a conditional bar to GMC … even where such activity is not a criminal offense under state law.” Related positions: a valid admission (meeting the long-held admission requirements) to a USCIS officer can support a GMC bar and a §1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II) finding; disclosures to a civil surgeon or panel physician support a §1182(a)(1)(A)(iv) drug-abuser-or-addict determination under the CDC Technical Instructions and are not, without more, a formal (a)(2) admission; CBP admissions at ports of entry have produced §1182(a)(2)(A)(i)(II) findings for noncitizens who admit use or industry employment — specific expedited-removal cannabis cases on official sources: Unavailable in Run 2 (reported cases are secondary/news only).

10.3 Effect of the April 2026 move; 2025–2026 changes

The Policy Manual text quoted above still describes marijuana, without qualification, as Schedule I — which after 28 Apr 2026 is true only for non-medical marijuana. No USCIS policy alert, Policy Manual update, or Federal Register notice revising the marijuana/GMC guidance to reflect the split schedule was located on uscis.gov as of 23 Oct 2026 Verify; until USCIS updates it, adjudicators' published instruction remains the 2019 text. Analytical note (not USCIS guidance): the INA grounds turn on violation of law “relating to a controlled substance (as defined in 21 U.S.C. 802)” — marijuana in both schedules remains a “controlled substance,” so rescheduling alone does not repeal the immigration grounds. What changes, arguendo, is narrower: the April 2026 rule places FDA-approved products and marijuana subject to a state medical licence to manufacture, distribute or dispense in Schedule III and provides an expedited DEA registration pathway for those licensees (21 CFR 1301.13(k)). It does not make an individual patient's possession or use federally lawful — a Schedule III substance still requires the federal prescription/registration framework, and a state medical card is not a federal prescription. Any “no federal violation” argument is therefore confined, at most, to duly licensed and registered activity. No BIA precedent, AAO adopted decision, or federal appellate decision resolving that argument was located as of Oct 2026 Verify Unavailable. Adult-use, industry employment by noncitizens, and use outside a state medical licence are unchanged: still Schedule I, still a federal violation for immigration purposes. 2025–2026 policy change: none located in either direction on uscis.gov Verify.

Part 11

Tribal Land

Run 2 — all sources accessed 2026-10-23.

11.1 Jurisdiction baseline

“Indian country” is defined at 18 U.S.C. 1151 (reservations, dependent Indian communities, allotments). The CSA is a law of general applicability and applies in Indian Country; there is no tribal exemption in 21 U.S.C. 812/841/844. Criminal jurisdiction is allocated by the Major Crimes Act (18 U.S.C. 1153), the General Crimes Act (18 U.S.C. 1152), tribal inherent jurisdiction (capped by the Indian Civil Rights Act, 25 U.S.C. 1302: baseline 1 year/$5,000 per offence; up to 3 years/$15,000 per offence, stackable to 9 years, where TLOA's conditions are met), and Public Law 280 (18 U.S.C. 1162; 25 U.S.C. 1321–1326), which grants certain states criminal jurisdiction over offences committed by or against Indians in the listed Indian country — in mandatory PL-280 states, state criminal law is the operative law for most state prosecutions, while the federal CSA continues to apply as a law of general applicability, subject to the civil/regulatory vs criminal/prohibitory distinction of California v. Cabazon Band, 480 U.S. 202 (1987). Tribal sovereignty does not displace the CSA.

11.2 Wilkinson Memo — and its rescission

The Policy Statement Regarding Marijuana Issues in Indian Country (Monty Wilkinson, 28 Oct 2014) applied the eight Cole Memo enforcement priorities to Indian Country, invited tribal leaders to consult with the local U.S. Attorney, and stated DOJ would focus enforcement where priorities were implicated — expressly not a defence, safe harbour, or change in law. Rescission: the Sessions Memorandum, 4 Jan 2018, rescinded prior marijuana guidance by name, including the Ogden Memo (19 Oct 2009), Cole Memo I (29 June 2011), Cole Memo II (29 Aug 2013), the Cole financial-crimes guidance (14 Feb 2014), and the Wilkinson Policy Statement (28 Oct 2014), directing prosecutors to “follow the well-established principles that govern all federal prosecutions,” with no replacement priorities. No Biden- or Trump-era (2025–2026) DOJ memorandum reinstating Cole/Wilkinson tribal cannabis guidance was located; a Mar 2022 letter from nine senators urging Garland to adopt a tribal non-enforcement policy produced no published DOJ guidance Verify.

11.3 Tribal hemp — the lawful federal channel

7 U.S.C. 1639p: a tribe may submit a tribal hemp production plan to USDA for approval (7 CFR Part 990), covering licensing, THC testing (total THC), disposal, enforcement, and reporting; a tribe without an approved plan is subject to the USDA plan (direct USDA licensing). Per USDA AMS (list updated 17 February 2026): 57 tribes run their own USDA-approved plans and 8 tribes are USDA-managed, alongside 38 states with approved plans and 12 USDA-managed states (HI, IA, KS, MS, MO, NE, NH, NC, OH, UT, VT, WI), plus 3 approved territories (Guam, Puerto Rico, U.S. Virgin Islands) (USDA AMS list, updated 17 Feb 2026). The §781 redefinition (12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026) applies to tribal hemp identically. Tribal cannabis compacts (e.g. Washington State–tribal compacts under Wash. Rev. Code §43.06.490) are state–tribal agreements; they bind the state, not the United States, and confer no federal immunity.

11.4 Enforcement history (2015)

  • Alturas / XL Ranch (Modoc County, CA), July 2015: federal agents on 8 July 2015, BIA and DEA special agents, assisted by other federal and state agencies and the Modoc County Sheriff, executed search warrants at cultivation facilities at the Alturas Indian Rancheria and the Pit River Tribe's XL Ranch, seizing at least 12,000 plants and over 100 lb of processed marijuana (DOJ, E.D. Cal.); no other tribal property was seized and no federal charges were pending; the investigation concerned the financing and management of the projects, and the unsealed affidavits alleged a commercial operation exceeding California medical-marijuana limits — enforcement proceeded during the Wilkinson period where Cole priorities (diversion off-reservation) were implicated.
  • Menominee (Wisconsin), Oct 2015: DEA raided the Menominee Indian Tribe's industrial-hemp grow (Wisconsin had no legal cannabis/hemp programme then), seizing/destroying ~30,000 plants the tribe maintained were lawful hemp research plants; the tribe sued and the court ruled for the government in Menominee Indian Tribe of Wisconsin v. DEA, 190 F. Supp. 3d 843 (E.D. Wis. 2016) (No. 15-CV-1378, 23 May 2016), holding the tribe is not a “State” under §7606 (7 U.S.C. 5940) and its cultivation was not allowed under Wisconsin law, so the College of Menominee Nation partnership did not make it lawful. The raid occurred 23 Oct 2015; the plant count (~30,000) is as alleged/reported, not a judicial finding pin-cite/docket.
  • Flandreau Santee Sioux (South Dakota), Nov 2015: after legalising marijuana under tribal law for a planned resort, and after consultations in which federal officials signalled a possible raid, the tribe burned its crop (~600 plants) on 7 Nov 2015 and suspended operations; no raid occurred; two non-Indian consultants were later prosecuted by the state. The episode is the standard illustration that Wilkinson-era “consultation” conferred no approval.

11.5 IHS/BIA; recent legislation; cross-border

No IHS clinical guidance authorising cannabis in IHS facilities, and no BIA cannabis-specific enforcement policy distinct from CSA enforcement, was located on ihs.gov/bia.gov Unavailable; federal facilities in Indian Country remain federal-law facilities for drug-free-workplace purposes (Part 9). No enacted federal statute creating a tribal cannabis safe harbour exists; 119th-Congress proposals touching tribes (STATES 2.0, H.R. 2934; S. 4315 Hemp Safety Enforcement Act; tribal provisions in CAOA/MORE) are proposals only (Part 13). Saint Regis Mohawk / Akwesasne cross-border: the territory straddles the U.S.–Canada border and movement across it implicates 21 U.S.C. 952/960 and customs law, but the Saint Regis Mohawk Tribe holds a USDA-approved tribal hemp plan (AMS list, 17 Feb 2026). No official federal source (CBP/DEA/BIA) specifically addressing Saint Regis Mohawk cannabis was located — Unavailable. Do not extrapolate from news reporting.

Part 12

Veterans & Federal Programmes

Run 2 — all sources accessed 2026-10-23.

12.1 VA — VHA Directive 1315

Instrument: VHA Directive 1315, Access to VHA Clinical Programs for Veterans Participating in State-Approved Marijuana Programs. Original issue 8 Dec 2017; reissued 28 July 2023; date due for recertification 31 July 2028 (VA VHA Publications list, va.gov — verified in index). It therefore remains in force as of Oct 2026; no post-April-2026 reissue was listed. Operative policy (2017 text, carried forward in substance in the 2023 reissue Verify 2023 PDF line-text — the publications list confirms the reissue; the full 2023 PDF was not re-opened line-by-line in Run 2):

“Clinical staff may discuss with Veterans relevant clinical information regarding marijuana and when this is discussed it must be documented in the Veteran's medical record. Veterans must not be denied VHA services solely because they are participating in State-approved marijuana programs. Providers need to make decisions to modify treatment plans based on marijuana use on a case-by-case basis… in partnership with the Veteran and … based on concerns regarding Veteran health and safety.”

“The prohibition on recommending, making referrals to or completing forms and registering Veterans for participation in State-approved marijuana programs” — VHA providers are prohibited from recommending, referring to, or completing state forms.

“If a Veteran presents an authorization for marijuana to a VHA provider or pharmacist, VA will not provide marijuana nor will VA pay for marijuana to be provided by a non-VA entity.”

“Possession of marijuana, even for authorized medical reasons, by Veterans while on VA property is in violation of 38 CFR 1.218(a)(7) and places them at risk for prosecution under the Controlled Substances Act.”

VHA Directive 1315 (reissued 28 July 2023; recertification due 31 July 2028) — va.gov · accessed 2026-10-23

Why VA clinicians cannot “prescribe”: independent of the Directive, state-programme marijuana was Schedule I until Apr 2026, and the new Schedule III state-medical category operates through state licences / the new 1301.13(k) registrations, not through VA prescribing. VA has issued no guidance authorising VA clinicians to recommend/certify under the new category as of Oct 2026 Verify — Directive 1315's prohibition stands until amended. Drug testing / pain agreements: a positive THC result is addressed clinically (treatment-plan modification, case-by-case per Directive 1315), not by automatic loss of VA health care or benefits; Veterans will not be denied VA benefits because of marijuana use, and participation in a state marijuana programme does not affect eligibility for VA care and services (VA, “VA and Marijuana – What Veterans need to know,” publichealth.va.gov). Note: that page was last updated 1 Aug 2023 and still describes marijuana as Schedule I; it predates, and has not been updated for, the 28 Apr 2026 rule. “VA and marijuana — what Veterans need to know”. VA research law: the enacted research statute remains the Medical Marijuana and Cannabidiol Research Expansion Act, PL 117-215 (2022); VA-specific research bills are proposals (Part 13: VA Medicinal Cannabis Research Act of 2026, H.R. 10655, introduced 1 Oct 2026). MilCon-VA amendments to defund enforcement of Directive 1315's form-completion ban were not enacted in the final negotiated bills located Verify final FY2026/FY2027 MilCon-VA text.

12.2 Federal housing — HUD

Statutes/regulations: 42 U.S.C. 1437d(l)(6) (drug-related criminal activity by a household member or guest is cause for termination); 42 U.S.C. 13661 (PHAs/owners must prohibit admission of persons currently engaging in illegal use of a controlled substance); 42 U.S.C. 13662 (termination for drug abuse); 24 CFR 5.852 (PHA discretion, in deciding admission/termination for drug-related activity, to consider rehabilitation and other circumstances — discretionary consideration, not a right to use); 24 CFR 966.4(f)(12); 24 CFR 982.553 / 960.204 (Section 8 / public-housing denial grounds).

HUD guidance: Memorandum, Office of Public and Indian Housing, Medical Use of Marijuana and Reasonable Accommodation in Federal Public and Assisted Housing (20 Jan 2011). A follow-on HUD memorandum of 10 Feb 2011 (Henriquez), “Medical Marijuana in Use in Public Housing and Housing Choice Voucher Programs,” applies the same position to the Housing Choice Voucher programme: PHAs/owners must deny admission to households with a member currently using marijuana illegally under federal law — a medical-marijuana card is not a reasonable accommodation, because the use is federally illegal; for existing residents, the termination decision is the PHA/owner's, exercising §5.852-type discretion case-by-case. HUD Multifamily guidance (2014), Use of Marijuana in Multifamily Assisted Properties: owners must deny admission to current illegal users of marijuana, must maintain policies and lease provisions allowing termination for marijuana use (and may not adopt policies permitting use), and may terminate existing tenancies, exercising discretion case-by-case (HUD Multifamily Memorandum, “Use of Marijuana in Multifamily Assisted Properties,” 29 Dec 2014) — because “the use of 'medical marijuana' is illegal under federal law even if it is permitted under state law.”

Effect of the April 2026 rule: no HUD memorandum, PIH notice, or rulemaking updating the 2011/2014 marijuana position in light of the Schedule III state-medical category was located on hud.gov as of 23 Oct 2026 Verify. The statutory predicate (“illegal use of a controlled substance,” 42 U.S.C. 13661) raises the same “unlawful?” question as Part 8.6 — an argument, not a HUD change. A statutory fix is pending: Marijuana in Federally Assisted Housing Parity Act of 2025, H.R. 6807 / S. 3537 (Part 13) — Not law. USDA rural housing follows the same federal-illegality approach in its occupancy materials Verify pin-cite.

12.3 Other federal programmes

  • Federal student aid: the FAFSA Simplification Act (PL 116-260, Div. N) repealed the former 20 U.S.C. 1091(r) drug-conviction eligibility penalty; the Department of Education implemented early, and “the 2023-24 FAFSA form no longer contains selective service or drug conviction questions” (FSA Knowledge Center, fsapartners.ed.gov — verified). Mere use was never itself a Title IV bar; the former bar was conviction-based and is gone.
  • SNAP / TANF / SSI / SSDI: no federal rule bars these programmes for mere use of marijuana. Distinct rules often confused with a use-bar: (i) 21 U.S.C. 862a imposes a lifetime SNAP/TANF ban for a felony drug conviction unless the state opts out/modifies (most states have) — conviction-based and state-variable; (ii) SSI/SSDI benefits are not payable for a month of incarceration, and alcoholism/drug addiction cannot be the material basis of a disability finding (42 U.S.C. 423(d)(2)(C)) — a disability-determination rule, not a use-bar.
  • Section 8 is covered at §12.2; federal employment at Part 9.
