13.5 Other enacted / moving vehicles in the 119th

  • Enacted: PL 119-37 (incl. Div. B §781) (12 Nov 2025); PL 119-74 (§531 rider) (23 Jan 2026); PL 119-103 (§101 continuing the rider through 11 Dec 2026; §2019 staging §781) (2 Sept 2026) — all Run 1. No other enacted 119th law principally about cannabis/hemp was located. The HALT Fentanyl Act was enacted in the 119th as S. 331, Public Law 119-26 (16 July 2025) (H.R. 27, the House bill, passed the House only) but concerns fentanyl-related substances, not cannabis/hemp, and is noted only to prevent confusion.
  • Farm, Food, and National Security Act of 2026, H.R. 7567 (House-passed): per CRS IF13136 (28 Sept 2026), as passed in the House it “would amend the testing standard to be considered hemp to total THC rather than delta-9 THC” and “require state and federal hemp production plans to further differentiate between industrial hemp and hemp produced for other purposes.” Passed the House 30 Apr 2026, 224–200 (sponsor Rep. Glenn “GT” Thompson (R-PA)); no §781 delay in the House-passed text; not enacted — Senate status: no Senate passage located as of 23 Oct 2026 Verify.
  • FY2027 Agriculture appropriations, H.R. 8646 (House-passed): per CRS, contains no provision delaying §781. Reported 29 Apr 2026 (H. Rept. 119-632); passed the House 4 June 2026, 213–210; not enacted as a standalone in that form Verify final appropriations vehicle. (Note: §§781–782 of H.R. 8646 itself concern FDA/NIFA animal-research funds — unrelated to PL 119-37 §781.).
  • Rescheduling/descheduling by statute generally: no separate enacted descheduling or rescheduling statute exists in the 119th. The Schedule III medical move came from rulemaking (91 FR 22714), not a bill.
Part 14

Federal History — Chronological Table

Run 2, completed with the Part 14 completion pass — each entry dated and sourced; state-law entries are labelled (state). The table now runs complete from 1914 through 11 December 2026, including the upcoming §781 and USDA dates. All completion-pass sources were accessed 2026-10-23. Two date discrepancies against the research brief are carried in the Research Notes: Raich is dated 6 June 2005 in every source consulted (brief: 5 June), and the Ogden Memo is dated 19 October 2009 in the primary DOJ text (brief: 20 October).

DateEventSource
17 Dec 1914Harrison Narcotics Tax Act (PL 63-223) enacted — federal tax/registration regime for opium/coca (cannabis not covered); the template for federal drug control by taxation.govinfo / U.S. Code history notes
1913–1930s (state)States prohibit cannabis beginning with California (1913, via pharmacy-law amendment) and spreading state-by-state; by the 1930s most states restrict it, many via the Uniform State Narcotic Drug Act (1932). Brief/context only.Secondary — state history Verify state-by-state
2 Aug 1937 (eff. 1 Oct 1937)Marihuana Tax Act (PL 75-238; 50 Stat. 551) enacted. Imposed registration and occupational taxes and a transfer tax (generally $1 per ounce to a registered transferee; $100 per ounce to an unregistered transferee) evidenced by tax stamps that Treasury in practice did not issue to the public — making lawful compliance effectively impossible.govinfo (50 Stat. 551)
1944La Guardia Committee Report (NY Academy of Medicine) — concluded marijuana's dangers were exaggerated (not a proven gateway, no widespread addiction/crime causation); rejected by Anslinger/FBN and the AMA editorials of the day. No change in federal law. Verify — rests on a secondary, non-federal source; not confirmed on an official federal page.Secondary — municipal/academy report, not a federal source (see Verify flag)
3 Nov 1951Boggs Act (PL 82-255) — first mandatory minimums for drug offences; marijuana treated like narcotics (2–5 yrs first offence, with escalation); premise that marijuana leads to heroin.govinfo
18 July 1956Narcotic Control Act of 1956 (PL 84-728) — sharply increased penalties (up to death penalty authorisation for heroin sale to a minor by an adult; marijuana mandatory minimums raised to 2–10 yrs first offence).govinfo
19 May 1969Leary v. United States, 395 U.S. 6 (1969) — Supreme Court unanimously struck down the Marihuana Tax Act's transfer-tax scheme as applied, holding compliance would compel self-incrimination in violation of the Fifth Amendment (and reversing Timothy Leary's conviction). The Tax Act regime collapsed; interim federal control continued via other statutes pending the CSA.supremecourt.gov, 395 U.S. 6
27 Oct 1970 (most provisions eff. 1 May 1971)Controlled Substances Act, Title II of PL 91-513. Marijuana placed in Schedule I, 21 U.S.C. 812(c)(10) — provisionally, pending the Shafer Commission report Congress itself commissioned. Simple-possession mandatory minimums repealed.govinfo (84 Stat. 1242); uscode.house.gov, 21 U.S.C. 812
22 Mar 1972Shafer Commission, Marihuana: A Signal of Misunderstanding — recommended decriminalisation of private possession for personal use. Rejected by President Nixon; Schedule I retained.Commission report (federal)
1 July 1973Drug Enforcement Administration created — Reorganization Plan No. 2 of 1973 consolidated BNDD, ODALE, and other units into DEA within DOJ.dea.gov / Federal Register history
24 Oct 1986Anti-Drug Abuse Act of 1986 (PL 99-570) — mandatory minimums restored/escalated (21 U.S.C. 841(b)), crack/powder regime, money-laundering offences; EO 12564 drug-free federal workplace issued the same era (15 Sept 1986, Part 9).govinfo
18 Nov 1988Anti-Drug Abuse Act of 1988, Pub. L. 100-690 (102 Stat. 4181) enacted. Created the Office of National Drug Control Policy; imposed drug-free workplace requirements on federal grant/contract recipients; created civil penalties for possession of personal-use amounts of controlled substances; expanded criminal penalties (incl. the 21 U.S.C. §848(e) death penalty for certain drug-trafficking murders, effective this date), public-housing lease termination for household drug activity, money-laundering and forfeiture provisions.congress.gov — Public Laws, 100th Congress; OJP abstract · accessed 2026-10-23
5 Nov 1996 (state)California Proposition 215 (Compassionate Use Act of 1996) adopted by California voters — STATE law only: added Cal. Health & Safety Code §11362.5, permitting physician-recommended medical use of marijuana. Federal law unchanged; marijuana remained in Schedule I.CA Secretary of State — Prop 215 text; CA Legislative Analyst's analysis · accessed 2026-10-23
14 May 2001United States v. Oakland Cannabis Buyers' Cooperative, 532 U.S. 483 (decided 14 May 2001). Supreme Court held there is no medical-necessity defence to the CSA's prohibitions on manufacture/distribution of marijuana. — Source: the Court's opinion, 532 U.S. 483; the Court's opinion itself was not opened directly on an official source (supremecourt.gov / govinfo) for this dossier.United States v. Oakland Cannabis Buyers’ Cooperative, 532 U.S. 483 (secondary — see Verify flag) · accessed 2026-10-23
6 June 2005Gonzales v. Raich, 545 U.S. 1 (decided 6 June 2005 — see Research Notes). Supreme Court held Congress may, under the Commerce Clause, criminalize intrastate, homegrown medical marijuana that complies with California law; the CSA reaches intrastate medical marijuana. — Source: the Court's opinion, 545 U.S. 1 (supremecourt.gov / govinfo); the Court's opinion itself was not opened directly on an official source (supremecourt.gov / govinfo) for this dossier — attempts to open it there could not be completed, so under this dossier's sourcing rule the fact is marked Verify.Supreme Court — 545 U.S. 1 (U.S. Reports, govinfo) (non-primary reprint — see Verify flag) · accessed 2026-10-23
19 Oct 2009Ogden Memorandum (DOJ) — Deputy Attorney General David W. Ogden advised selected U.S. Attorneys not to focus federal resources on individuals in “clear and unambiguous compliance” with state medical marijuana laws (see Research Notes).DOJ archives — Ogden Memo · accessed 2026-10-23
29 Aug 2013Cole Memorandum (DOJ) — Deputy Attorney General James M. Cole, “Guidance Regarding Marijuana Enforcement”: set eight federal enforcement priorities and signalled a hands-off approach where states maintain strong, effective regulatory systems.DOJ — Cole Memo (PDF) · accessed 2026-10-23
7 Feb 2014Agricultural Act of 2014, §7606, Pub. L. 113-79 (“Legitimacy of Industrial Hemp Research”) enacted. Allowed institutions of higher education and state departments of agriculture to grow/cultivate industrial hemp under agricultural pilot programmes where state law allows; defined industrial hemp as Cannabis sativa L. with ≤0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis. — in part: Source: 7 U.S.C. 5940 (govinfo); PL 113-79, §7606 for this dossier.govinfo — 7 U.S.C. §5940 (opened directly); CRS, Hemp as an Agricultural Commodity (secondary mirror — see Verify flag) · accessed 2026-10-23
28 Oct 2014Wilkinson Memorandum (DOJ, tribal) — EOUSA Director Monty Wilkinson, “Policy Statement Regarding Marijuana Issues in Indian Country”: the Cole Memo's eight enforcement priorities guide U.S. Attorneys in Indian Country. — Source: DOJ, “Policy Statement Regarding Marijuana Issues in Indian Country,” Memorandum of Monty Wilkinson, Director, EOUSA, 28 Oct 2014 (justice.gov) (see Part 11 and Research Notes).DOJ — Wilkinson Memorandum, 28 Oct 2014 (justice.gov) (textbook reprint, non-primary — see Verify flag) · accessed 2026-10-23
16 Dec 2014Rohrabacher–Farr rider first enacted — Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2015, Pub. L. 113-235, §538 (signed 16 Dec 2014): “None of the funds… made available… to the Department of Justice may be used… to prevent such States from implementing their own State laws that authorize the use, distribution, possession, or cultivation of medical marijuana.” (§539 also barred action in contravention of §7606.) — in part: the §538 text is quoted verbatim in the McIntosh opinion opened directly on govinfo, but Source: PL 113-235, §§538–539 (govinfo), enacted 16 Dec 2014; §538 text as quoted in United States v. McIntosh, 833 F.3d 1163 (9th Cir. 2016).McIntosh opinion (govinfo) quoting §538 verbatim (opened directly); PL 113-235, §§538–539 (govinfo) (secondary — see Verify flag) · accessed 2026-10-23
16 Aug 2016United States v. McIntosh, 833 F.3d 1163 (9th Cir.) (decided 16 Aug 2016). The appropriations rider bars DOJ from spending funds to prosecute individuals who strictly comply with state medical marijuana law; cases remanded for evidentiary hearings on compliance.govinfo — McIntosh opinion (PDF) · accessed 2026-10-23
4 Jan 2018Sessions Memorandum (DOJ) — Attorney General Jeff Sessions rescinded, effective immediately, the prior nationwide marijuana-specific guidance (the Ogden, Cole and Wilkinson memoranda), directing prosecutors to follow the general Principles of Federal Prosecution.DOJ — Sessions Memo (PDF) · accessed 2026-10-23
25 June 2018Epidiolex FDA approval — FDA approved Epidiolex (cannabidiol) oral solution for seizures associated with Lennox-Gastaut and Dravet syndromes (patients ≥2 years): the first FDA-approved drug containing a purified substance derived from marijuana. Approval and date confirmed directly on FDA's Drugs@FDA (NDA 210365, original approval 06/25/2018). — the “first FDA-approved drug…” wording and the original indications are FDA's own wording in its press announcement of 25 June 2018 (fda.gov); the approval record is at Drugs@FDA, NDA 210365.Drugs@FDA — Epidiolex (NDA 210365) (opened directly); FDA press announcement, 25 June 2018 (fda.gov) · accessed 2026-10-23
28 Sept 2018DEA Schedule V placement — DEA final order, 83 FR 48950, placed FDA-approved drug products containing CBD derived from cannabis with ≤0.1% THC in Schedule V.DOJ 2026 rule text recounting 83 FR 48950 · accessed 2026-10-23
20 Dec 2018Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018, Pub. L. 115-334 enacted. Removed hemp from the CSA: §12619 excluded hemp from the definition of “marihuana” (21 U.S.C. §802(16)) and excepted THC in hemp from Schedule I; hemp defined (now codified at 7 U.S.C. §1639o) as Cannabis sativa L. and derivatives/extracts/cannabinoids with ≤0.3% delta-9 THC dry weight.congress.gov — PL 115-334 text (§12619) · accessed 2026-10-23
6 Apr 2020Epidiolex descheduled — DEA notified GW Pharmaceuticals that Epidiolex is no longer subject to the CSA, effective immediately, following FDA's removal of its Schedule V designation. Verify — effected by DEA letter, not a Federal Register rule; no primary DEA source located. Secondary: Sheppard Mullin (Mondaq), 6 Apr 2020; GW Pharmaceuticals press release (GlobeNewswire), 6 Apr 2020.Mondaq — Sheppard Mullin · accessed 2026-10-23
4 Dec 2020House passed the MORE Act — H.R. 3884 (Marijuana Opportunity Reinvestment and Expungement Act) passed the House 228–164: the first time a chamber of Congress passed a comprehensive marijuana decriminalization/descheduling bill. Bill, not law — it did not pass the Senate and was never enacted.congress.gov — H.R. 3884, “Passed House (12/04/2020)” · accessed 2026-10-23

Proposal, not finalfederalregister.gov — 2024 NPRM · accessed 2026-10-23
12 Nov 2025Pub. L. 119-37, Div. B §781 enacted (Continuing Appropriations… Act, 2026, H.R. 5371; approved 12 Nov 2025, 139 Stat. 558). §781 rewrites the hemp definition in 7 U.S.C. §1639o: ≤0.3% total tetrahydrocannabinols concentration (including THCA) dry weight; exclusions for cannabinoids not capable of being naturally produced by Cannabis sativa L., for naturally producible cannabinoids synthesized/manufactured outside the plant, and a 0.4 mg/container cap for final products. Effective 365 days after enactment (12 Nov 2026), as limited by PL 119-103 §2019 (see 2 Sept 2026).uscode.house.gov — 7 U.S.C. §1639o with PL 119-37 §781 amendment note (opened directly); CRS IF13136 (crsreports.congress.gov) · accessed 2026-10-23
18 Dec 2025Executive Order 14370, “Increasing Medical Marijuana and Cannabidiol Research” (90 FR 60541) — directs the Attorney General to take all necessary steps to complete the Schedule III rulemaking “in the most expeditious manner” under 21 U.S.C. §811. The order itself changed no schedule.govinfo — EO 14370, FR 23 Dec 2025 · accessed 2026-10-23
22 Jan 2026ATF interim final rule (RIN 1140-AB03), “Revising Definition of ‘Unlawful User of or Addicted to Controlled Substance’” — effective 22 Jan 2026 (comments to 30 June 2026). Rewrites 27 CFR §478.11: an “unlawful user” must now show regular use over an extended period continuing into the present, without lawful prescription or substantially different from prescription; prior single-incident inferences (one conviction/arrest/positive test within a year) removed.atf.gov — ATF rulemaking notice, RIN 1140-AB03 · accessed 2026-10-23
23 Jan 2026Pub. L. 119-74, §531 medical rider enacted (Commerce, Justice, Science… Appropriations Act, 2026, H.R. 6938; 140 Stat. 5). §531 continues the Rohrabacher–Farr protection: no DOJ funds may be used, for the listed states (the enacted list excludes Nebraska), DC and the territories, to prevent them implementing their own medical marijuana laws. §530 likewise bars funds in contravention of §7606 hemp research. Verified verbatim in the enacted text (no §860 subsection (b) appears in the enacted §531).govinfo — PL 119-74 full text (PDF), §531 · accessed 2026-10-23
28 Apr 2026Partial final rule effective, 91 FR 22714 (FR Doc. 2026-08176; AG Order No. 6754-2026) — “Rescheduling of FDA Approved Products Containing Marijuana From Schedule I to Schedule III.” Places marijuana, marijuana extracts and delta-9 THC in Schedule III only to the extent included in an FDA-approved drug product or subject to a state-issued medical marijuana licence (new 21 CFR §1308.13(g)). All other marijuana — including adult-use — remains Schedule I. Same day, DOJ withdrew the prior hearing notice and issued a new notice of hearing (91 FR 22777/22778).govinfo — 91 FR 22714 (FR 28 Apr 2026) · accessed 2026-10-23
18 June 2026United States v. Hemani, No. 24-1234 (decided 18 June 2026). Supreme Court held a prosecution under 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) based solely on the defendant's admitted regular marijuana use (about every other day) is inconsistent with the Second Amendment as applied; Fifth Circuit affirmed. Narrow, status-only holding — §922(g)(3) not facially invalidated.supremecourt.gov — Hemani slip opinion (PDF) · accessed 2026-10-23
29 June – 15 July 2026New DEA hearing on the broader NPRM — pursuant to the Acting Attorney General's notice of hearing of 28 Apr 2026 (91 FR 22777), the hearing on the 2024 proposal to move all marijuana to Schedule III was held 29 June – 15 July 2026 at the DEA Hearing Facility, Arlington, VA (ALJ recommended decision pending as of Oct 2026).dea.gov — Marijuana Rescheduling Regulatory Actions; dea.gov — ALJ order confirming hearing dates · accessed 2026-10-23
2 Sept 2026Pub. L. 119-103 enacted (Continuing Appropriations and Extensions Act, 2027, H.R. 6500; 140 Stat. 987). §101 continues FY2026 appropriations — including the PL 119-74 Div. A §531 medical rider — at FY2026 rates/conditions, with funds available until 11 Dec 2026 (§106(3)). §2019 (“Section 781 Extension”) provides that until 11 Dec 2026 the PL 119-37 §781 amendments apply only to products described in 7 U.S.C. §1639o(1)(C)(ii)(I) and (1)(C)(iv)(I) — i.e., only the “not naturally producible” cannabinoid exclusions take effect on 12 Nov 2026.govinfo — PL 119-103 full text (§§101, 106, 2019) (opened directly); CRS IF13136 (crsreports.congress.gov) · accessed 2026-10-23

As limited by PL 119-103 §2019, on this date only intermediate/final products containing cannabinoids not capable of being naturally produced by Cannabis sativa L. lose hemp status (and are treated as marijuana under the CSA).govinfo — PL 119-103, §2019 (opened directly); uscode.house.gov — 7 U.S.C. §1639o note; CRS IF13136 (crsreports.congress.gov) · accessed 2026-10-23
Upcoming — 11 Dec 2026Remainder of §781 applies — the total-THC (incl. THCA) ≤0.3% definition, outside-plant synthesis exclusions and 0.4 mg/container cap take full effect; this is also the date through which PL 119-103 continues CR funding and the §531 medical rider (§106(3)).govinfo — PL 119-103, §§106(3), 2019 (opened directly); CRS IF13136 (crsreports.congress.gov) · accessed 2026-10-23

Research Notes — Both Runs: Could Not Verify

Every gap, unverified item, and unavailable figure from both runs. The Run 2 list below is compiled from the Verify / Unavailable flags the Run 2 report itself attaches in Parts 7–14, including the Part 14 completion pass; recommended follow-up for each is the primary source named beside it.

Audit outcome — 24 October 2026

Many items listed below were resolved by the independent audit — among them the USDA plan counts, the Part 13 bill numbers and sponsors, the Part 14 sourcing flags (Oakland Cannabis Buyers’ Cooperative, Raich, §7606, Wilkinson, Rohrabacher–Farr, Epidiolex), the VA benefits pin-source, and the DOT and FinCEN questions in Parts 6 and 9. What remains genuinely unverified is consolidated in Part One of this page, “Still unverified — consolidated,” with where each item can be confirmed.
